Foreword

Dear reader, I truly appreciate your time in reading this book. For me and millions of others like me, these pages are about much more than information. They are about the full engagement of heart and mind in the search for God and for real life. What I am sharing is a summary of fifteen years of research that has touched my heart and transformed my life. You may have already read my account of my journey from Islam to Christianity, Searching for God, Finding Jesus. That book is the heart of my story, detailing my relationships, emotions, and spiritual struggles in my search for God. No God but Him: Allah or Jesus? The heart of my story is an examination of religions and their claims. Throughout this book, I hope to make two general points in particular: that the differences between Islam and Christianity have major implications, and that the evidence of history strongly supports the claims of Christianity.
"God", "Allah" and "Yahweh"
Before we begin, the title of this book may be self-explanatory. Both Islam and Christianity are monotheistic, and believe that “there is no god but He,” but they differ fundamentally about who that god is: Allah or Jesus. There are at least four common uses of the Arabic word Allah. First and foremost, it is used to refer to the Muslim concept of Allah as described by Islam. This should come as no surprise. The second use is simply to mean God in a general sense. Thus, like the English word God, Allah does not need to refer to a specific religion. But the last two uses may be surprising: many Arabic-speaking Christians use Allah to mean the Christian Trinitarian God, and sometimes Christians use the term only to refer to the first person of the Trinity, the Father. To complicate matters further, Christians often do not clearly distinguish between the persons of the Triune God. So they can confuse their Muslim friends by saying something like, “Jesus is God,” and in the next breath, “Jesus is the Son of God.” While both statements are technically accurate doctrines, they only cause confusion by switching between uses of the word God. Their Muslim friends might respond to this with, “So is Jesus his own son?” And they have every right to ask for clarification. In this book, I will try to pay special attention to how I use these words. The term Allah will refer specifically to the Muslim concept of God, the term Jehovah will be used when I intend to refer specifically to all three persons of the Trinity, and the term God will be used when the occasion requires a general or intentional use. The terms Father, Son, and Spirit will be used to refer to specific persons of the Trinity. Finally, if this paragraph is difficult to understand, please spend more time in Part 2. I am truly glad you are reading this book.
The semantic challenge
One of the biggest problems in religious discussions is how we define our words. When some people say “Islam,” they basically mean the religion that a Muslim friend practices. But what a friend does can look very different from what an imam in Saudi Arabia does, so how do we decide which one most accurately represents Islam? There are so many branches and sects that claim to follow Islam, so how can we tell if they are really Muslim? Do we just assume that anyone who calls themselves Muslim represents Islam? If so, how can we argue with the claim that terrorist groups like ISIS do not represent Islam, when they clearly claim to be Muslim? This problem is harder than it sounds, but to prevent our terminology from becoming a moving target, we should at least narrow down a definition. I think a religious group should be defined through a historical lens. What gave this group its identity and distinguished it from other groups in its early tradition? In the case of Muslims, their defining act was to affirm that Muhammad was a prophet and to follow exclusively his teachings as divine revelations. In this book, we will call all those who do so Muslims. We will call Islam the teachings of Muhammad in that early period of identity formation. Using the same reasoning, Christians initially distinguished themselves from their Greco-Roman context by their belief in the God of the Hebrew Bible. However, they were distinct from other Jews because, because of the resurrection of Christ, they believed that Jesus himself was the God of the Hebrew Bible. Therefore, those who adhere to the monotheism of the Hebrew Bible and at the same time follow theology are, in my view, Christians. Christianity is then constituted by the teachings of Jesus as understood in that early period of identity formation.

Final thoughts and prayers
This book has been a dream of mine for about a decade. I have met thousands of people who are desperately seeking God and are caught between the debates between Islam and Christianity. I do not claim to be impartial, but I have participated in both sides of the debate and I know how frustrating it can be to try to navigate through all the arguments. My prayer is that this book will reach many who are in the throes of their search, and I pray that it will lead them to the altar of the One God. If that is you, know that I have prayed and wept for you, and this book was written for you. If you are reading this book more for learning than for a personal struggle, please pause right now and pray for those who are struggling between Islam and Christianity and are trying to know God. Pray that He will meet them and equip you to be a part of their journey.
Now I pray to Jehovah, the God of the universe, who is able to do immeasurably more than all we ask or imagine. Lord, I commit this book to you for your recognition and glory. I ask you to save lives and transform this world. May many come to a saving knowledge of you and a relationship that will fill your heart and theirs with joy. In Jesus’ name I pray. Amen.








Prelude
Fatima's dilemma

“Repent! Or you have blasphemed!” Her brother was seething with rage, his words still echoing in Fatima’s mind. Repent! You have blasphemed! They faced the threat: the penalty for blasphemy was death. Had he really blasphemed? He hadn’t meant to. It had been a heated argument, and he had accidentally blurted out the words… But what now? How could this have happened? She was struggling to think clearly. Her life was in danger. Fatima, lifting her hands from her face, glanced at her computer. It was where she had secretly stored her most private thoughts and inner struggles, where she could discuss new ideas and share her opinions with sympathetic ears. Her computer was a gateway to her friends and her freedom. But today it had betrayed her. As a result, she had spent hours locked in her room, fearing for her life. His brother could return at any moment, and if he didn’t repent, it might be the end of the matter. He had to think. He had to think quickly and clearly. Despite the betrayal, his computer remained his only solution. As he had done so many times before, he returned to his laptop to help him. He started a post by logging into an Arabic forum.
Time: 5:15 AM, July 24, 2008
Author: Rania
She had operated under the name “Rania” for years, but the community knew her well. They knew that she was really Sarah Fatima Al-Mutairi, a twenty-six-year-old, a spirited young woman, a passionate teacher, a patriotic Saudi, and a recent convert to Christianity. Born in Qassim Province, Fatima’s family came from a prominent Bedouin tribe and raised her in accordance with their ancestral religion, Islam. Her mother, wishing to have a devout daughter, enrolled her in a Quranic school at a young age, and Fatima devoted herself intensely to her Islamic faith. She began to learn the Quran, carefully covered her hair with a hijab, and even fasted twice a week. She began to outdo her family in religious zeal, avoiding television and secular music, and eventually abandoning her friends because of her passion. Fatima’s mother became concerned. She wanted a loyal daughter, not a fanatic. This was not the Islam he knew. Regretting his decision, he pulled Fatima out of the Quran school and enrolled her in the government system. During the following months, Fatima’s life became normal, yet she was passionate about her religion. She participated in online debates and defended the Prophet and her beloved religion against their attacks. During these discussions, she carefully studied Islamic history and theology, confident that her faith would stand up to scrutiny. But during these debates, she realized, amidst grief and despair, that she could no longer follow Islam. She stopped eating for several days, fell into depression, and became an atheist. But something told her that this was not the answer. She began her search for God anew, and this time she asked for his help. It was then that she came across the Gospels, especially the Gospel of Matthew. They captivated her. She read it four times, and was most influenced by the Sermon on the Mount. After months of deliberation, she accepted its message. The Christian community she was associated with advised her to keep her new faith a secret, as leaving Islam in Saudi Arabia was punishable by death. This was difficult for Fatima, passionate and outspoken, but she hid her conversion from everyone, kept her private thoughts on her computer, and communicated with her Christian community only online. She now returned to this online community in her moment of critical despair. After some thought, she titled her post and continued:
Time: 5:15 AM, July 24, 2008
Author: Rania
Title: I'm in big trouble.
Body: Peace of our Lord and God and Jesus Christ. I am in great trouble. My family started to doubt me because of the religious argument this evening with my mother and brother...
Her brother. Fatima didn’t need to explain to the group how dangerous it could be to argue with him. Fatima’s brother had a similar start in the same family, but his story unfolded differently. His passion for Islam had grown from a child and had become fanatical. He eventually joined the “Encouraging Righteousness and Forbidding Evil” group, Saudi Arabia’s religious police force dedicated to enforcing a strict version of Islam on its citizens. Although many Muslims oppose the group in particular and Saudi Arabia’s dogmatic version of Islam in general, religious rigor attracts zealous young men like Fatima’s brother.
Fatima’s fingers raced over the keyboard, words now pouring out of her, recounting the harrowing events of the evening. She explained that in a moment of weakness, she had complained about her lack of religious freedom in Islam. When her family pressed her to explain herself, she said, “The path of Christ is purer than the path of the Prophet, and there is a difference between them!” Her brother became furious and threatened, “Repent! Or you have committed blasphemy!” Even as Fatima tried to apologize, he entered her room, grabbed her computer, and began searching through her files. There, he found Fatima’s diary, her Christian confessions, and even a picture of the cross. Her darkest suspicions were confirmed. A fierce malice welled up in her eyes. She left him, giving him four hours to see what he had done. Repent! You have committed blasphemy! When he reached the end of his post, he made a simple request: His gaze frightened me. I don't trust him. Please pray for me." Four hours had passed. His brother would return any moment. He had to choose: would he repent and accept Islam, or would he remain steadfast in his Christian faith, at the cost of his life? Which is Islam or Christianity?
Islam or Christianity?
For Fatima, absolutely everything depended on this question. Regardless of the strength of her convictions, when faced with the threat of death, she probably thought for a moment about how certain she really was: Is the way of Christ really any different from the way of the Prophet? Can we really be sure that this or that religion is true? Yet, is the truth worth dying for? Millions of people face Fatima’s dilemma every year: whether to follow Islam or Christianity, to worship Allah or Jesus. Like Fatima, unless the seeker lives in a nominal or secular environment, the stakes are high: it can cost the seeker family, friends, career, and potentially life. For such seekers, it is not simply a matter of believing what seems right. They must be sure that it is worth the sacrifice. For me, it has been a decade since I decided to leave Islam, and the consequences of my decision haunt me every day. I knew long before I converted that this would happen, but I also knew that I was certain. I was certain that Islam and Christianity were not just two paths leading to the same God, but two very different paths with very different paths. I was certain that I had a very good historical reason to believe in the Gospel. I was certain that although I loved Islam, I could not ignore the problems that had crippled its foundations. But most of all, I was certain that following the One God was worth all the trials and all the suffering. I had to follow the evidence and the truth, no matter the cost. I left my religion of twenty-two years and became a follower of Jesus in 2005. In 2009, after graduating from medical school, I decided to leave medicine to share what I had learned about the Gospel, the message of Christianity. I sincerely believe that this message has the power to transform hearts and change the world. The God he proclaims is like no other, and it is an incomprehensible honor to be a part of his story and to introduce people to him. In sharing this message, I often encounter two types of people: Christians who enjoy criticizing Islam, and Muslims who want to argue but don’t want to learn. I am not writing this book for either of them. I am writing it for people who—like Fatima and I—need answers to these questions:
What are the differences between Islam and Christianity?
Can we be sure that Christianity or Islam is true?
Is the truth worth dying for?
It took me four years to answer these questions, and they are so important to me that I have been studying them for another decade. This book is my short answer. After sharing my findings, we will see how Fatima answered the same questions and discover the outcome of her story.





First question
Islam and Christianity
What is the difference between them?







In August 2005, I came to the most painful realization of my life: I no longer believed in Islam. I had no choice, and I could no longer delay the possibility I had been struggling with for years. I had been raised to love Islam as a child. I enjoyed memorizing chapters of the Quran and reciting them in daily prayers. I looked forward to fasting with my family during Ramadan each year, excited for the early morning prayers and the evening congregational prayers. I looked forward to celebrating each Eid with my extended family. My entire life revolved around Islam, and I was proud of my Muslim heritage. Ironically, it was my trust in Islam that brought my faith to a breaking point. Shortly after starting my undergraduate studies in 2001, I challenged a Christian friend of mine at my university to consider the truth of Islam. Using arguments I had heard in mosques and from Muslim authorities, I argued that Islamic teachings were certifiably true, while Christian teachings were certifiably false. His answers led to an investigation that ultimately lasted four years. What I discovered over and over again was that Christian teachings held up whenever they could be tested historically. The arguments against Christianity, which I had relied on all my life, were flawed and weak, and Christianity was strong. But it was something that happened later that shook my world and my heart to the core. My friend used the same critical standards I had used against Christian teachings to challenge Islam. Under the weight of this constant scrutiny, the foundations of Islam crumbled. In the summer of 2005, I realized that I no longer believed in the Shahadah, the Muslim declaration that “There is no god but Allah and Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.” The declaration of faith is the minimum requirement for a Muslim, and I simply did not believe it. I desperately wanted to believe it, because I found everything I loved in Islam: my family, my friends, my culture, my traditions, my heritage. Leaving Islam would have meant sacrificing everything I knew and destroying the people I loved most. At the same time, Christianity held no appeal for me. My family was not Christian, I had only three Christian friends, my only experiences visiting churches had left a bad taste in my mouth, I thought Christmas and Easter were pagan traditions, and I really didn’t know how I could fit in as a Christian. I didn’t want to believe in Christianity at all. But I realized it was too late: I already believed Christianity was true, and I couldn’t be a Muslim because I couldn’t honestly declare my faith. The only question that remained was whether I would take the final step and move forward in faith. It is one thing to find compelling evidence, and quite another to act on that evidence in faith. Especially when the cost is practically unbearable. On August 24, 2005, when I could resist no longer, I bowed my knee to Jesus and declared my faith in him. Shortly thereafter, my family was torn apart, and the next year of my life was by far the most bitter I have ever endured. I am now an outsider to both my family and all my friends in the Muslim community. It was only a few weeks ago that I received my first death threat. Ten years later, I still receive occasional death threats, I have never regained my old friends, and my family has never been the same. I feel the painful consequences of my decision every day. So when I hear people say that Islam and Christianity are essentially the same, I have to try to suppress my incredulous response. Are Islam and Christianity the same? My parents certainly don’t think so, nor do any of the dozens of friends I lost. This stereotype is a slap in the face to the hundreds of thousands of Muslims who have left Islam for Christianity and vice versa. Not only are these religions different, but the differences have far greater implications than I realized when I converted. I knew that the historical teachings of the two religions were different, but teachings don’t exist in a vacuum. They work together to influence our view of the world, which in turn changes our personalities.
For example, the Muslim concept of God, whom we call Allah, has different characteristics from the Christian concept of God. The most obvious of these, of course, is that God is not a Trinity, while the one Christian God exists in three persons: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. The concept of the personality of God is so different between Islam and Christianity that the basic premise of Christianity, "Jesus is God," registers as blasphemy to the average Muslim. Although both religions teach that there is no God but one, simply considering the personality of God shows that they differ significantly about his character.
And what we think God is like has a tremendous impact on how we see the world he has created. Why did God create humans: to share intimacy with them or to test them? What does he think about people: are they his servants or his children? How does he want us to live: to focus on love or to focus on the law? What does he tell us about life after death: to anxiously anticipate an unknown judgment or to joyfully trust in his grace? The Islamic view of God and the Christian view accept different answers, and how we answer these questions changes the way we view ourselves, others, and the world around us. Both Muslims and Christians believe that there is only one God, but is he Allah or Jesus? I can tell you from personal experience and in all sincerity: how we answer this question has the power to change us.















Part 1
Law or Gospel?
Two different solutions









Chapter 1
A way to life

As a young student, I was proud to be the voice of Islam in the large group of religious views at my university. The diversity of thought was beautiful because there was an environment where critical thinking was encouraged. As we presented our ideas and perspectives, others were free to both challenge our views and gain insights from them, and we were all sharpened by critical engagement. Tolerance means always accepting people, without accepting their ideas.
So, in the midst of challenging a colleague’s cherished beliefs, we also became the best of friends. David Wood was on my debate and forensics team at Old Dominion University, and when I saw him reading the Bible, I didn’t hesitate to question its reliability and corruption. We connected instantly because of our mutual passion for God and truth, even though our views were wildly opposed. We disagreed so much on fundamental issues that we often signed up for classes together so we could sit in the back of lecture halls and debate.
But as the circle widened and other perspectives entered the discussion, a surprising trend emerged. The first time this happened was when an agnostic teammate named Marie (someone who believes that the human mind is incapable of knowing for sure whether God exists or whether there is anything beyond material phenomena) overheard one of our discussions and joined in. David and I found ourselves agreeing with each other despite her views. The second time this happened was when a Buddhist friend named Zach presented his reasons for being Buddhist, and David and I both disagreed with him for similar reasons. Whenever we met people from other worldviews, the discussions highlighted the similarities between Islam and Christianity.
There is really no doubt that Islam and Christianity are close to each other in the broader spectrum of religion. They are both monotheistic, the two largest religious communities in the world, and they have many similarities. Each teaches the doctrine of an eternal, powerful, and wise God who rules the universe. It is God who created man from a male and a female, but people turned away from Him. Each teaches that there will be a Day of Judgment and a final judgment. Before that, it is very important for us to seek God and follow Him.
But the similarities between Islam and Christianity run deeper, beyond the trappings of monotheism: Both claim Abrahamic lineage. Both teach that God sent human prophets and angels to guide people to Him. Both teach that God inspired scriptures to guide mankind. Both teach that Satan is a deceiver who misleads the unwary. And both teach that believers should selflessly care for one another and proclaim the truth to nonbelievers. Perhaps the most striking common feature is their reverence for Jesus. Both Islam and Christianity teach that Jesus was born of a virgin and that he was the most miraculous man who ever lived. Both the Bible and the Quran teach that Jesus healed lepers, restored sight to the blind, and even raised the dead. In fact, both books teach that Jesus is the Messiah, and Muslims, like Christians, await his return. There is no doubt, then, that there is much in common between Islam and Christianity. It would be remiss to ignore this, especially given the range of worldviews that range from atheistic to pantheistic, as David and I witnessed at our university.
The Way of Life: A Law or a Person?
But the many similarities do not mean that the differences are not significant. Humans and chimpanzees share 95 percent of their DNA, but the remaining 5 percent is incredibly important! The same is true of Islam and Christianity. There is a lot of shared DNA, but the two are quite different phenotypically. Where the difference matters most is in the ultimate message of each religion. In Islam, the way to heaven is the Sharia, the laws that must be followed to please and please Allah. Sharia is literally translated as “the way.” According to the Christian message, the Gospel is the way to eternal life, Jesus. He said, “I am the way, the truth, and the life; no one comes to the Father except through me” (John 14:6). In Islam, the Sharia is the way, and in Christianity, Jesus is the way. How is it that the way to life in one religion is the Sharia, while in another it is a person? To understand, we must compare the law and the Bible.
Season 2
Comparing the Law and the Bible

Islamic worldview
The word Islam means “submission,” and the explicit message of Islam is precisely this: all human beings must submit to the absolute will of God. God created mankind with the express purpose of worshipping Him in the creation of the universe (Quran 51:56). To guide humanity, God sent prophets to all people to bring them out of ignorance (Quran 4:163-165). It is important to note here that the concept of prophet in Islam is not the same as it is in the Bible. Prophets in Islam have a higher status than all other people because they are men chosen by God to lead mankind. The Quran uses the term to mean a divinely appointed leader, not necessarily someone who prophesies.
Adam is considered the first prophet, but the Quran also mentions Noah, Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob, Job, Moses, Jonah, Aaron, Solomon, David, and of course, Jesus (e.g., Quran 4:163). Since these individuals all submitted to Allah, they became submissive (i.e., Islam). Therefore, they are considered to be people who have submitted (i.e., Muslims). All those who followed these prophets in submitting to Allah are also considered Muslims, even if they were born in the ages before Muhammad. Allah revealed His guidance to each prophet according to the people's needs and tolerance. For example, Moses' people were to rebel against Pharaoh, so Allah revealed "an eye for an eye, a tooth for a tooth."
But Jesus’ people were to be peaceful, so Allah taught them to “turn their faces.” Both Moses and Jesus, as well as other prophets, were given scripture. Angels conveyed Allah’s revelation to them, and the revelation was written down in the form of the Torah, the Gospel, and other books (e.g., Quran 5:46).
Unfortunately, people did not sincerely follow the prophets that Allah sent to them. So Allah, in His mercy, sent Muhammad and gave him the Quran. Thus, Allah gave mankind the final and perfect religion (Quran 5. 3). Thus, Islam is the culmination of Judaism, Christianity, and other world religions that began in line with Islamic teachings. All those who continue to follow these religions after the advent of Muhammad are either misguided or rebellious, and no religion will be accepted from them on the Day of Judgment except Islam (Quran 3. 81-85).
It is on the Day of Judgment that man finds his main motivation for following Islam. The Quran emphasizes that on that day, all people will be held accountable before Allah for their sins (Quran 6:164; 17:15; 35:18; 39:7; 53:38). This is a deeply rooted understanding in the Muslim psyche: although Allah has mercy and forgives us of our sins, no one else can intercede. Muslims should live as good a life as they can to come close to Paradise and hope for Allah’s merciful judgment to ensure their salvation.
The Islamic Solution: Sharia
But Islam teaches that the fundamental problem of humanity is ignorance and that man needs guidance to live a good life. When people learn what to believe and how to live, they will gain the pleasure of Allah.
As for correct belief, the emphasis is on the Islamic understanding of monotheism: Allah is not the Father, nor is Allah the Son (Quran 112). He is an absolute unity, a monad. The other components of belief have already been mentioned: belief in the prophets, belief in the divine books, belief in angels and the unseen, belief in the Day of Judgment, and belief in the predestination of Allah. Together, these are called the six articles of faith. There are many more Islamic beliefs, but this is the core. Correct action in Islam is taught through Islamic law, known as the “path to water.” Especially for desert people, this concept is powerful: following the law is the way of life.
Sharia governs virtually every aspect of a devout Muslim’s life, from the foods they should eat, the proper forms of currency, to the exact words they should recite during prayer. Of all Islamic practices, five are most important: declaring the Islamic motto, the Shahadah: “There is no god but Allah and Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.” Performing the five daily prayers; fasting during the month of Ramadan; giving alms; and making the pilgrimage to Mecca. Together, these are called the Five Pillars of Islam.
The creed and the Sharia are ultimately rooted in the life and teachings of Muhammad. He is the epitome of Islam, and as such, Muslims are expected to follow him as the perfect model. His actions and sayings in life are recorded in a vast body of literature, collectively called the Hadith literature. The Hadith are so important that they constitute the second level of Sharia after the Quran. Given the breadth of the teachings of the Quran and the extraordinarily vast scope of the Hadith literature, it is the task of scholars to identify the Sharia.
Muslim jurists study extensive hadiths and legal traditions before issuing a formal judgment, called a fatwa. These individuals are technically called jurists, but are often referred to as a Muslim leader, the Imam. Collectively, the consensus of these scholars is called ijma and is known as the third major component of Sharia.
Finally, we are at a place where we understand the message of Islam. Sharia is more than Islamic law. It is the answer to human ignorance and if followed, a peaceful life with Allah and His abundant blessings will be achieved. Sharia is derived from the Quran, which is exemplified in the life of Muhammad and explained by the Imams. On the Last Day, if we obey and do good, Allah will have mercy on us and we will enter Paradise where we will be rewarded eternally.
So in summary, when it comes to salvation in Islam, Sharia is literally "the way" and submission to the will of Allah is the primary expression of our worship.
Christian worldview
At the beginning of the Christian worldview is the one God, Jehovah. He exists as three persons who love each other perfectly. Therefore, the one God is, in essence, love. Out of this love, God created mankind in His own image so that God would love man and man would love God. It is important to note that this concept of love is often misunderstood by Muslims because of the different ways the word love is used in English. The specific concept of love we are discussing is often called agape love (unconditional love). This is not the love we imagine in a romantic relationship. It does not mean much emotion at all. The Bible gives a beautiful description of this love in 1 Corinthians 13, but essentially love is: self-sacrifice that makes others happy. This is who God, the Almighty and yet the most humble, is, the center of the universe and yet the most self-sacrificing. He created man to enjoy us and we in him with self-sacrificing love.
But for this love to be valuable, it must be voluntary, so Jehovah gave man the choice to love him or to reject him. When man disobeys God, he rejects God. By rejecting the source of life, we bring death upon ourselves. This time he repeats: the result of sin is death because it is the rejection of the source of life.
That is why, in the Christian worldview, sin against God is more than doing something wrong. It is rebellion against the universal providence. It is the most destructive force in the universe, the ultimate root of every aching heart, every broken family, every futile war, every heinous genocide. Sin spreads like a malignant cancer through generations, destroying civilizations like a plague. The effect of sin is catastrophic. Sin, like a hammer hitting a mirror, shatters the image in which man is made. When Adam sinned, the image of God in man was irreparably shattered.
This is the Christian worldview: Sin has ruined our souls and the entire world. There is no way to remove our sin. We cannot simply do a few good deeds to save our souls. There is nothing on earth that we can do. It takes a miracle, an act of God, to restore us and save this world.
The Christian Solution: The Gospel
But in the Christian message, there is good news. In Greek, the word for good news is euangelion, which is translated into English as “gospel.” And the good news is this: Even though we can’t reach God, because of his great love, God has come to us and made a way for us. God himself has paid the penalty for our sins and will restore our lives forever. All we have to do is repent of our rebellion, believe in what he has done, and follow him.
To pay for our sins, God—specifically the second person of the Trinity—entered the world. God took on human nature without changing His divine nature. He was born as a human, but not of the broken lineage of Adam. He was born unbroken, as humanity was meant to be, as we will ultimately be when we are miraculously restored.
He was given the name Jesus, which means “God saves.” Given his human nature, he grew up as a human, ate as a human, suffered alongside humans, and ultimately died as a human.
In all of this He never sinned, so He was able to bear our sins. He lived the life we should have lived so He could die the death we deserve. He took on the sins of the world by dying on our behalf so that whoever believes in Him and accepts what He has done will have eternal life.
From the perspective of a human being watching Jesus, it might seem like another man dying again. So to prove to the world that his death was not just another death, but a death that would give life to the world, and to prove that he was indeed the God he claimed to be, he rose from the dead. On the one hand, it was a sign to all who doubted that Jesus really had supernatural powers and deserved to be heard. On the other hand, it was a symbol to those who believed in him that death had been defeated. Jesus had conquered it for us.
Those of us who want to accept God’s sacrifice on His behalf must repent of our sins and give ourselves over to following Him. As we do, God—specifically the third person of the Trinity—makes our hearts a holy temple and lives within us. He transforms us from the inside out. In other words, as we follow Jesus, we become more like Him, the invincible human being, and the Holy Spirit gradually begins the miraculous work of restoring our irreparable souls. Like Jesus, we are filled with self-sacrificing love and begin to live for others instead of ourselves, as God lived for us. Those who progress in the path of becoming like Jesus even reflect Him in their willingness to die for others, as Jesus was willing to die for us. They become more like God: they enjoy self-sacrifice for others.
Finally, we are in a place where we understand the message of Christianity: the fundamental problem of humanity is sin, and we are powerless to save ourselves. The good news is that God loves us and has made a way for us by paying the penalty for our sin on the cross. Jesus proved by rising from the dead that He has the power to save. As we repent and follow Jesus, we exercise faith in Him and His salvation, and God begins the work of transformation in us. As we follow Jesus, the Holy Spirit makes us more like Him and sends us out into the world to love humanity with God’s self-sacrificing love. We can even lay down our lives for others, as Jesus set the example for us. Our final restoration will come when we are made new and unbroken, to live with Him and love Him forever.
So when it comes to salvation in Christianity, Jesus is literally “the way,” and our love for God is the primary expression of our worship.











Chapter 3
Questioning grace

In 2013, I was given the honor of joining Ravi Zacharias International, a global team of Christian thinkers and evangelists. One of my first partnerships in Hong Kong was with a charismatic Egyptian Muslim who had originally traveled to the East to pursue his dream of becoming a famous musician, but eventually became disillusioned with the music industry and found solace in Islam. Since then, he has become a “dawati,” a Muslim dedicated to spreading Islam.
He was very warm and hospitable, and although our discussion was passionate, his tone was encouraging and constructive. At the end of the conversation we hugged each other and encouraged the audience to ask us questions before leaving. Shortly after, two children approached me, a girl and a boy of about twelve and ten years old, apparently sent by their mother. When they asked me their question, I couldn’t help but smile, because I remembered that at their age I had asked the same question to Christians, at the insistence of my parents: “If God would just forgive all Christians and none of them would go to hell, why should every Christian do good when he can sin all he wants?” Now that we have examined the differences between the message of Islam and the Christian message, this question should not come as a surprise.
In Islam, the reward for following the law is heaven and the deterrent for disobedience is hell. Why would someone make difficult moral decisions if they were promised a reward without considering the reward? Since the solution to the human problem in Islam is a law, the idea of ​​the gospel of a heart transformed by God is alien to many Muslims—as it was to these children. The disconnect between the law and the gospel also leads to other common questions that Muslims ask Christians.
The doctrine of original sin
Since Islam teaches that each individual must bear his own sin, Muslims often question the doctrine of original sin: What does Adam have to do with our standing before God? Fortunately, there is a bridge of understanding between Muslims and Christians here, since Muslims believe that Adam was cast out of the garden when he sinned. It is also useful to note that there are different views on original sin in Christian thought.
The perspective I found most helpful when I was still a Muslim is that when it all comes down to it, Muslims and Christians don't really disagree much here.
According to this view, the Bible teaches that no man is responsible for the sin of his father (Deuteronomy 24:16; Ezekiel 18:20), which means that people are not guilty of Adam’s sin itself. When we are judged, we are judged only for our sins, but Adam’s sin began somewhere. Recalling the Christian view of the destructiveness of sin, when Adam sinned, it was as if his soul had been ground to powder. Although Adam was created in the image of God, it was this broken and irreparable image of God that Adam’s children inherited (Genesis 5:3). Since mankind inherited the broken image from Adam, all humans are broken and prone to sin. They are not judged because of his sin, but because of his sin, we, his children, are all born broken. Because of his sin, we all ultimately sin.
However, regardless of our view of original sin, Christians believe that everyone sins and that their own sin is sufficient to bring God's judgment upon them. Muslims generally agree with this.
God's justice: death for the smallest sin and mercy for the greatest sinner?
According to the basic tenets of Islam, Allah weighs good deeds against bad deeds when He judges us. Muslims generally believe that someone who has committed very little sin has little to worry about. For this reason, they often ask why God demands justice for even the smallest sin. As Dawati in Hong Kong pointedly asked me during our debate, “Is it just for a judge to sentence you to death for not crossing the crosswalk?”
It was a fair question, and when I asked the same question as a Muslim, I found that Christians often did not give satisfactory answers.
Usually, they point me to Romans 6:23, which teaches that “the wages of sin is death.” My response will always be honest: “That’s another reason not to trust the Bible. It’s nonsense. Why do we give the death penalty for even the smallest sin?” I wasn’t surprised when an “invite” raised the same challenge in our debate.
I wasn’t surprised when he asked the question: “How can God be just if he’s willing to forgive serial killers and genocidal dictators? You’re telling me that Hitler could have gone to heaven if he had become a Christian?” It’s an emotional question, but one that deserves a full answer.
To fully understand, we need to remember two things: the nature of sin and the nature of God. Remember that according to Christian teaching, sin is not just doing the wrong thing. It is a rebellion against God, the source of life. Death is not a punishment for our actions so much as a consequence. God does not execute us for not crossing the crosswalk. We get run over by a truck while crossing.
Regarding God’s nature, we must remember that He loves us completely. Just as a father loves his child regardless of what the child does, our heavenly Father loves us regardless of what we have done. For example, a father loves his son even if he is a thief.
He may even turn his son over to the authorities to face the consequences of his crime, but this is out of love and a desire for ultimate rehabilitation. In the same way, God loves his children despite their sins, although he allows them to suffer the consequences of their actions on earth so that they can repent and change their ways. In all of this he always loves us, because he is our perfect Father and he is love.
But there are at least two sides to such love, and they often appear in apparent opposition to each other: mercy and justice. A father can love his criminal child with kindness, but a father must also love his victim child with justice. Mercy and justice both express God’s absolute love. This creates a dilemma: Where does God draw the line between the two? If he desires justice, where is mercy for the criminal? If he does, where is justice for the victim? He cannot arbitrarily draw that line because he is God. So where does God draw the line?
To magnify this dilemma, God is also absolute. To illustrate, it is useful to imagine a human judge who demands justice for every crime and never forgives any. Although he is not at all merciful, he would be a very just judge. Now, if God did not punish all sins, he would be less than a just human judge. Can we really believe that God’s sense of justice could be potentially less than a human’s? Of course not. Similarly, if we could imagine a human judge who forgave every crime, he would be a very merciful judge, but not at all. If God did not forgive every sin, he would be less merciful than that human judge. But God is the most merciful; his mercy cannot be destroyed by man. What is the way out of this dilemma? Where does God draw the line between mercy and justice?
This is the infinite, intelligent love of God: He does not draw the line. He has mercy on everyone who has ever sinned, while at the same time demanding justice for every sin that has ever been committed. He does this by offering the consequences of our sins. The consequence of our sins is death, and God is willing to die for all of His children.
How could Jesus die for the sins of mankind?
This is perhaps one of the most common questions raised in Muslim-Christian dialogue, and it is important to remember the Muslim motivation for asking this question: Islam emphasizes that each individual is responsible for his own sins. No human being can intercede for another.
But here is another example where agreement is close: Christians, like Muslims, believe that no human being is simply in a position to bear the sins of another. To understand this, I must remember that Christians believe that Jesus is God. Muslims believe that God is able to forgive sins, and this is exactly what Christians teach through the Bible: it is God who forgives human sins. His death on the cross is his act of mercy for our sins. Rather than just forgiving us on Judgment Day in the distant future, he paid the price for our sins at a very real point in time and place: on the cross in the first century.
Another question is closely related: “Is it only for God to pass on sins? No one can bear another’s burden, because that would be unjust.” Here it is useful to note two points. First, people voluntarily carry the burdens of others all the time, and this is perfectly just. For example, when young people want to get a loan from a bank but have no credit, they have to find a guarantor, usually a mother or father, to guarantee them. If they then fail to repay the loan, the burden falls on the parent who guaranteed them. Similarly, the Bible teaches that God, a good and loving Father, has guaranteed us. When we are guilty in the judgment, our debt belongs to God, who has paid it.
Another point that is useful to point out is that the Quran does not actually teach that no one can bear the sins of another.
Looking carefully at the relevant verses of the Quran, all five of them teach that “no bearer of burdens shall bear another’s burden.” It is not that no one can bear the sins of another, but rather that anyone who is already a sinner is not in a position to bear the sins of another. Since Muslims believe that Jesus bore no sins, it must be consistent with Islamic theology with Jesus bearing the burdens of another.
What reason do Christians have for doing good works?
When the young siblings in Hong Kong asked me their question, I briefly considered the ways I could answer. I sat down next to them, nodded to their mother, and asked them a question in return: “Do you love your mother?” A little taken aback, they replied firmly, “Of course!” With a smile, I asked them a simple question: “When she asks you to do something, like clean your room, what do you think would make her happier: if you clean your room because you love her or if you clean your room because you were afraid she would punish you?” The sister answered without hesitation, “Because we love her.” And as the words left her lips, the realization dawned on her face: obedience under threat is hardly obedience, but coercion. Christian obedience, without threat and rooted in love, is what God truly wants.
I began to explain to her and her brother that when we respond to the gospel and live as children of God, our Father changes our hearts and compels us to obey out of love. For the next few minutes, they asked me questions seriously, left completely unfinished when their mother called them over.
Chapter 4
Diagnosis and release

In the spring of 2009, when I was graduating from medical school, I volunteered with a humanitarian organization called Doctors for Peace. They were doing great work all over the world, and the project I chose to help with was to educate young mothers in the slums of Santo Domingo. The Rio Ozama runs through the Dominican capital, and its low-lying banks are home to thousands of metal shacks. Every year, rains cause the Ozama to swell into crudely constructed shacks, forcing residents to flee for weeks as the floodwaters recede, only to return to homes infested with disease. At the time, infant mortality rates were high, as young mothers often couldn’t afford quality health care and had to watch their babies die. Doctors for Peace stepped in to equip key women in these neighborhoods, whom we called resource mothers, to educate mothers of newborns about disease prevention and hygiene. Thanks to their work, rates of neonatal morbidity and mortality dropped dramatically.
When I arrived, I realized that young mothers were dealing with a life-threatening illness that was undiagnosed: depression. In their cultural circles, depression carried the stigma of an imaginary illness of unstable women. For my project, I trained resource mothers to recognize major depression and postpartum depression in the young mothers they trained. Many resource mothers, especially those who suffered from depression themselves, were surprised to learn that it was a real illness. They had always been told that depression was only in their heads and that they should just “be happy.” But no matter how hard they tried, the depression was real.
Clinical depression is a widespread illness and can be devastating if left undiagnosed. These women had tried everything, but they never found relief because they didn't know what the real problem was. When I finally talked to them about the reality of depression, many of them shed tears, relieved to hear that their illness was real and that there was help available.
I share this story because I believe the spiritual realm shares this characteristic with the physical body: if we misidentify what ails us, the cure will not work and we will continue to suffer. Islam recognizes the world as ignorance and offers the cure of Sharia, a law that must be followed. Christianity recognizes the world as broken and offers the cure of God himself, a relationship with him that leads to a transformation of the heart.
What really ails humanity, and is there a cure? From my perspective, the Bible resonates with truth: People are broken in their hearts and souls, and no matter how educated or self-centered we are, following the law doesn’t seem to be enough to fix the problem. The problem with humanity goes deeper than what we do. It’s embedded in who we are. Having spent time with depressed and vulnerable people, such as those whose lives have been affected by various addictions, I don’t think ignorance is their problem. It’s brokenness. Having seen families torn apart by abuse or anger, I know that the answer seems to lie not in knowledge or following the law, but in transformed hearts. This brings me to my second observation: Humanity seems incapable of saving itself. We perpetuate cycles of destruction in our natural selves.
Our hearts are broken, so we break other hearts. We were abused, so we take advantage in return. Our families were torn apart, so we leave broken families in our wake. When our loved ones are killed, we kill for revenge. This is the way of humanity, and we need an afterlife solution to break these cycles. We need God to save us. The Bible is that radical solution. It teaches us that God offers us that afterlife grace and forgives us regardless of our sins. His love is great: “For I am convinced that neither death nor life, nor angels nor principalities nor powers, nor things present nor things to come, nor height nor depth, nor anything else in all creation, will be able to separate us from the love of God that is in Christ Jesus our Lord.” (Romans 8:38-39). He loves us and we are forgiven. Our souls can rest in the loving Father and His all-encompassing grace.
When we realize the depravity of our sins and the depth of our rebellion against God, it surpasses the capacity of the mind to comprehend this grace. What can we do to deserve such forgiveness? Nothing! He immerses us in His infinite love and absolute mercy, even though we cannot earn it.
It is in that great flood of grace that our hearts release their poison. When we have been forgiven so much, how can we hold our fellow men accountable for so little? In His love our hearts are renewed. We no longer hold grudges, we no longer seek revenge. With the revival He has wrought in us, we want to lift up the abused and restore the broken. He transforms our hearts, and we in turn set out to transform the world around us.
And that brings me to my final point: The gospel is all about God and what God has done. God brings life to the world, and when we rebel, God saves us. When we sin against God, God pays the penalty for our sins. When we sin against each other, God offers restoring grace. The message is all about Him, not about what we can do or have achieved for ourselves.
This not only frees us from the anxiety of our salvation, but it also frees us from the pride that comes from successfully following the law and assuming that we have earned our salvation.
Because we are so free from anxiety and pride, we are free to live for others.
So the Gospel correctly identifies the problem of humanity, which is our own brokenness. Through his supernatural and heavenly grace, he transforms us and frees us from cycles of destruction. Yet he is at the center, separating us from pride and anxiety, helping us to focus on others rather than ourselves. The Gospel is not just an answer that works. It is the only answer that will work.









Part 2
Monotheism or Trinity?
Two different gods





Chapter 5
Islamic Inquisition

Although many challenge the notion that Islam spread by the sword, there is no doubt that the sword figured prominently in the early history of Islam. It was with the sword that Muslim armies swept across North Africa and conquered Iran soon after Muhammad’s death. The caliphs, Muhammad’s successors, soon faced high-profile assassinations: Umar, the second caliph of Islam, was killed by Iranian vengeful forces. Uthman, the third caliph of Islam, was besieged and massacred by Muslim rebels. Muhammad’s cousin Ali, the fourth caliph, was also assassinated by his opponents, but not before he marched on the army of Aisha, Muhammad’s young bride. This was the first Islamic civil war, often called the First Fitna, and on that day, ten thousand Muslims killed each other on the battlefield. With their bloodshed, the first seeds of Shia-Sunni discord were sown. The sword was drawn from its sheath in the first seals of Islam.
After the era of Muhammad's companions, the Umayyad Caliphate ruled the Islamic empire for a brief ninety years until, through rebellion and open warfare, the Abbasid Muslims took over and established a dynasty that lasted 750 years. The reign of the seventh Abbasid caliph is particularly interesting, as it was he who imposed the Fitna al-Khalq (Trial of the People), the Islamic Inquisition. Al-Ma'mun became caliph in 813 after a power struggle that ended with the beheading of the previous caliph, his brother. From the outset, al-Ma'mun's rule was turbulent and his allies few. Although he never embraced Shiism himself, he began to ingratiate himself with the Shiites. In 827 CE, al-Ma'mun officially declared that Muhammad's cousin Ali, the leader of the Shiites and their first Imam, was one of Muhammad's best companions. Even more striking than the move toward Shiite empathy was his alignment with Shiite rationalism: he declared the Quran to be a created book.
This was a monumental announcement, and to understand its magnitude one must pay attention to contemporary theological debates in Islam.
At that time, debates about the Quran arose among Muslim religious scholars over the doctrine of monotheism. Monotheism teaches that Allah is absolutely one; and naturally, among some thinkers, the conclusion was reached that since Allah is one, He cannot have attributes. Attributes limit His absolute unity, because they are things that He has as opposed to things that He is.
This conclusion from Islamic philosophy may take some exploration. If Allah has attributes, He must always have them, because He is unchangeable. That makes those attributes eternal. If those eternal attributes are not part of His essence, not part of His being, then something other than God existed alongside God from the beginning. The existence of eternal beings separate from the essence of Allah was an affront to Muslim religious scholars committed to monotheism, the absolute unity of Allah.
Where this philosophical debate was most significant was the issue of the Quran. It was understood that the Quran is the word of God and the attribute of His speech. Is the speech of Allah eternal? If so, something other than Allah, His speech, would be eternal. It would exist in the past eternity alongside Allah. Religious scholars who defended monotheism, such as the Jahmiyyah and the Mu’tazilah, considered this blasphemous in light of the absolute unity of Allah. It is clear why: that is, there are two eternal beings, Allah and the Quran.
To support their claim to the Bible, rationalists cited verses from the Quran that supposedly place its content in history. If the Quran refers to events in Muhammad’s life, such as the Battle of Badr, how can it be the eternal word of God? More convincingly, they cited verse 2:43 of the Quran, which says: “We have sent it down as an Arabic Quran.”
If the Quran was created, how could it be immortal? So the rationalists argued that the Quran was created and taught the doctrine of its own creation. Anyone who disagreed was an enemy of monotheism. That is why when al-Ma’mun declared the Quran to be a created book, he justified his statement by saying: “He who does not acknowledge that the Quran was created does not believe in monotheism.” Al-Ma’mun said that those who believed in the immortality of the Quran were “the worst of Muslims” and “the tongue of Satan.” He formed the Inquisition. For fifteen years, during the reign of three caliphs, Muslim thinkers in major cities who believed in the immortality of the Quran were interrogated, flogged, and threatened with execution for challenging monotheism. One of these men, Ahmad ibn Nasr al-Khuza’i, was brought to the Caliph al-Wathiq, who questioned him about the Quran. When it became clear that Ahmad did not agree with its creation but believed in its immortality, the caliph became enraged and personally beheaded him. Ahmad's head was taken to Baghdad and displayed as a warning to others who might be tempted to make a similar mistake. Although such executions were not common, the victims of the mihna included prominent religious scholars such as Ahmad ibn Hanbal, the founder of a major Sunni school of thought. It was ultimately the tenth Abbasid caliph, Mutawakkil, who abolished the mihna. He, less interested in his own theology, simply forbade discussion of the nature of the Quran.
Although the Islamic Inquisition ended in this way, the arguments about the Quran did not end there. Ultimately, the majority of Muslims supported a view that was completely opposite to the defenders of monotheism. Based on the position of a man named Ash'ari, the average Muslim today believes that the Quran is eternal, despite the problems it poses for monotheism. And how did Ash'ari counter the rationalist argument? How could the Quran be eternal without challenging the absolute unity of God? His famous answer was: "Bala kiif" or "without any way." In other words, there is no rational solution; it is true that the Quran is eternal, and this does not contradict monotheism. In this declaration, Ash'ari emphasized revelation and tradition over reason and rationality, and his approach has dominated Muslim thinkers ever since. Over time, and without any logical defense, Ash'ari's position on monotheism became the standard view, changing the history of Islam forever. His position is so strong that, ironically, those who take al-Mamun’s place today face persecution. In 1995, a highly respected Egyptian scholar named Nasr Abu Zayd was declared an apostate by the Islamic courts for believing that the Quran was partially created. His marriage was officially annulled, he was threatened with death, and he was effectively forced into exile. However, in more tolerant Muslim environments such as Turkey, debates about monotheism and the Quran continue unabated.
The Mihna is now just an obscure footnote in the annals of Islamic history. Most Muslims today are unaware that, in the eyes of their predecessors, their beliefs about the Quran were a clear violation of monotheism. Yet the old debates resonate today. If a Muslim is asked how belief in an eternal Quran does not challenge monotheism, he must still answer in the same way that Ash'ari did more than a thousand years ago: No way. There is no logical answer.
Confidence in monotheism and skepticism about the Trinity
In my experience, Muslim believers take great pride in their monotheism, their conception of the unity of God. In contrast, they often see the Trinity as an indefensible, contradictory, and polytheistic doctrine, especially since most Christians are ill-equipped to articulate the Trinity or explain how it is monotheistic. It is because of this relative confidence in monotheism that the Trinity is one of the most common topics of Christian-Muslim conversation.
As a practicing Muslim, I simply had no idea of the contradictory forms of monotheism, nor did I realize how fragile relations between Muslims were because of it. I, and most Muslims I knew, simply thought that Islam’s doctrine of God was clearly defined, unanimously agreed upon, and completely unproblematic. In other words, we uncritically believed that monotheism was an impenetrable doctrine. But the Mihna shows that Muslims have significant disagreements about monotheism, and that there are multiple views within Islam. We have previously examined two views of monotheism in the Mihna that were so opposed that they caused bloodshed. A third view, equally divisive, is that of many Sufis, called Wahdat al-Wujud.
Literally translated as "oneness of being," this doctrine teaches that the entire universe is God. This is so offensive to some Muslims that they denounce Sufis as infidels and accuse them of monotheism. In contrast, Sufi Muslims often believe that their knowledge of God is the most intimate and pure form of Islam. These are just three of the different views on monotheism that have been widely held by devout Muslims throughout Islamic history.
The average Muslim is not aware of this. Perhaps more than anything else, my ignorance was why I was so confident in criticizing the Trinity. I want to point out that the word Trinity is not found in the Bible, and the doctrine took hundreds of years to establish. How is it possible that a doctrine that is central to Christian theology has a long and complicated history? But because I had not studied the history of monotheism, I did not know that the exact same criticisms were leveled at Islam: the word monotheism is not found in the Quran, and this doctrine also took hundreds of years to adapt.
When we look more closely at these two doctrines, we find that monotheism is similar to the Trinity in other surprising ways, but as with our comparison of the Islamic and Christian worldviews, the differences are significant. By the end of this section, we will see that monotheism is ultimately self-defeating, while the Trinity is not only coherent, but is what makes us truly human.
Chapter 6
Comparison of Monotheism and Trinity

In 2003, while I was still trying to convert my friend David Wood to Islam, I started watching him debate. Of the dozens of debates we watched, the Muslim debater I respected the most was Shabbir Ali. He spoke warmly, had a powerful stage presence, and seemed to be very familiar with both Islam and Christianity. I had the privilege of watching him debate in my hometown the following year, in front of nearly a thousand Muslims and Christians. I considered him the best Muslim debater in the world, and to be honest, I was a little surprised to see him live.
If someone had told me I would debate him myself one day, I would never have believed it! In the spring of 2015, I received an invitation to debate Dr. Ali at Wayne State University in Michigan. After much prayer and deliberation, I decided to accept and deferred the matter to Dr. Ali. When I received his response, I was heartbroken: He wanted to debate the Trinity.
Even though I had been a Christian for nine years and a full-time minister for five of those years, I still felt that the doctrine of the Trinity was at best something that Christians could explain and defend. It was not a convincing doctrine and certainly not something that would grab people’s attention. But I had already agreed to the debate and let Dr. Ali set the tone, so I could not back down now. As it seemed to me, I had accidentally entered into a debate with the best Muslim apologists in the world on the most difficult issue for Christians to defend. What was I supposed to do?
There was nothing I could do but pray and study seriously! So, together with my good friends and colleagues, I spent many hours on my knees and even more hours with my nose in books.
It was then, through prayer and study, that I realized how magnificent the Trinity is and how compelling it is when we trace it throughout the Bible and understand its implications. Studying the doctrine of the Trinity greatly increased my love for God. It was also in those same studies that I first learned the story of the Mihnah. When I discovered it, I was shocked. The Islamic Inquisition on Monotheism? Did Monotheism Have an Evolutionary History? The insights I gained from studying Monotheism more closely have since given me much more confidence in presenting the Trinity to Muslims.
My first discovery was the similarities between monotheism and the Trinity. Theologically, the debates about the Trinity developed among Christians in ways that were strikingly similar to the debates about monotheism among Muslims. To understand this, it is crucial to note that the Quran and Jesus are similar in their respective religions. In the case of Islam, the Quran is the eternal Word of God, and in the case of Christianity, Jesus is the eternal Word of God (John 1:1-14). This is more than a semantic coincidence. For Muslims, the Quran is the physical expression of Allah, and for Christians, the physical expression of God is Jesus. This is why each religion teaches its own eternality: they are manifestations of the one eternal God. But both of these teachings were formed on the anvil of intrareligious debates. As we have seen, this posed a significant problem for Islamic theology, because monotheism has traditionally taught that there is no division in God. How can the Quran, something that is somehow separate from Allah, be the eternal expression of Allah without compromising His absolute unity? Bill Keefe. On the other hand, Christian theology has no problem with the Word of God being an eternal expression of God that is somehow separate from God, because it does not teach the absolute unity of God. It teaches that Yahweh is three in one. But what does it mean for God to be three in one? Let’s make sure we have a good definition of the Trinity before we look at why Christians believe it.
Definition of the Trinity: Does it contradict itself?
The Trinity, like every other monotheistic doctrine, teaches that there is only one God. If we lose this, we lose everything! Christianity has always taught that there is only one God. Where Christian theology differs from other forms of monotheism is not in the number of gods, but in the concept of the personhood of God. The doctrine of the Trinity teaches that the one God exists in three persons. This is where Muslims often accuse themselves of contradicting themselves, but it is definitely not the case, and the reason is this: person is not the same as being. Your being is the quality that makes you what you are, but your person is the quality that makes you what you are. For example, we are human. This is what we are. That is why we are called human. But what we are is not the same as who we are. If someone asks, “Who are you?” I should not answer by saying, “Human!” That answers the question, “What am I?” not who am I? I am noble. That person is me. What I am is a human being. That being is my being and the person is separate.
Unlike man, who has only one person, God has three persons. He is one being, Jehovah, in three persons: Father, Son, and Spirit. He is more than capable of such an existence because He is God. If we say that God must have only one person, like humans, then we are making God in our own image. Who are we to limit God? It is up to God to tell us who He is.
This is where the debate really should be between Muslims and Christians: about revelation, not on the conceptual level of “trinity versus monotheism,” as if our reason alone can express or even decipher the nature of God. Based on their traditional teachings, Muslims and Christians must agree that God is greater than we can imagine, more complex than we can hope to understand. We are not in a position to determine the intricacies of God’s nature. If he tells us that he is one being in one person, we are obligated to believe him. If he tells us, “I am one being in three persons,” who are we to say no to God? As believers in revelation, we must turn to the Bible to learn about God.
Trinity in the Bible
The Bible is the reason why Christians believe that God exists in three persons. The doctrine of the Trinity is the best interpretation of the Bible. Just as the Quran does not systematically explain the doctrine of monotheism, the Bible does not explain the doctrine of the Trinity in one place. However, five elements are found repeatedly in the biblical text that are best interpreted through the lens of the Trinity:
1. There is only one God (e.g., Romans 3:30)
2. The Father is God (e.g. John 6:27)
3. Jesus is God (e.g. John 20:28; Romans 5:9; 2 Peter 1:1)
4. The Holy Spirit is God (e.g. Acts 5:3-5)
5. These three are distinct persons (e.g., John 14:16-17)
So, if there are three distinct persons who are God, but there is only one God, we are naturally led to the doctrine of the Trinity: one God existing in three persons.
The verse that shows the unity of these three distinct persons is Matthew 28:19, which says, “Go therefore and make disciples of all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit.” All three persons in this verse have the same name, because they are one entity.
Now you may have noticed that the above verses are found in the New Testament. A common question Muslims ask is, “If God is a Trinity, why do we have to wait until the New Testament to see it? Why didn’t God even mention the Trinity before?” My answer often surprises them: He started with the very first verse of the Bible. Genesis 1:1 says, “In the beginning God created the heavens and the earth.” If we look closely at the word we translate “God,” Elohim, we see that it is plural. If we were to translate it literally, we would translate it “gods.” But the reason we don’t translate it that way is because the verb in the sentence is singular. The word Elohim is plural, but the verse treats it as a singular noun. So, in the very first verse of the Bible, we see that God is in a way plural, but in a way singular. This fits perfectly with the Trinitarian model: God is plural in terms of his persons, but singular in terms of his being. The oneness of God interpretation makes less sense of the verse.
Of course, if that were the only example, there wouldn’t be much, but we find three signs of the Trinity in Genesis 1. The second is in verse 26, where God says, “Let us make man in our image, after our likeness.” He is referring to himself in the plural. Why does God call himself “we”? Some respond that it is the plural of glory; a king might call himself “we,” and indeed, Allah does so throughout the Quran. Such an explanation is anachronistic, though. Biblical Hebrew does not use the plural of glory, and such a literary device had probably not yet been invented. To say that the Bible uses the plural of glory is to employ later ways of speaking and writing in the Bible, which is poor methodology. God is not referring to himself here with a royal “we.” God is indicating that he is in some way plural. Another indication of His trinity is found in verse 27, which says, “So God created man in His own image, in the image of God He created him; male and female He created them.” After emphasizing that God created humans in His own image, the Bible then says that He created them male and female. This does not mean that God has a gender, but rather that there is a plurality in His image. This is reflected in the Bible’s use of Him to refer to the human race and then changing it to them. The human race is in one sense singular, one humanity, but in another sense plural, made up of male and female. This is the image of God: singular and plural. So when we read this chapter carefully, we see that in the very first chapter of the Bible there are three indications that God is plural and singular. The Trinity is present in the Bible from the very first verse, even several times in the very first chapter.
Before we continue, it is worth noting that the Trinity is implied throughout the Old Testament. In Genesis 18, we find that Jehovah comes as a man to speak to Abraham and inform him that He will destroy Sodom and Gomorrah. Then, in verse 19:24, the Bible says, “Then the Lord rained upon Sodom and Gomorrah brimstone and fire from the Lord out of heaven.” It seems that Jehovah is both on earth and in heaven, the one on earth raining down brimstone from the one in heaven.
Lest it seem that Genesis is not so, Amos 4:11 supports this interpretation: “I overthrew some of you, as I overthrew Sodom and Gomorrah, says the Lord…” Here Yahweh refers to God in the third person. This doesn’t make much sense in a monotheistic context unless we read the Old Testament through the lens of the Trinity.
Finally, an example of an Old Testament passage that demonstrates the divinity of all three persons of the Trinity can be found in Isaiah 48:12-16. The clarity of the statement can be lost because of the many statements that emphasize the speaker’s sovereignty, but if we remove the intervening statements, the text becomes: “I am he; I am the first and I am the last. My hand laid the foundation of the earth…” “And now the Lord God has sent me, and his Spirit.” Here, the first and last say that he was sent by the Lord God with his Spirit. The next verse calls the speaker “Jehovah,” “the Savior,” and “the Holy One of Israel.” In this passage, Jehovah is sent by Jehovah and the Spirit of Jehovah, and this makes little sense unless read through the lens of the Trinity.



Trinity and Monotheism in the Quran
Just as the Bible is the reason why Christians believe in the Trinity, the Quran is the reason why Muslims deny it. The clearest refutation of the Trinity is in the Quran 4:171. After denying the divinity of Jesus, he says: "Do not say [God] is three; rather, say that God is one." That is better for you.
But when we study the Quran more closely, it seems that the Quran does not actually deny the Trinity, but polytheism, that Jesus is a second god alongside Allah. For example, after denying that Jesus is God, 5:73 says: “They have disbelieved who say: ‘God is one of three (elements that make up God)’, while there is no god but God.” If the Quran defines the Trinity as three gods, with God being one and Jesus being the second god, who is the third? Apparently this is Mary, as is clear from 5:116, where Allah says to Jesus: “O Jesus, son of Mary, did you say to the people: ‘Take me and my mother for two gods besides the God of the worlds?’” So the Trinity that the Quran denies is actually three gods, three gods: Allah, Jesus, and Mary. At this point, some Christians argue that the Quran gets the Trinity wrong, and this rejects the Quran. Muslims often argue in response that there were probably Christians who worshipped Mary, Jesus, and God as a Trinity, and that the Quranic verses are aimed at them. I prefer to avoid this argument and instead emphasize that the Quran effectively denies polytheism, three gods, not the concept of a God in three persons as Christianity has traditionally taught.
In the rest of the Quran, Allah repeatedly states that there is only one God (e.g., 16:51; 47:19; 112:1), but always as a rejection of polytheism. The Quran never rejects the possibility of the existence of one God in three persons. This omission is notable, as this had been the orthodox teaching of Christianity for centuries before Muhammad and the advent of the Quran.
Ninety-nine names of Allah Almighty
In addition to denying polytheism, the Quran also describes the exaltedness of Allah, who deliberately keeps himself away from humans. He remains behind a veil and speaks to humans only through angels. Verse 42:51 states: “And it is not for any human being that Allah speaks to him except by revelation or from behind a veil or by sending a messenger, and He reveals by His permission what He wills. Indeed, He is the Exalted in Might, the Wise.” This verse is instructive for two reasons. In the last sentence, “He is the Exalted in Might, the Wise,” we find an example of the epithets that the Quran uses to describe Allah, who is also called by ninety-nine names. The Quran tells Muslims to call Allah by all His names (17:110), and Muslims often encourage each other to learn these names in order to gain a deeper understanding of God’s character. The implication of Allah’s transcendence is also instructive: it is not for man to know Him intimately. This is also reflected in His ninety-nine names, for there is no name that indicates that Allah desires intimacy with man. Two possible exceptions are Wali and Al-Wadud. Some take Wali to mean “friend,” but it actually means “protector,” emphasizing Allah’s protection, not relationship with Him. The other word, Al-Wadud, is more promising, because it means “beloved” and is used twice in the Quran. But when we look at this word more closely, it seems to have a declarative idea in mind, not a relational one, like “love.” This may seem like splitting hairs, but it is an important distinction. Only one of Allah’s ninety-nine names can imply His intimacy with man, and looking closely at this word does not yield anything that would imply a relationship. Of course, it would help if we had more context for the word, but we find only two instances of it in the Quran. And since they are titles, the surrounding words do not clarify the meaning. However, a look at the broader context is illuminating: in 11:90, al-Wadud is used to describe Allah just one verse after the warning, “Do not let your opposition to me cause you to be afflicted by the like of what befell the people of Noah or the people of Hud or the people of Salih,” and in 85:14 it is just two verses after the warning, “Indeed, severe is the punishment of your Lord.” None of this gives any assurance that Allah desires an intimate relationship with humans.
Indeed, nothing else in the Quran seems to indicate that Allah desires a relationship with humans. This is especially true of the relationship between father and son, for the Quran specifically denies God’s fatherhood (112:3) and in 5:18 it rebukes the idea of God’s spiritual fatherhood: “And the Jews and the Christians say, ‘We are the sons of Allah and His friends.’ Say, ‘Why then does He punish you for your sins? Rather, you are only human beings among those He has created.’” This verse is telling. When Jews and Christians suggest that they are children of Allah, the Quran tells us to condemn them and inform them that they are His creatures, just like all other human beings. It should also be noted that this verse actually uses the original and best word for “love” in Arabic, hab, but it uses it to explicitly reject people’s love.
This may come as a shock to Muslims who, like me, were raised to believe that God loves us. This is a common teaching among Muslims, but it is not taught in the Quran.
The verse that is often used to describe Allah’s closeness to us is 50:16, which says that Allah is closer to people than their jugular vein. What I was not taught, and what most Muslims I know are not taught, is that this verse is about a broad threat: Allah is so close to you that He knows your destructive thoughts well and He will cast the doubters into Hell. So, in fact, as part of our understanding of monotheism, we need to have a balanced understanding of Allah’s revelation to us.
