God's will is that man pursue the relationship of a servant to his master, not the relationship of a child to his father. Nothing in the Quran indicates that God desires intimacy with humanity. We are not His beloved - just one of His creatures.
Distance vs. Relationship
There is an inherent reason why Allah is unwilling to have a relationship with humans, while Yahweh does. Because of monotheism, Allah is one. He is not inherently relational. Yahweh, on the other hand, is three persons. He is inherently relational. The implications are far-reaching.
When Muslims say “Allahu Akbar,” they mean “God is great,” and Christians agree. Both Muslims and Christians believe that God is the greatest being in the universe. But if Allah is the greatest and is inherently distant and has no desire for relationship, Islam exalts the attributes of distance and nonrelationship. On the other hand, if Jehovah is the greatest being and his essence is intimacy and love, Christianity exalts relationships and community. Remember from Chapter 2 that the one God exists as three persons who love each other selflessly. So love is the fundamental principle of God (1 John 4:8). It is out of that selfless love that he created this world and expects us to act in his reflection through love. That is precisely why the Bible teaches what it does: “‘You shall love the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul and with all your mind.’ This is the first and greatest commandment. The second commandment is like it: ‘Love your neighbor as yourself.’” (Matthew 22:37-38).
Since God is the greatest being, relationship and love are the most important commandments for humanity. According to Christian teachings, God is our Father. He loves us as a perfect father and will always love us. He wants us to have an intimate relationship with him, to turn to him with our fears and failures, with our dreams and victories. He wants us to be happy with him and in him. All of this is because God is three persons and is love in his very nature. All of this is very different from the traditional teachings about Allah in Islam.















Chapter 7
Question complexity

In the 1920s, a scientific discovery changed the world forever. Until then, physics had been one of the most unchanging and well-known fields of scientific knowledge. Isaac Newton’s principles had been proven over and over again for hundreds of years. Some 19th-century physicists, such as Pierre-Simon Laplace, even began to boast that science was now equipped to explain all motion in the entire universe, both past and future. It seemed that man had conquered physics.
But in the late 20th century, scientists began to notice gaps in their understanding of the universe. In particular, the behavior of light did not always fit within Newton’s framework. Through the work of intellectual giants like Max Planck, Albert Einstein, Niels Bohr, and Erwin Schrödinger, physicists began to unravel the hidden secrets of light. Combining insights gained in the 1920s, Paul Dirac published his principles of quantum mechanics, and the world would never be the same. One of Dirac’s students, John Polkinghorne, shares how Dirac explained this discovery to his students at Cambridge. He took a piece of chalk and cut it in half. Placing one piece on one side of his lecture and the other on the other, Dirac said that classically there is a state where the piece of chalk is “here” and one where the piece of chalk is “there,” and those are just two possibilities. However, replace the chalk with an electron, and in the quantum world there are not only states of “here” and “there” but also a whole host of other states that are mixtures of these possibilities—a bit of “here” and a bit of “there” lumped together. In other words, something can be in two places at once in ways that defy reason. According to Polkinghorne, quantum theory allows states to be combined that are classically mutually exclusive. It is this counterintuitive possibility of plurality that separates the quantum world from the everyday world of classical physics.
This understanding, a counterintuitive and seemingly contradictory truth, opens the door to 21st-century technological advances that humanity could previously only dream of: technologies like MRIs, smartphones, and Blu-Ray players all owe their existence to it. Quantum physics and fields like quantum computing and quantum optics hold the future full of hope.
As a Muslim, I was taught from childhood that the doctrine of the Trinity was impossible because it was too complex and even contradictory. But the more humanity explores the universe, the more we realize its astonishing complexity. As my eyes were opened to the deeper truths of the universe, I came to accept that complexity does not in any way invalidate the Trinity. Why should we assume that the Creator is simpler and easier than our creation?
I might suggest that it should be the other way around: by definition, we cannot understand God. If God created our minds, then He must be greater than our understanding. Who are we to ask Him to be simple enough for us to understand? But as we have seen, the Trinity is not contradictory. There is no contradiction in the claim that there is one God in three persons. The common Muslim challenge that the Trinity is unrealistically contradictory and complex is not a valid problem. Quite the contrary, as we learned earlier through the story of the homeland, the Islamic formulation of divine simplicity poses a problem in explaining the eternality of the Quran. As we will now learn, the simplicity of monotheism has another flaw.
The Trinity and the Self-Sufficiency of God
Let us consider once again the basic teaching of monotheism: God is one. This means that in eternity past, before He created anything, Allah was alone. One, all alone. Until He chose to create the world and Allah had something to relate to. This is a major theological problem, because Islam teaches, through the ninety-nine names and more, that Allah is a relational being. But how could He be a relational being if He had no relation to the world before He created it?
It is important to note that I am using the word relationship here not to mean intimacy but to mean a relationship with something else. For example, Islam teaches that Allah, al-Rahman and al-Raheem, are the Most Gracious and the Most Merciful. Every believing Muslim knows this, as these are two of the most common names for Allah. But these attributes imply that Allah is a relationship, that He is kind to one and merciful to another. He may have the ability to be kind on his own, but he cannot be merciful unless there is something else. The same is true of mercy; something else must exist and be the object of His mercy for Allah to be inherently merciful.
So for Allah to be truly merciful and compassionate, He must first create the world. In other words, Allah is dependent on His creation in order to be Allah. He cannot be Rahman or Ar-Raheem unless He creates the world for mercy or compassion. Allah’s attributes are dependent on creation. Therefore, as in traditional Islamic teachings, it is illogical to say that Allah is inherently merciful or Rahman or any other attribute, whether or not it is in the ninety-nine names. If Allah is one, He needs His creation to have a relationship.
On the other hand, the doctrine of the Trinity teaches that the three persons of God have always loved each other with a self-sacrificing love. God has always been intimate, always loving. His mercy and justice are not conditional on his creation, for these express his eternal love for humans. That love was never conditional on the existence of humans.
Because of monotheism, Allah is dependent on man to be God. Yahweh, because of his triune nature, is truly independent and self-sufficient. Thus, the simplicity of monotheism is a fatal flaw for orthodox Islam in two ways: one is its inability to explain the eternal Quran, and the other is that it makes Allah dependent on his creation. The complexity of the Trinity, far from being self-contradictory, is what makes him logically consistent and self-sufficient. A more pertinent question that Muslims ask concerns the Bible. Jews and Muslims agree that God is a unity, not three persons. Doesn’t the Jewish understanding of monotheism stem from the Bible, and does it not deny the Trinity?
Shema: "The Lord our God, the Lord is one."
The most important scripture for examining Jewish monotheism is the Shema. It is similar to the Jewish Testimony and is found in Deuteronomy 6:4. It says, “Hear, O Israel: The LORD our God, the LORD is one.” But we must be careful to understand the Hebrew and its subtleties. In the first place, this verse could be translated, “Hear, O Israel: The LORD our God, the LORD is one.” But even if we translate it as “The LORD is one,” what kind of “one” is the Hebrew referring to? Absolute unity, as in the teachings of monotheism, or a composite unity, such as the man and woman who make up the human race, as we saw in Genesis 1? The Hebrew word for “one” used in the Shema is “echad,” and this word is often used in the Old Testament to refer to a composite unity. To illustrate, let’s look again at Genesis 1. Verse 5 says that the evening and the morning were one day. In other words, a day is a combination of evening and morning. Since the Bible refers to something that is one but is made up of many parts, it uses the word echad. In Numbers 13:23, a cluster of grapes is called an echad because a cluster is made up of many grapes. In Ezekiel 37:17, Ezekiel is told to hold two sticks together as if they were one. Again, in Hebrew, the word echad describes this compound unity. As a final example, the Bible, referring to the image of a man and a woman, says that through marriage, a man and a woman become one. The word the Bible uses to describe two in one? Of course, echad. So when the Shema says, “The Lord is one,” the Bible uses that word specifically, allowing it to mean a composite unity. Putting this together with our Bible references in the previous chapter, the Bible tells us that there is only one God, Jehovah. The New Testament is clear that three separate persons are each God, but not each other. We must conclude, then, that there is one God in three persons. The Old Testament, starting on the first page, explicitly suggests that God is several, and the Shema uses verbs that we would expect if Jehovah wanted us to know that He is one God in three persons.
Jews and the Trinity
When I mention these findings to Muslims, I am often asked why Jews do not interpret the Bible in such a way that the one God may have multiple persons or expressions. When I reply, they are surprised that some do! One example is the Zohar, the famous and highly respected foundational text of Jewish mystical thought. Noting the Shema, it says: “These three are one... So the secret of the three divine manifestations, which are designated by ‘the Lord, our God, the Lord,’ is that the three states constitute a single unity.”
But the belief that Yahweh existed in multiple person or terms goes back beyond the mystical rabbis of Jesus’ own time. Jewish scholar Alan Segal argues that some first-century Jews had a “dualistic” conception of God. Daniel Boyerin, himself an Orthodox Jew and scholar, argues that the rabbis declared such notions heretical only in response to Christian theology, not before. In other words, according to some leading Jewish scholars, views such as the Trinity were already present among the Jews when Jesus taught in Israel. Only after the Jews and Christians had parted ways did the rabbis declare such notions heretical because they were a reaction to Christian theology.
Chapter 8
Are Muslims and Christians
Do they worship one God?

In April 2015, I had the opportunity to encourage Christian students to connect with their Muslim neighbors. It was a church service at a Christian college in upstate New York, and at the center of the largely Caucasian audience was an Iraqi woman, clearly recognizable by her hijab, the veil that Muslim women typically wear. I have learned the hard way that whenever I address an audience, I must do my best to present my message without offending people more than I have to. If people are going to find the gospel offensive, I would rather it not be because of my presentation. So at the appointed hour, I tried to read her response to my argument and testimony, but she showed no reaction. She simply listened. At least she wasn’t overtly offended—so far so good!
At the end of my talk, a woman in the audience asked me a controversial question: “Do Muslims and Christians worship the same God?” My answer to this question had offended Muslims in the past, and I was worried that it would do so again. But another thing I’ve learned the hard way is that when a question is asked, I should try to answer it directly. So I said a quick prayer in my heart and began.
I began by stating the obvious: Christians worship the Trinity, while Muslims worship God as a unity. This is not an accidental difference. Islam goes to great lengths to condemn the Trinity as blasphemy (4:171). The Quran rejects the relational aspects of Allah, saying that He is not the Father (5:18) and that He is not the Son (112:3). This establishes its own doctrine of God, monotheism, in complete opposition to the Trinity, and this doctrine becomes the central doctrine of Islamic theology.
Most people who say that Christians and Muslims worship the same God are aware of this difference, but they consider it relatively insignificant. It is not an insignificant difference, though. It has major implications. Since humanity is made in the image of the Triune God, love is woven into our nature. The Trinity gives us the most solid and powerful basis for self-sacrifice and altruism.
This is an important point to unpack. Of course, many people are very altruistic, regardless of their worldview. As secular humanists show, one does not need to believe in God to truly care for others. There are even people who do not believe in any kind of morality, but still want to care for people. Ultimately, however, such unfounded altruism is a feeling, something a person just wants to do. Unless someone believes in a transcendent basis for altruism, their desire to care for people is irrelevant and fleeting, little more than a whim. According to this amoral worldview, there is nothing worthy of being kind. Even if someone wants to be altruistic, the next moment it is perfectly consistent with their worldview to want to be selfish.
Others believe in an ethic that is essentially derived from evolutionary theory. People should care for each other because the work of life is for survival, and we should care for other members of our species. Although this is a more basic basis for altruism, it is ultimately still a weak one: Why should I, a thinking individual, participate in the work of life? What if I don’t want humanity to survive? Moreover, if my primary purpose in doing so is to care for my own species and thus to care for myself, is it really altruistic of me to care for others?
Thus atheistic altruism, while it may be sincere and feasible, is ultimately either sentimental or utilitarian and selfish. To more firmly ground altruism, one must consider theistic models. In the case of Islam, Allah commands Muslims to care for orphans, widows, the poor, the wayfarer, and others. A Muslim can obey out of selfish motives, such as a desire to avoid hell or to gain paradise, or he can obey out of a selfless desire to please God. Thus theism, as in the case of Islam, offers a deeper form of altruism.
But the Christian model of theism goes even further. Much further. Remember that God’s eternal love is inherent in who He is. Each person of the Trinity loves others selflessly. It was out of this selfless love that God created humanity. In other words, we are made in the image of a loving, selfless God, so by nature we are designed to be loving and selfless. When we are self-centered instead of self-sacrificing, we act against our nature.
From a Christian perspective, people should love others selflessly, not just because it’s a good idea, not just because it helps our species survive, not just because it brings us a reward, and not just because it pleases God. People should love selflessly because that’s who we are. Humans are made in the image of a selfless God. Loving others is what makes us truly human.
No other worldview has so deeply embedded in humanity the commitment to love and care for one another. As a foundation for altruism, nothing surpasses the Trinity. So I answered the woman’s question by emphasizing that Christians worship the Triune God, and that this makes a huge difference in how we view ourselves and the world around us. Shortly after my answer, the Iraqi woman approached me. In a thick Arabic accent, she thanked me for my talk, and I thanked her in a thick American accent for listening. I asked her what brought her to the Christian college, assuming her friend was a Christian.
She replied that just five weeks earlier, she had seen Jesus in a dream. He told her to go to a certain church in her town to learn the truth about Him. At the church, a woman befriended her and shared the Gospel with her. Without hesitation, she renounced her Islamic faith and accepted the gospel of Jesus. From that day on, she had learned everything she could about God, and when her friend found out that I was speaking at a Christian college, she brought her with her. It was her friend who had raised the question: Do we worship the same God?
So it turns out that the Muslim woman in question is not a Muslim after all, but a new Christian sister. Out of curiosity, I asked her friend why she was still wearing the hijab, to which she replied that they were so busy discussing Jesus that they hadn’t had a chance to talk about it yet! I was so happy to hear that answer. There is nothing inherently wrong with the veil, and focusing on it would be a waste of time compared to learning the truth about Jesus. The question of whether Muslims and Christians worship the same God is a complex one, and there is much more that could be said. Ultimately, when we understand the Trinity, we realize that this doctrine is not just a theological curiosity. It has far-reaching implications for how we live and how we see the world, and it makes the Christian God decidedly different from the Muslim God. It is what makes God unique and eternal in His love. This is how God can be in us through the Holy Spirit, while being over us as Father, and suffering for us in the Son.
And it is this Son that most distinguishes the Christian God from the Muslim God. We must learn about him not only in light of the Trinity but also in light of his life on earth. Now that we understand the doctrine of the Trinity, that the one God exists as three persons, we will better understand what Christians mean when they say that the second of these three persons came into this world as a man named Jesus.

















Part 3
Muhammad or Jesus?
Two different founders







Chapter 9
Council of Nicaea

The church had no political or military power in its first three hundred years, living by faith and enduring persecution. Christian martyrdom did not mean dying with a sword in hand, but rather dying for the faith. Jesus’ words were still fresh in their minds—“For all who take the sword will perish by the sword” (Matthew 26:52)—and the young church fought no wars.
Ironically, the Roman effort to kill was the midwife of the church. It was the Roman prefect of Judea, Pontius Pilate, who unwittingly helped lay the foundation of the Christian faith by ordering the crucifixion of Jesus. A few years later, Nero infamously accused Christians of the crime he himself had committed. The Roman historian Tacitus reports that Nero “wrongly accused those who were called Christians of sin and punished them with the most terrible tortures.” It was during this period of imperial persecution that Peter and Paul were executed. But by killing Jesus and the Church Fathers, the Roman Empire strengthened the spirit of Christianity: many embraced martyrdom in order to have the honor of following the Lord and his apostles in suffering for God. Tertullian bears witness to this indomitable spirit with his famous words: “Whenever we are shot for your sake, we multiply. The blood of Christians is like seed.”
Christians faced these imperial or local persecutions in parts for almost three hundred years, and the night grew darker just before dawn. In February 303 AD, Emperor Diocletian ordered the destruction of a church and its Bible, and issued a decree that the same should be done to churches throughout the Roman Empire. The historian Eusebius of Caesarea, an eyewitness to these events, tells us in his Ecclesiastical Histories: “Royal decrees were everywhere issued ordering that churches be razed to the ground, and that the Bible be destroyed by fire.” He continues that the persecutions intensified in the days to come: “The first decrees that others issued were that… those who refused to offer sacrifice to the Roman gods should be tormented with innumerable tortures. Who could then count the multitude of martyrs?” It was during this, the greatest and most sustained persecution of Christians by the Roman Empire, that a young man named Constantine rose to the position of emperor.
In October 312 AD, Constantine was preparing to fight an army twice his size. According to Eusebius, as he led his troops into battle, he had a vision: a shining cross above the sun, with the words written in the sky: “By this means, conquer.” The vision astounded Constantine, but that night, Jesus appeared to him in a dream and told him to use this symbol as a shield in all his battles. So Constantine ordered his men to mark their shields with the Christian symbol. One can only imagine that the soldiers did not want to have the symbols of a persecuted minority on their shields, but they complied. The next day, the soldiers won a decisive victory, and Constantine marched into Rome as the victorious emperor.
After his supernatural encounter, Constantine immediately attempted to reverse Diocletian’s persecution. He granted freedom of worship to all religions, Christianity and other persecuted minorities, with the Edict of Milan in 313 AD. This moment was a turning point in the history of the church: not only were Christians allowed to worship without risking their lives, but they were also allowed to have their own Bibles and the ability to assemble openly. Finally, for the first time since the crucifixion of Jesus, all Christian leaders could meet openly to discuss the Bible and their teachings under imperial protection, without reprisal.
The Council of Nicaea and the Arian Controversy
The bishops' first and greatest concern, shared by Constantine, was the growing dissension in the church over a man named Arius and the teachings he espoused. To examine the situation of Arius, Constantine invited 1,800 bishops from across the Roman Empire, in modern-day Spain, to meet at Nicaea in 325 AD. Although he offered to provide transportation and lodging, only 318 were able to come. Some, like the bishop of Rome, were too old to attend, while others were physically unable to make the journey due to infirmities from persecution or other causes. Those who were able to come were described by the church historian Theodoret as "the assembly of martyrs." He describes Paul of Neo-Caesarea, a bishop in modern-day Turkey, as an example of someone who had suffered greatly under the tyranny of Licinius. He was deprived of the use of both hands by the use of a red-hot iron, which had severed and destroyed the nerves that moved the muscles. Other bishops of the council, such as Paphnutius of Egypt, had their eyes gouged out and their limbs torn to pieces. Yet they bravely endured the long journey to Nicaea to participate in the discussions. These men had experienced the greatest persecution of Christians the Church had ever seen, and rather than compromise their faith, they were tortured and torn to pieces. These were the men who were now summoned to debate the controversial teachings of Arius: “That the Son had a beginning, but God was without beginning... He is neither part of God nor of anything secondary.”
The council allowed Arius to share and defend his position. To clarify the issue, Arius emphasized that he believed Jesus to be divine. As he had said in a letter to the bishop of Nicaea, Arius believed that the Son “deserves no substance. But that by his own will and counsel before time and before the ages, he subsisted as perfect God, only begotten and unchangeable, and was not before he was begotten, or created, or purposed, or established. For he was not undone.” Arius therefore believed that Jesus was indeed a deity, but not God. The Father “begotten, created, or purposed, or established” Jesus before time and the creation of the world. God had created a second God. When what Arius believed was made clear, the council recognized his teachings as heresy and, since the church had always taught monotheism, declared Arius a heretic. With the agreement of 316 bishops, the council promulgated the Nicene Creed, the first church resolution in history.
Nicaea in popular memory
Seventeen hundred years later, the Council of Nicaea holds a prominent place in public memory, but the details are often misunderstood, as demonstrated by an encounter I had with a young Muslim man in June 2010. David Wood and I attended an Arab festival in Dearborn, Michigan. We had heard that the event attracted more than two hundred thousand Arabs, and we thought it would be a great opportunity to talk to people about Jesus. So, wearing a T-shirt that said “Jesus Always Loves You,” I walked through the festival with David and waited for people to approach us with questions.
We had an exciting day with many enlightening discussions. As we were leaving, a group of teenagers stopped us and indicated that they had questions. As a youth pastor at the time, I was always encouraged by connecting with teens interested in faith issues, but this was especially encouraging because they had an air about them that reminded me of my own childhood: a confident zeal for Islam and a complete skepticism about Christianity. I was not at all surprised when one of the first questions they asked concerned the Council of Nicaea. One teenager commented that Nicaea was “the first time they concluded that Jesus was God.” I was truly impressed that he correctly identified the theme of the council. In the mosques I attended as a youth, it was often repeated that Christians decided at the Council of Nicaea to remove books from the Bible and put others in. We found this to be completely unfounded. The books of the Bible were not even discussed at Nicaea. I have since noticed that for whatever reason, people decide to bring all sorts of church conspiracies into this council. So it was encouraging that the teenager got the point right, but completely misunderstood the text of the statement. This was not the time when the church finally decided that Jesus was God.
On the other hand, when the Church was finally able to meet for a council after three hundred years of intermittent persecution, their first concern was to lay down without debate the cornerstone of their faith: “Who is Jesus?” All 318 bishops present agreed that Jesus was divine. No one suggested that Jesus was merely a man. And when both sides had heard all the arguments, more than 99 percent agreed that Jesus was no less than God himself. Not just a man, not just a prophet, not just a goddess, but God: “God of gods.” There is a reason for this unanimous view: the belief that Jesus is “in the very nature of God” was one of the earliest beliefs of the Church. The Gospel has always been about God and what he has done for humanity: he created them, he loved them, he lived among them, he saved them, he guides them, and his kingdom is at hand. Without understanding this, the Bible cannot be understood. The doctrine of the divinity of Jesus makes a huge difference in how the Christian faith works and what it means to follow Jesus. Just as the divinity of Jesus is central to Christian theology, the prophethood of Muhammad is central to Islamic theology. There is a reason why Muslims must affirm the status of the prophet every time they recite the Shahadah. If Muhammad is not a prophet, Islam will fall apart.
When people gloss over Christianity and Islam as if they are the same, they ignore this fundamental fact: Jesus has a very different status in Christianity than Muhammad does in Islam. Divinity is distinct from prophethood.
The positions of Jesus and Muhammad, apart from being role models for their followers, are in no way parallel unless we combine Islamic and Christian teachings. Let's carefully consider the Christian view of Jesus and compare it to the Muslim view of Muhammad so that we can understand exactly how they differ and why it matters.









Chapter 10
Comparison of the Apostle and Christ

It’s been a decade since I left Islam, and in that time I’ve had some frank conversations with my extended family. Amidst the many abstract theological issues, I try to pay attention to the more internal questions, because they tend to provide insight into a person’s true place. One question I’m often asked is, “We respect Jesus, so why can’t Christians respect Muhammad?” In a sense, it’s a fair question. Muslims rarely, if ever, say anything negative about Jesus, and I’ve heard Christians say very negative things about Muhammad. While I agree that Christians should tread carefully when covering sensitive topics, this question is a classic example of the inappropriate blending of Muslim and Christian perspectives.
From my experience, what Muslims generally “respect” is the Islamic understanding of Jesus, not the Christian understanding.
For example, a family member who is particularly close to me once lamented that Muslims honor Jesus, while Christians do not honor Muhammad, but the next time he said that Jesus would be a “weak and helpless God” if he died on a cross. When I protested that this was disrespectful to Jesus, he did not understand it. It took me a few moments to show him that from my perspective, this was very offensive. However, he hardly noticed the offense, because he was so immersed in his own perspective. What he needed to understand the Christian perspective was an explanation of who Jesus was from the beginning.
A God who comes to this world
As we saw in the previous chapter, the Quran explicitly states that Allah is behind the curtain, so Muslims consider God to be an entity that does not enter this world. However, Christians do not believe this, because their Bible teaches exactly the opposite: the Bible repeatedly shows that God has come among his people.
Beginning in the third chapter of Genesis, the very beginning of the Bible, we find God walking in the garden where Adam and Eve were (Genesis 3:8). In Genesis 18, God appears as a man to Abraham and speaks to him. In Exodus 34:5-6, God stood with Moses and walked before him. In Exodus 24:9-11, Aaron, Moses, and seventy-two elders all saw God at the same time and even ate supper with Him. In an even more tangible experience, Jacob wrestled with God in Genesis 32:24-28. In Exodus 13:21, when the Hebrews needed guidance in the wilderness, God personally led them as a pillar of smoke by day and a pillar of fire by night. In another very important account, God commanded the Hebrews to build a tabernacle for Him, a place where He would come down and “dwell among them” (Exodus 25:8-9). After it was built, God filled the tabernacle with His glory (Exodus 40:34). These many appearances of God on earth are often called theophany.
Another theology in the Torah, Exodus 3:4, actually has parallels in the Quran. In this passage, God calls Moses from a burning bush. The bush was burning because of God’s presence, which is why God says, “Take off your sandals, for the place where you are standing is holy ground” (verse 5). There was nothing holy about the ground itself, but God’s presence made it holy. God’s presence in the bush is reflected in Quran 27:7-14. As Moses approaches the bush, a voice from it calls out, “Blessed is he who is in the fire.” The Quran also imagines God physically in the fire. That the ground was holy is reflected in 20:12, where God says, “Take off your sandals, for you are in the holy valley of Toi.” As in the Bible, there is nothing inherently holy about the valley. If it is sanctified by the presence of God, it makes perfect sense. However, regardless of how a Muslim interprets the Quran, Christians traditionally interpret the Torah verses as theophany. Yahweh is a God of love who desires intimacy with his people, so he comes among them.
God promised to come from childhood.
In the days of the prophet Isaiah, God informed His people that He was about to do something special: He would be born into this world as a child. In Isaiah 9:6, the Bible says, “For unto us a child is born, unto us a son is given: and the government shall be upon His shoulder: and His name shall be called Wonderful, Counselor, Mighty God, Everlasting Father, Prince of Peace.” Christians have understood this verse from the very beginning in Jesus: God, who has come to this earth many times before, is announcing His future incarnation. The Almighty God is born as a child. This comes just a few chapters after Isaiah 7:14, which says, “Behold, a virgin shall conceive, and bear a son, and shall call his name Immanuel.” Putting this together, Christians believe that hundreds of years before Jesus, the Hebrews were told that God would be born among them.
The Word became flesh and tabernacle among us.
When God finally lived among his people, a new era began. The Bible from this era is called the New Testament. According to early church tradition, John, one of Jesus’ three closest disciples, wrote an account of Jesus’ life in the New Testament called the Gospel of John.
He begins his narrative by reminding readers of who Jesus was before he lived on earth. John 1:1-3 is a very important passage for understanding the Christian view of Jesus. As we learned in the previous chapter, Christians believe that there is one God who exists as three persons, the second person being the Word. Just as the Quran is in one sense separate from God and in another sense an expression of His divine knowledge and speech, Christians believe that the Word is in one sense separate from God and in another sense a part of Him. This is precisely the teaching of John 1:1: “In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God.” The Word was with God (i.e., separate from Him in one sense). Yet the Word was God (an expression of Him in another sense).
The next verse teaches the doctrine that the Word is eternal. Again, just as Muslim theologians have classically taught that the Quran is the created knowledge and speech of Allah, so the Bible teaches that the Word is eternal alongside the Father. “In the beginning was with God” (John 1:2). From the very beginning, before time, the Word was present with God. It had to be so, because he was the expression of God, who is eternal.
Also, just as Muslim scholars have traditionally taught that the Quran is best understood as the knowledge or speech of God, the Greek word for word is Logos, and it encompasses two concepts: reason and speech. These two meanings are included in the English derivatives logic and dialogue. When God created the world, He used His divine reason and speech. These are the ideas embodied in the concept that God the Father created the world through the Logos, found in John 1:3. “Without Him was not anything made that was made.” Thus the world was made through the Logos, the Word—the divine reason and speech.
Finally, departing from any parallel in Islamic theology, we find John 1:14: “And the Word became flesh and dwelt among us.” The Logos, the Word who is God, became a man and lived among us.
According to the disciple John, it is Jesus Christ. Jesus is eternal and it was through him that the world was created.
What is interesting about John 1 is that it is full of theology from the Torah, emphasizing that this is not a new belief but a continuation of what God had done in the time of Moses and the prophets of old. An example is the phrase John chooses in verse 14. When he says the Word “dwelt among us,” he is literally saying the word tent among us, reminding his Jewish readers of the Almighty God in Exodus 40:34 and what God had done. In the time of Moses His glory once again dwelt among men like a tent, but this time by coming as a man.
This is the Christian view of Jesus: the God who created the world, walked with Adam, talked with Abraham, wrestled with Jacob, stood with Moses, ate with Aaron, and led the Hebrews. His prophecy that he would be born for us as a son of man. Jesus is “God with us,” the second person of the Trinity, the eternal Word through whom the world was created.
He is a real man.
Before we continue, it is important to emphasize that Christians do indeed believe that God became a man. He was born as a baby, ate, grew in wisdom and stature, wept, suffered, and died. He did not just appear as a man. He became a man, but by the word transformation, Christians do not mean a change in the nature of God. God never changed during any of the theologies in the Torah, and He did not change during His incarnation. Christians traditionally teach that God, rather than changing, took on a human nature in addition to His divine nature. This doctrine is called the hypostatic union. When the Bible says that Jesus “grew in wisdom and stature” or that Jesus died by crucifixion, Christians believe that this is speaking with respect to His human nature, not His divine nature. Christians have traditionally taught that everything Jesus did on this earth, He did as a man. He taught as a human teacher, received prophecies as a human prophet, and performed miracles as a man with divine power. That is why he tells his disciple Philip that people can do the works that Jesus did, and even greater works (John 14:12).
But even though Jesus was a man, he was not of Adam’s lineage, as we should recall from chapter 1, and he passed on his broken image to his children. Jesus, who was born into the world by the Holy Spirit (Luke 1:35), was human as humans should be: invincible. Because Jesus never sinned as a man, and because he also had a divine nature, he could forgive the sins of mankind by taking the punishment for sin. This is one reason why Christian theology would not work if Jesus were not God. Also, because Jesus was never broken by sin, he lived a perfect life as an example of how the rest of us should live. Not an example in the sense of someone who made all the right choices, but an example in the sense of a perfect prototype, a model after whom all humans are made.
Prophets in Islam: Men to Follow
Functioning as a model is perhaps the only similarity between Jesus in Christianity and Muhammad in Islam, but even in this similarity the mechanics are different. For Muslims, Muhammad is the perfect model not because he is the unbroken image in which we are made, but because he is the most perfect example of man, the greatest who ever lived. Indeed, for some Muslims, Muhammad's greatness is legendary.
When I was ten, my mother enrolled me in a Sunday school run by our local mosque in Norfolk, Virginia. The mosque itself was Sunni, but the teachers and students came from many branches of Islam. I had two teachers there, a Sunni Islamic studies teacher and a Shiite Quran recitation teacher. For all practical purposes, we believed the same things, which is why my mother could enroll me there. But of course there are differences between traditions, and sometimes I heard things there that I hadn’t heard before.
One day, my Islamic studies professor was talking about the nature of Muhammad. He emphasized that “Muhammad played many roles: prophet, merchant, general, husband, father, son, and so on. He excelled in each role.” So far so good. That’s what I’d always been taught. But then he said something new: “Muhammad never showed emotional weakness. If he cried and his tears fell on the ground, the earth would become barren!”
Although my parents greatly revered Muhammad, this was a step or two further than they had ever gone before. Not all Muslims believe in this kind of superhuman status for Muhammad, but Muslims traditionally consider him the greatest man who ever lived, and this account shows how highly some Muslims hold the Prophet of Islam.
Prophethood in Islam is more than a role. It is a position. Prophets are greater than ordinary people, appointed by Allah to lead a people. To consider Muhammad the chief of the prophets is indeed a great claim. It was on the basis of this prophetic position that Muhammad established his authority and preached Islam.
Since Allah had made him a prophet, the religion he taught was true and should be followed. Muslims, based on their special status with Allah, have two beliefs about prophets: that all prophets are innocent, and that Allah hears the cries of the prophets in persecution and saves them from death. However, the Quran seems to teach that prophets sinned (e.g. 28.15-16; 38.24-25; 47.19), and that they were often killed (e.g. 2.61; 3.183; 5.70). Accordingly, Muslim scholars tend to disagree with these popular views, although they are not unanimous. Regardless, most Muslims believe that Muhammad was specifically innocent, especially because God commands Muslims to follow him (33.21).
The Prophet and the Sharia
Muhammad’s status as the perfect model is one reason why Muslims have gone to great lengths to record his life. In the centuries after his death, more than five hundred thousand narrations of Muhammad’s life were in wide circulation. As we learned in Chapter 1, these narrations are called hadith, and they record details of Muhammad’s life, from childhood anecdotes to the decisions he made as a general and statesman.
As my Sunday school teacher taught, Muhammad had a series of roles and he played them well. So when Muslims want an example of the perfect husband, or the perfect statesman, or the perfect general, or the perfect businessman, they turn to Muhammad’s life for exemplary guidance. The hadiths record what Muhammad said and did on thousands of occasions, and they told the Muslim people to do it in turn.
That is why, unlike the teachings of Jesus, which focus on principles and transformation of the heart, guidance in Islam is in the details. Sharia comes to the Muslim people in the form of thousands of decisions by jurists and scholars familiar with the traditions of Muhammadan life. Everything goes back to the exemplary life of the Prophet.
Thus, the primary basis for accepting Islam is that Muhammad is a prophet of God and that the guidance he brings, the Sharia, is largely interpreted from his life record as an exemplary leader. For these reasons, Muhammad's prophethood is fundamental to Islam.
Misunderstanding of Jesus the Christian and Muhammad the Muslim
Some early Orientalists, noting the great emphasis placed on the person of Muhammad in Islam, called Muslims “Mohammedans.” When I was a Muslim, this title bothered me because it seemed to imply that we worship Muhammad. I now realize that this was simply a careless design: the focus of the Christian faith is entirely on the person we worship, so it is appropriate to call followers of Christ “Christians.”
In Islam, although much attention is paid to Muhammad, he is not someone Muslims worship, so it is offensive to many Muslims to call them Muhammadans. The same kind of misunderstanding regularly occurs in Muslim-Christian conversations, when Muslims assume that Christians worship a man. Since I became a Christian, many Muslims have accused me of deliberately worshipping creation rather than the Creator, but this is because they ignore Islamic views that Christians hold: that Jesus was only a man and that God did not come into this world. If Muslims and Christians could understand each other on these issues, the dialogue would be much more fruitful. Now let’s look at some common questions that are clarified by what we have learned so far.
Chapter 11
Questioning the God-Man

In the summer of 2012, I spent eight weeks in Oakland, California, studying Arabic through Middlebury College. I had just graduated from Duke University, where I had focused on the Bible and the Quran during my master’s degree. Although my mother had taught me how to recite Arabic as a child, I could not use the language to communicate, so I knew that a greater familiarity with Arabic would be a great help in my future graduate studies. I entered Middlebury just above the introductory level, which meant that I would be prohibited from communicating in any language other than Arabic for the entire eight weeks. The program was so strict about this rule that we had to sign a contract the day we arrived. No English, anywhere, for two whole months. Not even in the evenings or on the weekends!
Until then, I hadn’t realized how important language was for stress relief. No jokes, no storytelling—just lots of hand gestures and listening to the upperclassmen speak with great intensity. It was a very difficult time, but it forced us to learn how to cope quickly. Within a month, we were able to communicate with each other in terribly poor Arabic. Luckily, I had a friend near Oakland who was also an Arabic language student, and she regularly contacted expatriates in the area. She asked me if I would be willing to meet one of her Muslim friends from Saudi Arabia, and I happily agreed. That afternoon, I met a young student named Sahar. She told me about life as a woman in Saudi Arabia, including how the government had asked her to get permission from her younger brother so she could study in America. When I asked her what would happen if she refused, she replied, “She knows better than to say no to me!” The conversation soon turned to religious matters. Sahar pointed out that she was a staunch Muslim and had no intention of converting, but she had questions about the Christian faith. After asking many questions, she finally asked the one that seemed to be the most problematic for her. How can you believe that Jesus was born through a woman’s birth canal and had to use a bathroom? Aren’t these things beneath God?”
This is a very common question, but now we should be able to see why Muslims ask this question: Allah does not enter this world in Islam, while Yahweh has done so many times. Allah remains behind the scenes and sends messengers, while Yahweh is intimate and walks among us. When we remember that Yahweh is different from Allah, and that Jesus is the second person of the Trinity, the answer to many similar questions becomes readily apparent.
How could God die, and who was ruling the world at the time of Jesus' death?
These two questions were the first I asked David about the divinity of Jesus as a Muslim, and they are the most common questions Muslims ask me now. Since Islam has no concept of divine incarnation, these questions are understandable. Indeed, these are questions that Christians should ask themselves at some point, but they are not difficult to answer when we remember what we have learned in this chapter.
When someone asks me, "How can God die?" I ask for clarification, because the question can be posed from several angles. Almost always the questioner says something like, "God is immortal, so He cannot die." To that, I respond with a question of my own. I know what you mean, but let me ask you a question: When humans die, does our soul cease to exist? Of course, Muslims will answer, "No, our soul does not die," to which I reply, "So even when we die as humans, it is the body that dies. It is not that we give up our existence altogether. It was the same with Jesus: He was killed out of respect for his earthly body, but God did not cease to exist."
However, sometimes by asking, “How could God die?” Muslims are essentially asking, “Who ruled the universe?” There are many possible answers to this question, but the one I prefer is simple: the Father. That is why it is essential that Christians explain the Trinity adequately to Muslims if they are to address such issues. The Father is not the Son, and the Father did not die on the cross. It is unjust for God to punish Jesus for the sins of man.
This leads to another kind of question, one that even informed Muslims ask. During my closing statement in 2015, Dr. Shabbir Ali used some of the most scathing language I have ever heard to challenge the Bible. He said that if a father sent his son to die for the sins of the world, then that was “cosmic child abuse.” What kind of father is God if he punishes his son for the sins of others? At this point, we should be able to easily see the problem with this assessment: Christians do not believe that God punishes a random victim. Jesus is God. The judge himself voluntarily pays on behalf of the criminal. In contrast to Dr. Ali’s caricature, a more appropriate picture is shared by Brennan Manning in his book Ragamuffin Bible. In 1935, New York City Mayor Fiorello LaGuardia presided over a trial in which an old woman was caught stealing bread to feed her grandchildren. Although LaGuardia wanted to show mercy, the shopkeeper demanded justice. LaGuardia found her guilty and fined her ten dollars, but immediately took ten dollars from his own wallet and paid the fine on her behalf. The judge, acknowledging the woman's guilt, paid the fine and released her.
This is a beautiful picture of mercy and justice, but it fits the Bible better if we change a small detail: if LaGuardia wasn’t just a judge, but a shopkeeper from whom the woman stole. When we sin, we sin against God. He must judge us, but then He pays the price for what we have done. When we remember the Christian perspective of Jesus: He is God, it all makes sense.
No one has seen God.
Many Muslims have asked me how Jesus could be God, if the Bible says, “No one has seen God” (1 John 4:12). It makes sense for Muslims to ask this question and interpret the Epistle of John in light of monotheism, a one-pointed view of God. But the disciple John, the man through whom God wrote this Bible verse, is also the author of the Gospel of John, and he explains it to us in John 1:18: “No one has ever seen God; the only Son, who is in the bosom of the Father, he has made him known.” In other words, when the Bible says, “No one has ever seen God,” it is referring to God the Father. Jesus, who is God and is with the Father, knew Him. That is why Jesus could say to his disciple Philip, “He who has seen me has seen the Father” (John 14:9). To see Jesus is to see God, the same as to see the Father. So although no one has seen God the Father, people have seen God the Son. This means that every time someone in the Bible saw God, they saw the second person of the Trinity, Jesus. This problematic verse is easy to understand when we remember that Jesus is the second person of the Trinity.
The greatness of a king
Sahar’s question to me that summer afternoon in Oakland intuitively captured a feeling that I think many Christians could learn from: God is the King of the universe, unimaginably holy, and His birth on this filthy earth is far below His greatness. So I acknowledged her question, but then I asked her one in turn. Sahar, let’s imagine you’re on your way to a very important ceremony, dressed in your best clothes. You’re arriving on time, but then you see your daughter drowning in a pool of mud. What would you do? Let her drown and come back with dignity, or save her but arrive at the ceremony covered in mud?” Her answer was very matter-of-fact, “Of course, I would jump in the mud and save her.” With more nuance, I asked her, “Let’s say there were others with you. Would you send someone else to save her, or would you save her yourself?” Considering this, Sahar replied, "If it's my daughter, how can I send someone else? They don't care about her like I do. I would definitely go myself."
I paused for a moment and continued, “If you, a human being, love your daughter so much that you would lay down your dignity to save her, how much more can we expect from God, if He is our loving Father, to lay down His greatness to save us?” He thought about this for a moment, and the conversation continued. When dinner was over, my friend brought me back to the Arabic program. During my last week in Auckland, as the program was coming to a fruitful and merciful end, I received another text message from my friend inviting me to dinner, this time to meet a new Christian from a Muslim background. When I arrived, I was met with a brilliant dawn! The message of God’s self-sacrificing love had overcome him, and he could no longer remain a Muslim. A few days after our dinner, he had accepted Jesus as his Lord and Savior. Now it was time to rejoice with Him, share stories about our amazing God, and chart the way forward in His discipleship.
Chapter 12
Libya's best friend

In early December 2013, David Wood and I happened to be in Texas when the state was hit by a massive ice storm. The scene was surreal. The normally warm, sunny landscape was bleak and barren, with cars backing out of the driveways because the driveways were covered in thick sheets of ice. The sky was a dull gray, and confusion pervaded the air as airports canceled flights and people scrambled to find a way out of the state. We had just left a restaurant where we had met a young Pakistani woman who had recently converted from Islam and was seeking counseling to reconcile with her family. Her struggles were unnerving, and my spirits were heavy as we drove through the darkness back to our hotel. But as I entered the hotel lobby, I saw the headline on the TV, and my heart ached even more: A young American teacher had just been shot dead in Libya. A local news station was reporting that Ronnie Smith, a Texas man, had gone out for a morning jog when gunmen in a black Jeep shot and killed him. The news said that Ronnie had graduated from the University of Texas and was teaching chemistry in Benghazi. That made me cringe. Benghazi was a very unstable, war-torn city where militias held more power than the government.
When I went online, my suspicions were confirmed. Ronnie Smith was a Christian who wanted to serve the people of Libya. He was a deacon at his church in Austin and took his faith seriously: Since Jesus was willing to die for those who had sinned against him, Ronnie believed that following Jesus meant being willing to serve those who were his enemies and even risk your life. The gift God had given him was to teach chemistry, so he decided to serve the Libyans as a teacher.
Fully aware of the danger, he and his wife moved their family to Libya immediately after the Libyan revolution, while the country was still in turmoil. They decided to stay even after the attack on the American consulate by a group called the "Servants of Sharia" in which the US ambassador, Christopher Stevens, and three others were killed.
The Smiths stayed because they valued the lives of their neighbors and students more than their own safety. One of those students, Youmna Zantani, told reporters how Ronnie had affected her and other students. "After everything that happened in Libya, we lost hope, and he was the only one who supported us, motivated us, and told us that as long as we studied, everything would be fine. He devoted a lot of time to all his students. He decided to come here and help us, risking his life."
Rooney's motivation
Even as I write this, tears well up in my eyes. I think of great men like Ronnie Smith and I have to ask myself, how did Ronnie get to be this way? How did this man become so loving and selfless that he was willing to risk even his own family to serve those who could never give him anything in return? Thank God, I need not be surprised. It turns out that Ronnie, in response to a survey a few years before his death, recommended a sermon by John Piper entitled “Missions Are Profitable When You Die.” “It was through sermons like this that God called my family and me to unknown peoples,” Ronnie said of the sermon.
In that sermon, Piper impresses upon his congregation that Jesus “died and suffered for people all over the world in every nation.” If they are to truly follow him, his followers are called to do the same. “The love of Christ must be personally presented through religious missionaries to the people for whom he died… presenting the sufferings of his cross to the nations through the sufferings of his people.” Christians must be prepared to suffer with Jesus because their Lord suffered on the cross and he intends for the whole world to be influenced by his exemplary Christians.
Quoting Hebrews 13:13, “Let us therefore go out to him outside the camp, bearing the reproach which he suffered.” Piper spends more than half of his sermon explaining that Christians called to follow Jesus must be prepared to suffer for the sake of the world. He tells stories of martyrs who followed Jesus in reaching the world with his love. As I read the sermon, one example caught my breath in light of Ronnie’s death: Piper spoke of Raymond Lull, a Christian who traveled to North Africa knowing he might be martyred for sharing the gospel with Muslims. Lull was stoned to death as soon as he began preaching in Algeria. “What a way to go!” Piper said to the subject.
This is the teaching that inspired Ronnie Smith and made him the great man who served the Libyans to the point of death: Jesus wants us to follow him in bringing his love to the world, even if it kills us. When we consider the message of the gospel, all the pieces fall into place. God didn’t just tell us to love selflessly. He is our Lord and our Exemplar, and he did it for us first.
The Creator of the world, God, surrounded by angels and worshipped in unimaginable glory, was willing to descend to this world and suffer for people so that they might be saved and know His love. If He is the example for Christians, then Christians should be willing to leave any comfort and go anywhere so that people might know the love of God and be saved.
God was willing to suffer for those who sinned against Him, those who used the hands He had given them to crucify Him. He not only told His followers, “Love your enemies and pray for those who persecute you” (Matthew 5:44), but He also exemplified it. If He is an example for Christians, then Christians should be willing to selflessly serve even their enemies, knowing full well that they may be killed for it.
The reason Christians can follow Jesus with boldness is that we are not concerned with trying to save ourselves. The gospel is that God has promised us salvation through his infinite mercy and grace. This is Christian teaching: Christians can follow Jesus and die for their enemies because the gospel promises and implements salvation and removes all fear of death. This frees us to love everyone, friend and enemy, and enables us to live according to the image in which we are made, the image of a God who is inherently loving. Combining this insight with what we have learned in the previous two parts of the book, the gospel calls us to live and love according to the image of the Triune God in whom we are made, and enables us to follow Jesus, the Lord and perfect example, even in the face of death.
This frees us to love everyone, friend and foe, and enables us to live according to the image in which we are made, the image of a God who is inherently love. Combining this insight with what we learned in the previous two parts of the book, the Gospel invites us to live and love according to the image of the Triune God in whom we are made, and enables us to follow Jesus, the Lord and perfect example, even in the face of death.
The Love of Christ: A Speaker to the World
Ronnie Smith knew all this well and left behind an example of a true Christian when he left this world. What was most sad, however, when I read his story, was that few in the media gave a clue as to what motivated Ronnie to be in Libya. Here was a vessel of Christian love, and few highlighted the source. But a few days later, the spotlight shifted to the lavish love of Christ.
A week after his murder, Ronnie's wife, Anita, wrote an open letter to the men who killed him. Its message was so transcendent and transcendent that it stunned the world. She didn't mince words.
To his attackers: I love you and I forgive you. How could I not? Because Jesus taught us to “love our enemies”—not to kill them or seek revenge. Jesus gave his life out of love for the very people who killed him, and for us today. His death and resurrection opened the door for us to walk in peace and forgiveness on the straight path to God. Because of what Jesus did, Ronnie is now with Jesus in heaven. Jesus came not only to take us to heaven when we die but to bring peace and healing to the earth. Ronnie loved you because God loves you. Ronnie loved you because God loved him, not because Ronnie was so great, but because God is so great.
Anita forgave the men who killed her husband. She forgave them because Jesus forgave us. She loves them because Jesus loves them. Read it again: She said she loved them! She loves the men who brutally killed her best friend, her husband of ten years, and left her infant son fatherless! How could she love those men? Shouldn’t she want to get even? Shouldn’t she want to hunt them down and take back what they took from her? It’s a forgiveness and a love that makes no sense and is unlike anything in this world. But as the media reaction shows, it’s love that captivates the world.
Jesus and Muhammad lived very different lives and were role models for their people in very different ways. The message of Islam is that Muhammad was the best man who ever walked the earth, but I think this world needs an example that transcends this earth. It needs a heavenly solution, something that makes no sense from a natural perspective and yet leaves all who witness it with a deep sense of awe that this is the answer. Ronnie's death was the voice of a martyr testifying to the love of Christ, and Anita's letter was her loudspeaker. That love can change the world.












Part 4
Quran or Bible?
Two different books








Chapter 13
Burning the Bible

On the first day of April 2011, twenty thousand demonstrators gathered outside the gates of a United Nations compound in Mazar-i-Sharif, a city long considered one of the safest in Afghanistan. The signs hastily carried by the crowd bore clichéd denunciations: “Death to America” and “Death to Obama.” They were given little time to prepare innovative slogans, for on the very day, as worshippers gathered for Friday prayers at local mosques, three imams struck a spark and ignited a flame in the crowd with one goal: bring the Americans to justice.
The people responded en masse, choosing the UN compound to express their discontent. The demonstration quickly turned into a protest, and the protest turned into a riot. The Afghan National Police failed to control the crowd and refused to allow the assistance of a NATO-led security force. The UN guards were tasked with defending the compound. As the crowd grew and their confidence in their numbers grew, some of the more zealous protesters began to riot and pelt the UN building with stones. The guards were helpless, and the situation quickly changed: the insurgents overran the guard towers and threw them to the ground. Taking weapons from the guards, they first began beating the guards, then shooting them. After killing four, they set the compound on fire and began hunting the foreigners.
The rebellion did not disperse until the Westerners were beheaded. Among the dead were two Scandinavians: the first female pilot in Norwegian military history, and a Swedish human rights researcher who had arrived in Afghanistan a month earlier. Additional casualties included four guards, all Nepalese Gurkhas; a Romanian political expert; and five Afghan insurgents.
The loss of twelve is tragic, especially when you consider that the UN is a humanitarian organization and those who join it often do so to help with foreign crises. But what makes this event more than just a tragedy is how absurd the violence was. The thugs demanded justice from the Americans, but instead slaughtered innocent Europeans and Asians. What happened? Why did they attack the UN?
A year ago, an anonymous Florida activist and pastor announced his plan to burn the Quran “for crimes against humanity” on the ninth anniversary of 9/11. He became an international pariah overnight. Many media outlets and the government urged him to reconsider, saying that burning the Quran could lead to American casualties abroad. The activist, under pressure from the White House, insisted that although he was not convinced, he had listened to their words and planned to talk to key imams. 9/11 came and went, and he did not burn the Quran. The media spotlight left him, the situation died down, and everyone went silent. But off-camera, the dialogue failed. Six months later, when virtually no one was watching, he pushed ahead with his plans. He held a mock trial, convicted the Quran of violence and oppression, and carried out the sentence by burning it. The video, like dozens of others showing the burning of the Quran, remains shrouded in mystery, except that the media broadcast it globally. Government officials, in turn, issued their own condemnations. As one news source reports, most Afghans were led by Hamid Karzai to condemn the burning. In response to the Afghan president’s announcement, the arsonist mullahs of Mazar-e-Sharif have escalated their violence and killings.
The sequence of events is so tragic that it is easy to overlook its absurdity. In response to the activist’s claim that Islam causes oppression and violence, Afghan imams called for his silence and hastened the killing of innocents. The media, which had been happy to condemn the activist’s dangerous behavior, gave him the spotlight, thereby hastening the deadly response from the East. The Mazar-i-Sharif rioters, who could not distinguish between Westerners and non-Westerners, took the lives of innocent Europeans. They were stereotypically killing Obama, the most pro-Islamic American president ever, who declared, “I consider it part of my responsibility as president of the United States to combat negative stereotypes of Islam wherever they appear.” Of course, even commenting on the Gurkhas’ connection to this atrocity makes us shake our heads. But let us now focus on the main issue: Throughout this series of events, no one doubted that burning the Quran would lead to riots and killings. It was a hard fact that no one even suggested otherwise. Not the media, not the government, not even Muslim leaders. Everyone knew that burning the Quran would lead to violence.
We should compare this to a similar event, both in time and proximity. In 2009, at Bagram Airfield in Afghanistan, the U.S. government announced that it had burned dozens of Bibles. A military official gave an enlightening reason: Bibles were trash, and the military burned trash.
This bears repeating. The United States government officially declared that the Bibles were garbage and burned them accordingly. I repeat this not to stir up emotion, but to provide a contrast: in this instance, there was no concern that any Christian would be subjected to a deadly crime because of the government burning the Bibles.
To further clarify the matter, let us consider how the Bible burning unfolded. The Christian soldier from whom the Bibles were confiscated did not complain publicly, and the church that sent them caused an outcry, even though the army should have just sent the Bibles to the church instead of burning them. This announcement was another attempt by the army to appease Afghan Muslims. Recently, a news outlet had broadcast a video showing soldiers at a Christian church service, and military officials feared that this would lead local Muslims to think that the army was there to evangelize. To quell their anger, the army, who suspected that there might be such anger, announced that they were burning the Bibles. They emphasized that they were so committed to not offending Muslims that instead of returning the Bibles, they would burn them as soon as the church tried to send them back.
Let us return to the main point: In all the government reports and decisions, no one believed that burning the Bible would lead to riots or murder. It was a hard fact that no one suggested otherwise. Not the soldiers, not the media, not the government, not even the church leaders. Everyone knew that burning the Bible would not lead to violence.


Difference between Quran and Bible
Why? Why is it so obvious that burning a Quran in Florida endangers lives halfway around the world, even if done by an obscure activist? Why is it so obvious that burning dozens of Bibles and calling them “trash” provokes no such reaction, even if done by a government?
I am not arguing that Afghan Muslims are illiterate and ill-tempered, easily provoked to thoughtless anger. Nor am I suggesting that Christians are so indifferent or so docile that they cannot be passionate about their holy book. Both are caricatures that miss valuable insights. I believe that the answer lies, at least in part, in the fact that the Quran holds a different place in the hearts and minds of Muslims than the Bible does in the hearts and minds of Christians. Both books are certainly sacred to their people, but their uses are different, their histories are different, and indeed their natures are different.










Chapter 14
Comparing the Quran and the Bible

When the Quran burning debate was over, my co-worker and I decided to make our official position on the matter public: We certainly supported free speech, but we concluded that burning the Quran was a poor idea. Aside from unnecessary provocation, we felt that a more effective use of free speech would be to discuss the teachings of the Quran rather than to burn its pages. But I had another reason not to support the practice: the place the Quran holds in the hearts of Muslims is beyond most Westerners’ estimation, and so is the crime of burning it. There is nothing on earth that Christians respect as much as Muslims do. This is not to say that Christians do not have great respect for the Bible, for they certainly do. But the traditional respect Muslims have for the Quran is almost incalculable. To understand this, we need to remember a point from the previous two chapters: for Muslims, the Quran is the eternal word of Allah himself. It is the closest thing to God incarnate.
For Christians, the eternal Word of Jehovah is Jesus. The Quran has the same place in Islam as Jesus has in the Christian faith. So let’s put it together: To understand the insult of burning the Quran, a Christian would have to imagine someone burning Jesus Christ. Burning the Bible is indeed insulting, but not nearly as insulting. Christians do not simply view the Bible as the eternal expression of the Triune God. Christians believe that God inspired men to write exactly what He wanted them to write at specific times in history. So Christians believe that the Bible is inspired by God but is not eternal. Once we begin to understand this fundamental difference, we begin to understand much more.
Since Muslims believe the Quran to be the eternal utterance of God, they do not believe that the Quran was written by men at all. It is the very word of God, written on a heavenly tablet, read from it by Gabriel and dictated to Muhammad. This is not inspiration in the Christian sense, but revelation: God revealed it to Muhammad piecemeal and dictated it through the angel Gabriel. Muhammad had nothing to do with shaping the text. He merely transmitted it. That is why, as a young American Muslim child who spoke Urdu at home with his Pakistani parents and English at school with his friends, I was taught that Arabic was literally the heavenly language, the best language. My mother was so insistent about teaching me to read Arabic that by the age of five, before I had learned English or Urdu, I had recited the entire Quran in Arabic. My mother was serious about the traditional Islamic view: if God’s eternal speech was written in Arabic, then Arabic must have been God’s chosen language.
This respect for the Arabic language underlies many Muslim practices. It is why Muslims recite prayers in Arabic, even if they have not yet learned the meaning of the words they recite. It is why Muslims hesitate to call non-Arabic versions of the Quran “translations,” because they believe there is a mystical value and hidden meaning in the Arabic language that cannot be translated. It is why some Muslims look for mathematical patterns in the Quran as evidence of divine authorship. It is also why Muslims are confident that the Quran is literary inimitable, because Allah Himself created it in a heavenly language.
Christians do not imagine that some deep mystical values have been lost, only interpretive concepts that can be explained through biblical study and interpretation.
By understanding the different natures of books for Muslims and Christians, we begin to understand the differences between the Quran and the Bible: the Islamic view of the Quran's immortality influences the place it holds in the hearts of Muslims, something that finds its Christian counterpart not in the Bible but in Jesus himself.
Composition of the Bible
Each religion’s view of the nature of its holy book is closely related to the date of its composition. Since the Quran is eternal, it was not composed at a specific time. Rather, it was revealed to mankind through Muhammad in parts over a period of twenty-three years. It is said that when someone asked Muhammad a question or encountered a problem, he would sweat and a recitation would come to him. The Syriac word for “recitation” is the Quran, and Muhammad delivered the Quran to his followers, usually in small packets of verses. Muslims then recite these fragments in their prayers and commit them to memory. All of these recitations were eventually collected into a book and organized into 114 surahs. A surah can contain verses that were revealed two decades apart. There may be several reasons for its current order, but given what is clearly discernible, the Quran can be described as having its longer chapters towards the beginning and its shorter chapters towards the end. In addition, Muslim scholars have attempted to distinguish which chapters of the Quran are revelations given to Muhammad during the early period of his prophethood, known as the Meccan verses, and which chapters are the last chapters of his prophethood, known as the Medinan verses.
In contrast, Christians have traditionally taught that God inspired specific men in history to write His words and used their experiences and language to convey His message. God masterfully used the words of chosen men to convey exactly what He wanted to say and has preserved that message through the millennia. The result is the Bible, a collection of sixty-six books written by about forty men over a period of fifteen hundred years.
The first thirty-nine books are called the Old Testament and the last twenty-seven are called the New Testament. Testament is almost synonymous with covenant and covenant, so the Old Testament was written during the time of God’s covenant with Moses, while the New Testament was written after God came to earth, with the advent of the new covenant initiated by Jesus and prophesied in Jeremiah 31:31-33.
Contents of the Bible
Since Muslims believe that the Quran was revealed through the medium of a single person, it makes sense that it has essentially only one genre and one point of view: Allah speaking to Muhammad. Although there are important exceptions, such as the first chapter, which is the speech of men, the Quran is more or less the same throughout its text.
On the other hand, since the Bible was written by many people over many years, it makes sense that there are many genres and perspectives. The Old Testament is often divided into three parts: the Law (Hebrew: Torah), the Prophets (Navi’im), and the Writings (Ketuvim). Jews refer to the text using an abbreviation of these three Hebrew words: Tanakh, which confirms the diversity of the Bible. For example, long sections of ceremonial law can be found in the Torah, complete books of history in the Prophets, and Hebrew poetry in the Writings. When we look at the New Testament, we see that it is divided into five historical books, twenty-one epistles, and one book of apocalyptic prophecy. Unlike the Quran, the Bible is very diverse in its literary genres and perspectives.
Seeing the New Testament as an appendix to the Tanakh may help Christians understand how Muslims view the Quran. It is the final revelation that confirms what came before it—in a sense, the “newest testament.” The Quran makes it clear that the Torah is indeed inspired scripture, as is the Gospel (5:68), but the Quran now comes as the final scripture for the guidance of mankind.
Oral or written methods of the Bible
It is also essential to note the difference between written and spoken texts. To understand this, consider the difference between writing a letter and having a transcript of a telephone conversation. Although the same person may express similar ideas, the way they write is very different from the way they speak, primarily because people have more time to consider what they need to say when writing and more time to consider what is being said when reading.
For these reasons, written communication is more complete and less repetitive than oral communication, with a set of accompanying features. Since Muslims teach that the Quran was revealed to Muhammad by Allah through the angel Gabriel, and that Muhammad then transmitted it orally to his scribes, it stands to reason that the Quran is primarily an oral text. Not only is it used more orally and recited aloud in prayer for people to hear and remember, but it is also read like a text of spoken communication. For example, instead of sharing complete stories, Allah usually says, “Remember the time when...” and begins in the middle of a story, assuming that the listener has already heard the story elsewhere. Otherwise, he starts at the beginning and stops in the middle of the story. This is why devout Muslims can often refer to the names of prophets, but not to complete life stories. Because of its oral nature, there are almost no complete stories in the Quran, with the exception of the story of Joseph. On the other hand, the Bible consists mainly of written texts. The stories and records are linear and complete. This is why devout Christians can share the birth-to-death narratives of not just Joseph, but dozens more: Jacob, Moses, David, Solomon, John the Baptist, and Jesus, among many others.
The manuscripts and final form of the Bible
An oral understanding of the Quran helps to understand the controversial phenomenon of abrogation. While Muhammad was alive, he made recitations that abrogated previous recitations. In other words, he told his followers that parts of the Quran that he had previously narrated were no longer to be recited as part of the Quran. This was met with resistance, as people asked him how the word of God could be abrogated.
