The answer is recorded in 2.106 of the Quran, which states that God can replace verses in His divine book because “He is over all things competent.” People accustomed to written texts may object to the concept of abrogation. How could God order the removal of parts of His eternal word? But the abrogation of the Quran makes more sense when we remember that the Quran was originally an oral text. People were not expected to tear pages out of their Qurans or cross out verses with a red pen. Rather, they were expected to simply stop reciting those passages in prayer and thus forget them. Although Muhammad’s revelations were considered inspired texts that he recited, long before the final form of the Quran, the books of the Bible were finalized before the church recognized them as scripture. At that point, there was no room for abrogation. Thus, the Bible, having a different method and a different history, was never abrogated.
The Sufficiency of the Bible
Although not all abrogated verses were removed from the Quranic text, Muslim scholars have classically taught that there are different types of abrogation, including abrogation of a verse without content and abrogation of content without a verse.
In other words, they teach that God willed that certain verses be recited but not acted upon, and that other verses be acted upon but not recited. For these reasons, Muslim scholars turn to the records of Muhammad’s life, the Hadith, for clarification. Furthermore, as we have seen, most Islamic practices actually originate from the Hadith, not the Quran.
This is why Muslims do not consider the Quran sufficient for Islamic practice, but rather require authentic hadith. Very few Muslims believe in the sufficiency of the Quran, and these “Quranic-only” Muslims are often considered heretical by mainstream Muslims. On the other hand, the Bible does not need supplementary texts to decipher its teachings or provide complementary Christian practices. It is sufficient and serves as the sole authority for Christian doctrine.
Bible interpretation
The abrogation of the Quran, its partiality, and its heavy reliance on hadith have traditionally been part of the reasons why the average Muslim does not engage in Quranic interpretation. What if a verse they are reading is abrogated and no longer applicable? What if the context of the verse is not clear, because the verses before or after it could be from completely different occasions? Furthermore, the Quran is in classical Arabic, a language that no one speaks natively anymore. For these and other reasons, Muslims generally defer to scholars and imams for the explanation of verses and texts. This is why the vast majority of Muslims themselves do not use the Quran directly for anything other than worship: memorization and recitation for prayer. The Bible, because of its written form, its sufficiency, and its translatability, is accessible enough for the average literate Christian to read and learn from directly. You do not need to be a scholar to interpret what the Bible says. Of course, it is helpful to know more context and the insights of scholars and theologians are valuable, but the nature of the biblical text allows devout Christians to go directly to it and learn from it.
The epistemological purpose of the Bible
Muslims certainly believe that the Quran serves primarily as a guide for humanity, but since they do not usually interpret it themselves, it is fair to classify this as an indirect purpose for the average Muslim. Rather, it is the mystical value of the Quran that is its primary purpose. This is illustrated in the average devout Muslim’s treatment of the Quran: reciting it in Arabic, even if the Muslim does not speak Arabic. In this case, Muslims are not seeking guidance, but blessing. So great is the trust of traditional Muslims in the mystical value of the Quran that it is the main reason for the authenticity of Islam. Muslims are certain that Islam is true because the Quran is so complete.
This epistemological use of the Quran is traditionally traced back to Muhammad himself, who pointed to the Quran as the basis for his claim to prophethood. “How could a man produce such a perfect book? It must be from God.” The primary use of the Quran, then, is to serve as a basis for why Muslims believe in Islam. On the other hand, as we have seen, the Bible is understandable to the average Christian who can access it in his own language and interpret it for himself. There are, of course, some who use the Bible for mystical purposes, but this is not its traditional or primary use. Similarly, there are some who believe in the message of Christianity because of the Bible, but this is not usually the reason people are Christians. Unlike the Quran, the primary use of the Bible is to serve as the basis for what Christians believe, not why they believe it.
And so, in discussing the differences between the Quran and the Bible, we have come full circle. The primary purpose of each scripture is related to its nature: the Quran is valuable in its mystical transcendence, while the Bible is valuable in its translatable and accessible guidance. There are many other ways in which the Quran and the Bible differ, but the ones we have just covered give us some insight into the common disagreements and misunderstandings between Muslims and Christians about the Bible.











Chapter 15
Questionable texts

At the end of the 20th century, one name dominated the conversation among Muslim Christians: Ahmed Deedat. Born in India in 1918, Deedat immigrated to South Africa as a young man and began his adult life as a furniture salesman. In that multicultural environment, Deedat encountered evangelicals who challenged him to think about the Bible, and in response, he began studying Islamic apologetics. By his mid-twenties, he was teaching apologetics and for the next forty years, he devoted his life to promoting Islam, publishing pamphlets and even founding a seminary. Despite the support of his friends and some significant financial donations, most of his early work was unsuccessful.
But everything changed for Deedat in the 1980s, when he began debating with prominent Christians such as Josh McDowell and Jimmy Swaggart. He presented challenges from a different Islamic perspective that were unusual for someone accustomed to debating skeptics and non-believers. The freshness of his arguments, coupled with his sharp rhetoric, often seemed to give him the upper hand over his opponents. His reputation grew almost overnight, and he was greeted with honors by the presidents of Muslim countries and rewarded for his services to Islam.
As Deedat's fame grew, so did his provocative rhetoric. In response to the Pope's positive stance on interfaith dialogue, Deedat challenged John Paul II to a debate. When the Pope refused, Deedat published a pamphlet entitled "His Holiness Is Secretly Working with Muslims." He also began to argue that the Bible was pornographic. Further provocative rhetoric during a Good Friday sermon in Sydney led to him being criticized by the Australian government and even banned from entering Singapore. Deedat suffered a massive stroke in 1996, at the height of his career. Among other neurological impairments, Deedat was no longer able to speak and was bedridden until his death in 2005. Some believed that God was punishing him for his remarks, and there were other rumors that Deedat had renounced Islam just before his death. However, in the Muslim world, both of these suggestions are vehemently denied. Deedat's fame continues to be legendary, even after his death. His oratorical style of argument has been taken up by the current figure of Islamic apology, Zakir Naik, who has a following of millions of Muslims around the world.
The technique that Nike uses, a technique that Deedat mastered and that many Muslims use today because of him, is to challenge the Bible while declaring the Quran to be infallible. Usually they see the Bible as full of contradictions, while they claim that the Quran has no contradictions.
The Bible and Contradictions
There is much to be said about this challenge. In principle, Christians agree that the Bible is inerrant and inspired, and will not contradict itself. But now that we have discussed the differences between the Quran and the Bible, it is perhaps clearer why this is a central element of Christian-Muslim dialogue.
First, the Quran is written in a uniform style, while the Bible is written in many genres. When someone accustomed to only one mode of speaking comes across texts like “God is love” in 1 John and “God hated Esau” in Psalms, they often forget to consider genres. From history to law to poetry and even an account of the love affair between Solomon and his bride, the Bible is a collection of very diverse books, and we should not read all books the same way. The genre of a book affects how we interpret it. For example, we should not read the book of Psalms like the book of Romans; the former is a book of poetry and should be read as a heartfelt sacred expression, while the latter is a treatise examining Christian doctrine and should be among the first foundations of a systematic theology.
Another example of poor interpretation is reading the Gospel of John in the same way as the Book of Revelation. Even though they were written by the same author, their genres are very different: the Gospel of John is historical and biographical, while the Book of Revelation is an apocalyptic book and is intended to be understood in prophetic terms. So, although we can be quite certain that Jesus literally walked in Galilee (e.g., John 1:43), reading about a dragon sweeping the stars in the same literal way is poor interpretation (Revelation 12:4)! Understanding genre is a fundamental part of biblical interpretation, yet Muslim apologists often raise the challenge of contradiction without regard to genre.
With this in mind, Christians often accuse the Quran of contradictions. For example, one website accuses the Quran of over 120 contradictions. Some examples: 6. 163 says Muhammad was the first believer, 7. 143 says Moses was the first, and 26. 40 says that the magicians were Pharaoh. 88.6 teaches that the only food in Hell will be thorns, while 69. 36 says it is pus, and 37. 66 says it is a devilish fruit. 7. 54, 10. 3, 11. 7, and 25.29 teach that Allah created the world in six days, while 41. 9-12 show a total of eight days. 2.29 says that Allah created the earth before the heavens and 79.30 says that He created the heavens before the earth. 96.2 says that Allah created man from blood, 25.54 says from water, 15.26 says from mud and 30.20 says from dust; 109.1-6 says that non-Muslims worship different gods, while 2.139 says that Jews and Christians worship the same God as Muslims. 4.48 says that Allah does not forgive idolatry and 4.153 shows Him forgiving idolatry. 4.78 says that all blessings and calamities are from Allah, while 4.79, the very next verse, says that blessings are from Allah and calamities are from those who suffer them. These are just a few of the hundreds of alleged contradictions in the Quran. Even though the contradictions of the Quran are far more devastating to Islam than the contradictions of the Bible are to Christianity, I find these arguments about contradictions futile and bordering on the utopian. I prefer to engage in constructive dialogue with any text that doubts and debates the merits of what they claim, rather than reading the texts in a hostile manner and accusing them of contradictions.
How do you deal with this verse?
Along the same lines of interpretation, Christians often approach their Muslim friends and ask them how they interpret certain verses of the Quran. For example, Muslims in the West often say that Islam is a religion of peace, which Christians ask how they deal with verses like 9:111, which says: “Allah has purchased from the believers their lives and their possessions in return for which they will have Paradise.” Muslims often respond at this point that they are not sure how to handle it, but this does not change their view of Islam as a religion of peace. Christians may be disappointed by the apparent meaning of a verse, but they should remember that Muslims generally do not interpret the Quran themselves. And by asking for an interpretation of a verse, they may be asking a Muslim to do something they have not done before. Muslims receive such guidance and interpretations from their Imams, and a Christian should not be surprised to hear the response, “Don’t ask me, I’m not a scholar!” What might be more helpful for a Christian who wants to point out such verses in the Quran to a Muslim friend is to find a scholar in that Muslim’s line of authority and see if that scholar’s interpretation says the same thing. If so, present the verse with the scholar’s judgment and continue the conversation from there.
The Bible has been corrupted.
Finally, we come to the most common Muslim accusation against the Bible: that its text has changed over time. It should also now be clear why Muslims accuse the Bible of being changed: because the Quran says that the Bible teaches the same thing that the Torah and the Gospel confirm, but the Bible’s teachings are clearly different. Along the same lines, the Quran teaches that Muhammad was prophesied in the Bible, but it seems that there was no such prophecy. The interpretation of what the Quran says about the Bible is a matter of debate. There are several passages in the Quran that Muslims use to teach that the Bible has changed, but closer examination seems to show that these verses are actually teaching that Christians and Jews abandoned their teachings because they were lying—not that the words of the Bible themselves have been corrupted. The general meaning of the Quran seems clear: Jews and Christians still had their holy texts at the time of Muhammad and could follow the straight path by reading them. However, Muslims usually accuse the Bible of significant changes. When making this criticism, a small minority of Muslims consider minor changes to the Bible to be significant in their view. To understand this, we must again look at it from an Islamic perspective. Muslims believe that because of the mystical nature of the Quran, the Quran must be preserved completely, letter by letter. But a changed letter is not a problem for Christians, because Christians are concerned with preserving the meaning of the Bible, and the same thing can be expressed in different ways in Greek without changing the meaning. For example, the words "Jesus loves Peter" can be written in sixteen different ways in Greek. The letters can be changed in sixteen different ways, without affecting the meaning.
However, what most Muslims think of when they say the Bible is corrupted is the removal or insertion of New Testament teachings, deliberate changes by the ruling powers. This kind of corruption of the Bible text simply has never happened and could never happen. For example, let’s consider a book of the Bible called 1 Peter. When the disciple Peter wrote this letter, he sent it to its recipients. They copied it and sent copies to churches in other cities. Those churches copied it and sent it to other churches. Now let’s imagine that the church to which Peter sent it had five copies made, and to each church they sent one copy to make five more. Even at this early stage of the biography, there are thirty-one copies. If someone wanted to effectively change the text, they would have to collect all thirty-one copies. But no one had the means to do that. No one had the authority to rule over the entire Christian world until the fourth century, three hundred years after Jesus. At that time, there were thousands of copies of the Bible, and even if someone had the authority over them, they did not have the practical ability to collect them all. Even if someone had the ability to collect all the texts and edit them, there would certainly have been a record of such a massive call. It is almost impossible to imagine any Christian calmly handing over the Bible to be changed without any sign of resistance or complaint. So the only way the Bible could have been corrupted on such a large scale is if someone in early Christian history had the authority and power to collect all the texts, destroy them, and issue official copies, thereby causing complaints and resistance. Yet such a person or record of events never existed. Interestingly, such a person and such a record exist in the early history of the Quran.




Chapter 16
The first burning of the Quran

Muhammad's third successor, Caliph Uthman, held authority over the entire Muslim community from approximately 644 to 655 AD. Earlier in his time, differing recitations of the Quran had caused discord among Muslims, and Uthman decided to do something before hostilities spread.
About twenty years after Muhammad’s death, the leader of the Islamic empire collected all the manuscripts of the Quran, destroyed them in a fire, and published an official, standardized version. When this happened, Muhammad’s faithful companions strongly resisted the collection of their texts, and the record of their opposition survives today. The Bible cannot therefore be changed because in early Christian history there was no central control over it, and thus it was never collected or edited. But certainly in early Muslim history there was such control over the Quran, and there was a precise time for its collection and editing. The history of Islam makes no attempt to hide the official burning of all the Qurans and the publication of an official, standardized version. Apart from ignorance or bigotry, I am not sure how anyone can continue to accuse the Bible of corruption when the Quran is under constant surveillance. In fact, the history of the Quran was one of the factors that kept me from accusing the Bible of corruption when I was a Muslim. Because of Uthman's control over the Quran, there is no basis for accusing the Bible of corruption on a large scale without condemning the Quran.
Bible Diversity
In addition to its more pristine textual history, the diversity of the Bible is a strength. It shows God’s love for diversity. There is no superior language, no superior people, no superior writing style. Rather, God intends for people to learn from the books of history, from the law, from poetry, from proverbs, from apocalyptic literature, and so on. He can teach through a fisherman like Peter, through a theologian like Paul, through a statesman like Moses, through a queen like Esther, and through his own incarnation. There is beauty and strength in diversity, and the Bible reflects this.
More than anything else, to accept the Quran, one must first accept Muhammad, because he is the only one who received Quranic verses from Gabriel. On the other hand, entire communities testified to the inspiration of the Bible, and they did not come from the mouth of one person.
The Bible speaks to the human heart.
As a Muslim, despite having ample evidence to trust the Bible and having learned about Uthman’s praise of the Quran, the ultimate turning point for me was when I asked God Himself to guide me. At the end of my rope, utterly distraught and tearful, I asked God, whether it was God or Jesus, to guide me through His Holy Book. I needed His comfort, and I was turning to Him. I began by opening the Quran. This was the first time I had opened the Quran for personal guidance, rather than reciting a memorized portion of the Quran or asking an imam for help. As I looked through its pages, I realized that there was not a single passage in it that would comfort me while I was in pain. While there were certainly verses that promised me that God would reward me for doing the right thing, there was nothing that said God loved me as I was or sought my comfort despite my failures.
On the other hand, the Bible was filled with God’s peace and love for me. God spoke to me through Matthew 5:4, which says, “Blessed are those who mourn, for they will be comforted.” There are no conditions here, no performance requirements. God gives comfort to those who mourn. Verse 6 reinforces this: “Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, for they will be filled.” Blessed are the righteous, but blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness. The Bible speaks to me of God’s love as if it were written for someone like me, someone who is suffering and who only seeks God.
He spoke to me. Not to Muhammad, not to seventh-century Arabic speakers, but to me. And that is the power of the Word of God – it goes through the ages and resonates with all of His people. There is no word like the Word of God.




Part 5
Jihad or Crusades?
Two different holy wars








Chapter 17
The First Crusade

In Clermont, France, on November 27, 1095, Pope Urban II issued the First Crusade. He urged Christians to travel from Western Europe to the Middle East, where Muslims had lived for more than 450 years, to fight the “infidels” and “barbarians,” “a cursed race utterly alien to God.” He encouraged not only knights but even mercenaries and robbers to “gain eternal reward” by joining the effort to “exterminate that vile race from the lands.” Many were moved by the pope’s call to arms, and Europeans soon left to carry the cross east to fight them. In the spring of 1096, an army of crusaders led by Count Emiko advanced through the Rhineland, where Jewish moneylenders were believed to hold storehouses of wealth. Emiko's army moved from city to city, slaughtering innocent Jews as "enemies of Christ" and plundering their wealth to finance the Crusade. His army killed thousands of Jews and forced others to convert or suffer a similar fate. As the Crusaders continued on their way to the Holy Land, their attacks on various cities delayed their arrival in Jerusalem until June 1099. After several days of siege, the Crusaders finally managed to overcome the city's defenses and make their attempt. A contemporary account, "The Acts of the Franks," describes the massacre from the perspective of a soldier. As soon as one of the knights scaled the ramparts, all the city's defenders quickly fled over the walls and through the city. Our men pursued and killed as far as the Temple of Solomon, and there was such slaughter that our men were ankle-deep in the blood of the enemy... Our pilgrims, entering the city, pursued and killed the Saracens as far as the Temple of Solomon. The Saracens gathered there and resisted fiercely all day, so that the whole temple flowed with their blood. Finally the pagans were defeated, and our men captured many men and women in the temple and killed them or kept them alive as they saw fit... Then the Crusaders dispersed through the city and seized gold and silver, horses and mules, and houses full of all kinds of goods. After that, our men went with joy and tears of joy to the tomb of our Savior Jesus, and there they paid their debt to him. Shortly after this bloody conquest, the Crusader lords wrote a letter to the Pope, describing their journey and culminating in a description of how the Muslims suffered at their hands. “If you want to know what they did to the enemy they found there, know that in the Porch of Solomon and in his temple our men rode in Saracen blood up to the knees of their horses.” History, and the intervening nine hundred years, do nothing to erase the vivid images of the First Crusade from the collective memory of modern Muslims.
American Muslims, the Crusades, and Jihad
I can’t speak for all Muslim Americans, but my family is very patriotic. My father served faithfully in the United States Navy for twenty-four years, starting as a seaman and retiring in 2000 as a lieutenant commander. It was he who taught me, while I visited his naval base in Groton, Connecticut, to put my hand over my heart during the singing of the national anthem. My mother, among other things, impressed upon me the importance of voting. On presidential election day, I learned that my parents were voting for opposing candidates. When I suggested to my mother that they both stay home that night because their votes would be nullified, she replied, “Son, it’s our duty to vote.” During those years, we often traveled to New York City for vacations, since it was two hours away from our home. My sister developed a deep love for the city. His eyes always widened as he gazed at the skyscrapers, and he never wanted to leave Manhattan. During his teenage years, the most prominent decoration in his room was a large, framed picture of the New York City skyline on his wall. It was still there on September 11, 2001, when our world came crashing down around us. I am convinced that the attack on the World Trade Center hit patriotic American Muslims harder than the average citizen. We, along with the rest of the nation, were oblivious to the deaths of our fellow Americans, but at the same time we were faced with an identity crisis. Terrorists had killed our fellow Americans in the name of our God. While we struggled to come to terms with these conflicting realities, most Americans had no ambivalence, for they could simply denounce Islam. We were forced to kind of stand up for our faith and at the same time stand up for our country, all this given the potential that people would demonize not only Islam, but us Muslims as well.
Fortunately, prominent Americans stood up for Muslims, not least former President Bill Clinton. In a speech in Washington, D.C., less than two months after the attack, Clinton said: “In the first Crusade, when the Christian soldiers took Jerusalem, they… killed every woman and child who was Muslim on the Temple Mount. Contemporaneous descriptions of this event describe soldiers walking up to their knees in blood on the Temple Mount, a holy site for Christians. I can tell you that story is still told in the Middle East today, and we are still paying the price for it.
President Clinton, always popular with our Muslim community, insisted to his audience that Christians had committed wanton violence in the name of their God. This sentiment resonated with Muslims more than Clinton had imagined, because it fit with the narrative we had inherited in the Muslim community: that Islam had spread peacefully after the advent of Muhammad, only to be opposed by violent Crusaders in the East and the Spanish Inquisition in the West. Christians were the aggressors and Muslims were the victims. Jihad, as we had been taught, was either spiritual or defensive.
With Clinton’s admission, others seemed to agree. Most Americans I knew looked back on the Crusades with a sense of shame. Georgetown University scholar John Esposito described the Crusades as the beginning of hostilities between Muslims and Christians: “Five centuries of peaceful coexistence preceded by political events and an imperial-papal power play that led to a series of centuries of so-called holy wars that pitted Christianity against Islam and left a lasting legacy of misunderstanding and distrust.” Ridley Scott, with a similar understanding, released a film in 2005 called Kingdom of Heaven, which depicted Christians as aggressors against civilized Muslims who simply wanted peaceful coexistence. At the time, it seemed to me that jihad had been vindicated even in the eyes of the West, and that no one could point a finger at Islam given the crimes of the Crusades.
However, I was convinced that no one should judge a religion by its followers. That is why, when I considered the truth of Christianity and Islam, I did not let the mujahideen – those who wage jihad – influence my view of Islam, and I did not let the crusaders influence my view of Christianity. In my mind, there were only two people whose behavior mattered: Muhammad and Jesus. I was convinced that neither the crusaders nor the mujahideen had anything to do with the core teachings of their religions. But as I came to realize, that was a step too far.
Crusades in the context of the text
Years after I left Islam, I expressed my dismay at Christians who named themselves after Christ but did not try to live by his principles when I spoke to colleagues. When I used the Crusades as an example of such hypocrisy, a good friend challenged me with a very simple question: “Nabil, have you ever researched the history of the Crusades?” I was embarrassed to admit that I had not. I simply assumed that everyone agreed that the Crusades were a terrible blot on the history of Christianity. Without overlooking the atrocities, my colleague suggested that I take a fresh look at the Crusades so that I could gain a full understanding. When I began to research, it was immediately clear that neither side had clean hands. In 1268, the Muslim sultan Baybars I, known as the Lion of Egypt, taunted the Christian ruler whose city had just been conquered in his absence. Baybars vividly describes what Count Bohemond VI would have done if he had been in Antioch:
Death... came from every side and every way among the besieged: we killed all those whom you appointed to guard the city or defend its entrances. If you trampled your knights under the hooves of horses, gave up your provinces to plunder, divided your wealth with all your might, gave the wives of your subjects to be sold in the market. If you saw the altars and crosses overturned, the pages of the Bible torn and thrown to the wind, and the tombs of your ancestors desecrated. If you saw your enemies, the Muslims, trampling the tent and burning the monks and priests and you alive in the sanctuary. In short, if you saw your palaces surrendered to the flames, the dead devoured by the fire of this world, the churches of St. Paul and St. Peter utterly destroyed, you would certainly cry out: By Derek, I wish I were dust!
According to the records, Baybars burned Antioch and emptied it of its inhabitants. Fourteen thousand Christians were killed and one hundred thousand were enslaved. This is a crime on par with the massacre of the First Crusade in Jerusalem.
I didn’t expect this, because the narrative I had always heard was that the Crusaders were the only ones who committed the atrocities, not the Muslims. It occurred to me that the only way to prove my inherited narrative was to make the massacre a belated reprisal for what the Crusaders had done in 1099, so I began to study the context of the battles, including the First Crusade. It was then that I realized that the narrative I had inherited was woefully inaccurate.
Just a few years before Pope Urban II called the First Crusade, the Seljuk Turks had conquered Nicaea, the same city where Constantine had convened the First Council of the Church 750 years earlier. The Seljuk Turks were Sunni Muslims and had taken Nicaea from the Byzantine emperor, who was a Christian. It was he, the Byzantine emperor, who asked Pope Urban II for help in defending his lands at the Council of Piacenza in 1095. In other words, the Muslims were actively attacking and conquering Christians, and the First Crusade was a defensive effort.
But what I later learned was even more shocking: The Seljuk army consisted of warriors called Mamluks, slave children who were eventually trained to become professional young warriors. These slave warriors were first used by Muslim caliphs in the ninth century and were ubiquitous in Islamic lands for a thousand years afterward. According to one scholar, sixteen of the seventeen major Muslim dynasties in history systematically used slave warriors. These slave boys were often captured from places like Egypt, where Christian lands had been conquered by Muslims. This meant that Muslim rulers captured Christian boys and turned them into slave warriors to fight against other Christians. Records show that Muslims had been conquering Egyptian Christians and taking their children from the very beginning of Islam. Amr ibn al-Aas, one of Muhammad's companions, savagely raided northern Egypt in 640 AD, just eight years after Muhammad's death.
John of Nicea, a bishop in the Nile Delta, records one of these conquests:
"[When] they had broken down the walls of the city with great difficulty and effort, they immediately took control of it and put thousands of its inhabitants and soldiers to the sword, and they took a great deal of booty, and they took the women and children captive, dividing them among themselves, and destroyed that city."
Reading the Chronicles of John, I realized that this was one of Amr's more generous conquests. He records the capture of Nacius, a city left defenseless when his soldiers fled the fleeing Muslim army.
John tells us how Amr treated the defenseless Christians: “Amr and the Muslim army... entered Nekeus and took it.
Finding no soldiers, they put to the sword all the men, women, and infants they found in the cemeteries and churches.
They showed no mercy to anyone. After conquering this city, they marched to other regions and plundered them, putting to the sword everything they found... Let us stop now, because it is impossible to recount the sins that the Muslims committed after the capture of the island of Naxios.
This was just one of many massacres recorded in the writings of Bishop Amr ibn al-As, a friend of Muhammad. History records similar Muslim raids on Christian lands from the mid-600s to 1095 and beyond. By the time the Byzantine emperor appealed to the Pope for help, two-thirds of Christendom had fallen to the Muslims.
That is why, says Crusades scholar Thomas Madden, “The Crusades were in every sense a defensive war. They were the West’s belated response to the Muslim conquest of two-thirds of Christendom.”
This becomes abundantly clear when we read the full text of Pope Urban II's appeal to European Christians for the First Crusade:
Your brothers who live in the East urgently need your help and you must hasten to help them. They have often been promised. For as most of you have heard, the Turks and Arabs have attacked them... and have overcome them in seven wars. They have killed and captured many and destroyed churches and devastated the empire. If you allow them to continue like this for a while without punishment, the faithful of God will be attacked by them much more. Therefore I, or rather God, beg you as heralds of Christ to publish this everywhere and persuade all people of every rank and degree, infantry and knight, poor and rich, to quickly help those Christians and destroy that vile race from the land of our friends.
The context is also clearer. The Pope ordered that the Jews not be massacred, and since some of the Crusaders were massacring Jews in the Rhineland, other Christians protested and did their best to protect them.
Archbishop Rothard of Mainz attempted to shelter the Jews in his personal residence until he was overpowered by Count Emiko. The bishops of Speyer and Worms likewise tried to protect the Jews as best they could. The crusaders who killed the Jews ignored their Christian leaders and brothers, and their actions should not be attributed to the whole of Christendom.
Revisiting the narrative of the Crusades
This is at least one reason why not all Christians in the First Crusade should be painted with the same brush. Historical understanding requires a more nuanced approach, and it becomes even clearer when we begin to analyze the Crusades from a different perspective. For example, we should not see the Crusades simply as “Christians versus Muslims” or vice versa. Christians and Muslims were too divided for such a unified view. Christians were not united among themselves, which is why it took hundreds of years for Western Christians to finally come to the aid of Byzantine Christians. Muslims were even less united, which is a major reason why the Crusaders were able to conquer Jerusalem in 1099. The Seljuk sultan had just died in 1092, and several family members were competing for power. The Fatimid dynasty, a Shiite caliphate based in Egypt, took advantage of this internal division to wrest Jerusalem from the Seljuk Empire in 1098, just a year before the Crusades. The Fatimids themselves had just emerged from a civil war and were still reeling from the assassination of the caliph and his grand vizier in 1094. All of these factors combined to make the fiercely divided Muslim lands vulnerable to attack. This is why, says Crusade scholar Jonathan Riley Smith, “Although none of the crusaders knew it, they were moving toward a door that had been wide open.” The medieval historian Christopher Tyreman tells us: “The appearance of the Western armies in the First Crusade in 1097-8 merely added another foreign military presence to a region already crowded with competing external laws. Among many, in fact, they were among the least relevant, having essentially been defeated when Saladin recaptured Jerusalem in 1187. Perhaps this is why Muslims more or less forgot about the Crusades by the end of the nineteenth century. According to Riley Smith, “Muslims looked upon the Crusades with indifference and complacency. In their eyes, they were the absolute victors… The first history of the Crusades in Arabic, which appeared in 1865, was a Christian history. Christians had to invent an Arabic word for Crusades, because Muslims apparently did not think of them until the early twentieth century. Given the historical facts, the common Muslim view of the Crusades—the view I have inherited—is a view. The modern invention of the narrative of an aggressive crusade against peaceful Muslims, along with the themes of Ridley Scott’s “Kingdom of Heaven” and John Esposito’s “Five Centuries of Peaceful Coexistence,” seems to have fanciful aspects based on motives other than history. The fact is that the Crusades were launched to defend the Byzantine Empire after two-thirds of the Christian world had been conquered by centuries of Muslim attacks. Muslims understood this and held no grudge against the Crusaders until modern times, when post-colonial narratives became prevalent.
Learning from Jihad and the Crusades
Given the facts, did the Crusaders commit unjustifiable crimes? Absolutely. Did the Mujahideen commit unjustifiable crimes? Absolutely. I am in no way trying to excuse those who committed these crimes. However, I do not think that a religion should be judged by some of its followers. The actions of some Christians do not necessarily say anything about Christianity, and the actions of some Muslims do not necessarily say anything about Islam. As I concluded when I was still a Muslim, what matters is the teachings of Jesus and the teachings of Muhammad. But to say that neither the Crusaders nor the Mujahideen had anything to do with their religions is a step too far. As it turns out, both Jesus and Muhammad had a lot to say about violence, and the Crusaders and the Mujahideen listened.





Chapter 18
Comparing the traditions of the founders

In 2009, David Wood and I received invitations to the annual conference of the Islamic Association of North America, themed “Life, Liberty, and the Pursuit of Happiness,” held over the Fourth of July weekend in Washington, D.C. We registered to attend, eager to hear the messages and mingle with the Muslim attendees. We saw lectures on the history of the Quran and the status of women in Islam, met with respected Muslim scholars, and even interviewed one of the conference organizers. Although we thought there was a lot of misinformation, there is no doubt that the organizers and speakers were very hospitable and sincere about their messages. Many of my childhood Muslim friends and acquaintances were at the conference, and I made some awkward attempts to reconnect with them. Two of these friends, both former Ahmadiyya converts to Sunni Islam, agreed to join David and me for dinner after the conference. Talking about halal Chinese food, the four of us discussed a wide range of topics, but what sticks in my mind is our conversation about apostasy. I asked one of my friends, “What do you think about the punishment for leaving Islam?” I was curious, because my friend, a former Ahmadi, came from the most peaceful sect of Islam, while the punishment for apostasy among Sunni Muslims is a violent death. “There’s no question about it,” he began. “The Prophet (peace be upon him) made it abundantly clear that apostates should be killed for leaving Islam.” His serious tone showed no sign of ambivalence. “I agree that he did, but never mind! You really can’t think that people should be killed for their beliefs? I mean, it’s the 21st century. People believe in freedom of worship.” My friend didn’t budge. “Leaving other religions is fine, but people should be killed for leaving Islam. Yes, here in America there is freedom of worship. Since it is illegal to kill apostates, American Muslims are bound by the laws of the land and cannot kill former Muslims. But in Muslim countries, apostates can and should be killed. I couldn’t believe my friend. I had to challenge him a little more to see how serious he was. “So if we were in a Muslim country right now, would you kill me?” Indeed, without malice or sarcasm, he replied, “Yes, I would kill you right now. That is the order of the Prophet.” A silence reigned at the table as everyone waited for my answer. “Well then, thank God we are not in a Muslim country! May God protect America!” I laughed. The obvious attempt at humor moved the conversation forward, but his words stuck in my mind.
Peaceful Muslims, apostasy, and the example of Muhammad
The contrast between my friend’s views and the summit’s theme was stark. All weekend the summit had been trying to prove that Islam was compatible with American values and Western freedoms, but here was a passionate young Muslim who said that American law was the only thing that would stop him from killing me for my beliefs. How are we to understand the discrepancy? My friend did what Muslims have traditionally done: he looked to Muhammad for Sharia law. The hadiths are full of references to Muhammad killing apostates and ordering their killing. So, for a Muslim who follows Muhammad as his role model, there is little question: apostates must be killed. The evidence is so strong that all major schools of Islam, Sunni and Shia, teach the law of apostasy, and disagree only on the details and conditions. Of course, killing people for their beliefs is an assault on Western moral sensibilities, including the sensibilities of many Muslims in the West. So in adapting their understanding of Islam to Western moral concepts, they often argue that Islam could never teach such a thing. Unfortunately, quite the opposite is true: Islam has always been like this. From a historical perspective, the denial of the punishment for apostasy is a modern phenomenon, as is the insistence on a largely peaceful Islam. Some Muslims point out that the Quran does not command the killing of apostates, and they are certainly correct. But Islam is not a biblical faith. It has always used the hadith to supplement the Quran. Anyone who prays the five daily prayers must confess this, because not only are the words for the prayers not mentioned in the Quran, but even the number of daily prayers is not mentioned. The Quran mentions only three daily prayers and does not specify the words or modes for any of them. Although there are legal “Quranic-only” Muslims, they have always been an extreme minority in Islam. Most Muslims who argue against the law of apostasy using a purely Quranic approach use an argument that, if consistently applied, would radically alter their Islamic practice. In this vein, some peace-loving Muslims argue against the law of apostasy by citing Quranic verses such as 2.256, which states: “There is no compulsion in religion.” However, in saying this, they also deny the example of Muhammad. Muhammad did not interpret that verse in that way, as his practice in the hadith repeatedly shows. This is why classically, Muslim theologians have listed 256.2 among the abrogated verses we discussed in the previous chapter. For example, Ibn Kathir says that 256.2 was abrogated with 29.9, which tells Muslims to fight Jews and Christians. Prominent Muslim scholars have vigorously defended these traditional interpretations throughout the twentieth century.
Of course, I hope that peaceful Muslims will gain a majority in the global Muslim community and change the Islamic practice in their favor. Most Muslims who advocate a peaceful practice of Islam, far from being insincere, truly believe that Islam is a religion of peace. In my mosque, that was exactly what we were taught and repeated to others. But to follow peaceful Islam, one must ignore or reject a vast portion of the traditions of Muhammad’s life, as well as almost the entire history of Islamic jurisprudence. My friend in DC used to be part of the same peaceful mosque my family attended and was taught by the same peaceful imams. Eventually, he left that version of Islam and embraced a more violent version in the name of following Muhammad’s true teachings. Muslims who consistently follow the record of Muhammad’s life believe in an Islam that does not meet Western notions of peace. These are the majority of Muslims throughout history.
Muhammad and Jihad
The Economist magazine published an article entitled “Continuing History” on August 22, 2015. The first image in the article is horrifying: dozens of burqa-clad Nigerian teenage girls abducted from their school in Chibok by Boko Haram. The article then goes on to discuss the thousands of Yazidi women captured by ISIS, the Islamic State. In Dabiq, ISIS’s professional magazine, ISIS argues that these women are war booty and that sexual slavery is a practice sanctioned by the Quran. Does Islam permit sexual slavery? We must do what my friend in Washington did, and Muslims have always done, to answer questions like these: turn to the hadith. When we look at the life of Muhammad, we find numerous narrations of Muhammad not only condoning sexual slavery but also encouraging Muslims who were hesitant to use their newly captured women for sex, whether they were pregnant or because their husbands were still alive, according to the hadith. The narrations state that the reason 4.24 of the Quran was revealed was so that men would not hesitate to have sex with captured women whose husbands were still alive. These narrations of Muhammad’s life influenced Muslims throughout the classical era of Islam and shaped their laws of war. These are the narrations that ISIS now refers to. Of course, many Muslims disagree with ISIS’s actions, but their reasons are illuminating. An open letter sent to ISIS by 140 Muslim scholars states: “After a century of Muslim consensus on the prohibition of slavery, you have violated this. You have chosen women as concubines.” They argue that Muhammad was in favor of freeing slaves, which may be very true, but they do not go so far as to say that Muhammad did not condone sexual slavery. That is not what the record shows, and ISIS does. Unfortunately for these 140 Muslim scholars and for the rest of the world, ISIS is interested not in the consensus of Muslim scholars a hundred years ago, but in the example of Muhammad in the seventh century. ISIS is taking not only women but also boys. The world was recently horrified by reports that captured boys were forced to strip and were subject to execution if they had pubic hair. But again, this was based on Muhammad’s treatment of the Qurayzah Jews. Muhammad had ordered that children were not to be killed in battle except in night raids. So when a group of people, such as the Qurayza Jews, were taken captive, the boys were separated from the men when they had pubic hair, and the men were executed. ISIS simply took this narrative from the life of Muhammad and applied it to them. Of course, this Muhammadan contribution also explains why the Seljuk Turks and almost all other Muslim dynasties had no qualms about capturing boys and enslaving them to become warriors. Muhammad himself enslaved Jewish boys, perhaps an act of mercy given the fact that their fathers were all beheaded that day. So jihad, in its classical practice and in those manifestations that are considered “Islamic” or “radical” today, is often an attempt to simply follow Muhammad. Those who argue for more peaceful practices of Islam must do one of three things: deny the example of Muhammad’s life altogether, as Muslims who accept only the Quran do. Declare Muhammad's teachings extinct, as 140 Muslim scholars have done; or reject the parts of Muhammad's life that have been recorded in history, as the average Muslim would.
Jesus and the Crusades
Following Jesus leads to a very different understanding of war. Jesus never led an army. Far from it, he never even endorsed violence. When it comes to Jesus’ most explicit command on war, his message is unmistakable: “Put up your sword, for all who draw the sword will perish by the sword” (Matthew 26:52). Even in matters of self-defense, his teaching is so utterly peaceful that it seems to come from another world: “But I tell you, do not resist an evil person. But whoever slaps you on your right cheek, turn to him the other also. And whoever wants to sue you and take your coat, let him have your cloak as well. And whoever forces you to go one mile, go with him two miles” (Matthew 5:39-41). This is in line with the eschatological way in which Jesus told his followers to treat their enemies: “But I say to you, love your enemies and pray for those who persecute you” (Matthew 5:44). Jesus’ teachings were so peaceful that they posed a problem for early Christians, who saw themselves as obligated to defend the oppressed with violence. Since Jesus clearly did not authorize war, such Christians developed an elaborate concept of “just war,” beginning with Augustine in the early fifth century. By describing the harsh conditions of war, Augustine argued that war could be within the will of God but was still a necessary evil and something that required repentance. When the Crusades began, Christians relied on Augustine’s arguments and other similar views to justify their defense of Byzantium, initially treating their wars as a necessary evil to combat a greater evil. However, it was during the Crusades that holy war was viewed positively, as a means of forgiveness. In this, they went far beyond Augustine. The idea gained popularity, as one contemporary historian wrote: “God has brought about holy wars in our time, so that the order of knights and the multitudes that follow them… might find a new way to obtain salvation.” Thus, until the Crusades, more than a thousand years after Jesus Christ, Christians saw holy war as a positive endeavor that actually forgave the sins of the crusaders, rather than a sin that required repentance.
Comparing Muhammad and Jesus on Violence
In contrast, it was Muhammad himself who taught that fighting in jihad was holy and good. According to the hadith, Muhammad taught Muslims that attacking the Christian city of Constantinople would absolve the fighters of their sins: “The first army of my followers to attack the city of Caesar will have their sins forgiven.” Jihad was so good in Muhammad’s eyes that there is nothing like it in this world. When a man asked him if there was any act in this world as good as jihad, Muhammad replied: “I know of no such act.” Timeline: Jesus’s outspoken words against violence led to more than three hundred years of non-resistance among Christians, and then Augustine’s arguments led to six hundred years of war as a justifiable evil. Until Christians were a thousand years removed from Jesus, they believed that holy war could absolve sin, while Muhammad himself taught Muslims that fighting in jihad could absolve sin, and was, in fact, the best thing in the world. There is much more that could be said about jihad and the Crusades, but one thing is certain: Jesus never endorsed violence and absolutely did not endorse anything like the Crusades, while Muhammad engaged in jihad as the greatest thing a Muslim could do. Violent jihad is the result of a strict adherence to the life and teachings of Muhammad, while strict adherence to the life and teachings of Jesus leads to pacifism and selfless love for enemies.












Chapter 19
Questioning Christian peace

A week after David Wood and I went to the ISNA National Conference in 2009, we traveled to London to engage Muslims in public debate and discussion. Of the twenty or so public debates I have attended, my favorite was the one David and I conducted together, where we discussed two former Christians who had converted to Islam. The topic was “Is Islam a religion of peace?” And in addition to the interesting topic, the two-on-two nature of the debate kept the interaction fast-paced and engaging. One audience member commented after the debate that it was “more exciting than football,” and I agree. During the debate, it became clear that the two Muslim debaters were arguing, to some extent, that Islam is peaceful when juxtaposed with a particular view of Christian doctrine. Focusing on Jesus’ words, such as: “Do not think that I have come to bring peace on earth. I have not come to bring peace, but a sword” (Matthew 10:34), they argued, and so has Christianity, and so has Islam. If Christianity is peaceful, even though violence is permitted, then Islam can be peaceful. To be fair, Augustine and Christians after him did justify war to some extent by asking questions about verses like this. But my answer to Augustine is the same as the one we gave to Muslim debaters: “What is Jesus really saying in this verse? We need to read the context.” In the context, it is indisputably clear that Jesus is not talking about war, but about division within families: “Do not think that I came to bring peace on earth. I did not come to bring peace, but a sword. For I came to set a man at variance with his father, and a daughter against her mother, and a daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law. “A man’s enemies will be those of his own household.” (verses 34-36). This is not about war. This is a common misunderstanding I encounter among Muslims I speak to: they challenge Jesus’ peaceful teachings by taking his words out of context. As we saw in Part Four, this makes sense given the Quran’s interpretation and its relative lack of emphasis on context. But in biblical interpretation, the context of a passage is a crucial element in determining its meaning. When Jesus says he has come to bring a sword, he tells us exactly what he means: his coming will divide families. A closer study of Jesus’ words shows that the “sword” Jesus refers to is not a romafaya, a type of sword used only for war. The sword Jesus brings with him is a machair. Like a knife, a machaira is a long knife or a short sword designed as a multipurpose tool, such as cutting meat or gutting fish. Also like a ghacho, a machaira can be used for fighting, but that is not its only or primary purpose. Its primary purpose is to cause division, and here Jesus says that he came as a machaira to divide families. This explanation helps us understand another commonly misunderstood passage. In Luke 22:35-38, Jesus tells his disciples to take machaira swords with them on their journey. Ancient Christians who advocated just war asked the same question that many modern Muslims ask today: “Why would Jesus tell his disciples to bring swords or at least to defend themselves?” Now that we know what a machaira is, the answer is simple: He was telling them to be prepared for a long journey and to take the right tools with them. The context is useful again: Jesus gives them a list of travel accessories (money belt, bag, and sandals) and a sword appears on that list. Jesus told his disciples to make it clear that two swords would be enough. If he were envisioning a battle, two swords would never be enough among that number. But if they are considered travel tools, they are abundant. Another verse that can be confusing if overlooked is Luke 19:27, where Jesus says, “But those enemies of mine who did not want me to reign over them bring here and slay them in front of me.” Reading the entire text clarifies the statement. Jesus is telling a parable and sharing a teaching about a hypothetical king. He does not actually want his enemies brought before him and slain. Throughout Luke’s Gospel, Jesus tells many parables, including those about a wicked judge who ignores a woman (Luke 18), a farmer who sows seed (Luke 8), a vineyard owner who orders a tree to be cut down (Luke 13), and a woman who searches for a lost coin (Luke 15). These parables do not mean that Jesus himself is a wicked judge who ignores women, a farmer who sows seed, a vineyard owner who orders the trees to be cut down, or a woman who searches for a coin. Similarly, his parable in Luke 19:27 does not mean that he is a king who wants to kill people. Instead, Jesus uses stories to present memorable images, and his parable in Luke 19:27 predicts the outcome for those who reject God on the final day of judgment. The final issue to consider in relation to these verses is a fundamental hermeneutical principle: always interpret unclear verses in light of clear verses. Jesus’ clear words and teachings continue to be “love your enemies” and “lay down your sword.” We never see Jesus carrying a sword in the Gospels, and the only physical conflict in the Gospels that causes Jesus to rebuke his disciple for fighting. This, in addition to the context of the verses in question, helps the reader see the consistency of these verses with Jesus’ peaceful teachings.
Violence in the Old Testament
Another common objection from the Islamic perspective, one that two debaters raised in the very first minute of our public debate in England, concerns violence in the Old Testament. Although there are many ways to formulate this objection, its basic point is that God commands violence in the Bible, therefore Christianity is not peaceful. As we saw in Part 4, this objection fails because Christians are under a new covenant, not the covenant made with Moses and the Hebrews. The commands God gave to the Jews are not the commands given to Christians. However, there are related and more subtle objections that deserve attention. God commands violence in the Old Testament. For this reason, as a Christian, I believe that God can command violence. As a result, I cannot object to Islam solely on the basis of its use of violence. At the same time, the nature of violence in the Old Testament versus Islam is significantly different. What we see in the Old Testament is a judgment that God has ordained at a very specific time and place, a judgment that is delayed for four hundred years. In Genesis 15:13-21, God promises Abraham that his descendants will inherit the land of Canaan. But Abraham himself will not receive this land because “the iniquity of the Amorites is not yet complete” (Genesis 15:16). God foretells violent battles between the Hebrews and the Canaanites, but the Canaanites are given four hundred years to repent. This is the kind of violence we see in the Old Testament: judgment directed at a specific people for specific sins and delayed as long as possible. On the other hand, the violence that Muhammad teaches extends to all non-Muslims in Islamic lands unless they meet certain conditions. According to the Quran, polytheists have three options: convert to Islam, leave the land, or be killed (e.g., Quran 9.3-11). These options are not limited to a place or person, but apply to all who do not believe in Islam in lands claimed by Muslims. Looking back at the text, 9.5 says: “Kill the polytheists wherever you find them, and seize them, besiege them, and lie in wait for them in every ambush. But if they repent and establish prayer and pay the poor-due, then open the way for them.” In other words, the Quran commands Muslims to kill people for being polytheists, but if they become Muslims, then let them go. The next verse offers another option of exile. Jews and Christians are allowed another option in the Quran: to pay a tribute tax upon surrender (e.g., Quran 9.29). In this case, it is more obvious that the violence was based on their beliefs. The Quran literally says: “Fight those of the People of the Book who do not believe in God and the Last Day... and do not follow the religion of truth [i.e., Jews and Christians].” In other words, fight Jews and Christians who do not accept Islam and do not believe in God. The next verse makes the point clear: “And the Jews say, ‘Uzair is the son of God,’ and the Christians say, ‘The Messiah is the son of God... May God destroy them.” Their punishment is deserved because of their beliefs. This was the verse that justified the offensive against the Romans in the disastrous Battle of Tabuk, even though the Romans had never attacked Muslims. Given these teachings, it is not surprising that the life of Muhammad in Sahih Muslim records that he said, “I will drive out the Jews and the Christians from the Arabian Peninsula and leave none but Muslims.” Before moving on from this point, it is worth noting that the chronology of these verses promoting violence against Jews, Christians, and polytheists is all found in Surah 9 of the Quran, and according to Islamic sources, this is the last Surah of the Quran to be revealed to Muhammad. As it were, it is the most violent teaching found within its pages. This is especially important given the traditional Islamic concept of abrogation, which teaches that earlier verses can be abrogated by later ones.
On the other hand, violence in the Old Testament was part of God’s campaign to make his sovereignty known early in Jewish history. It is nowhere near the final message of the Bible. For Christians, the final commandments are the Great Commission: to spread the good news of God’s love and mercy throughout the world by increasing the number of disciples of Jesus. While the culmination of the Quranic teaching is the most violent chapter of the Quran, the culmination of the Bible’s teachings is grace, love, mercy, and sacrifice. When we look closely at Old Testament violence, we see that it has very little bearing on Christian practice. This conclusion reinforces our basic claim: to justify violence in Christianity, one must divert attention from Jesus. Strict adherence to Jesus’ teachings simply does not allow for any basis for violence. Conversely, one must divert attention from Muhammad to argue that Islam is a religion of peace, because he says that a Muslim who does not fight in jihad or at least does not express a desire to fight is a hypocrite.
Chapter 20
Jesus against Jihad

Before leaving London in July 2009, David and I stopped at Speakers Corner in Hyde Park. For a few hours every Sunday, Britons gather in the park for a rousing display of free speech, climbing ladders to deliver their messages to a throng of listeners. From politics to protests to religious preaching, speakers never lack for subject matter or passion at Speakers Corner, and hundreds of people gather to listen. While many are there simply to shake the speakers or watch the spectacle, some of the audience is genuinely interested in the conversation. David and I, each over six feet tall, abandoned the ladders and proclaimed the gospel where we stood. Almost immediately, we were surrounded by Muslims who, in return, loudly challenged our views. It was harder to answer them over the noise of the crowd than I had anticipated, and after about thirty minutes of conversation and recitation, my voice was too hoarse to continue. I gave up my effort and joined the various discussions that were taking place around us. I saw two passionate sympathizers and decided to join in their conversation. A young Mennonite woman was discussing women’s rights with a Muslim man. When the young woman pointed out 4:34 of the Quran, which allows men to beat their disobedient wives, he replied that 1 Corinthians 14:34 went further in denying women’s rights. Up to this point the dialogue had been fruitful and I had simply been observing the discussion, but what happened next has stuck in my mind for years. The young woman opened the Bible she was holding and attempted to clarify the content of 1 Corinthians 14. As she was turning the pages, the man forcefully squeezed her hand and said, “I don’t trust your Bible. Don’t bother me!” Without thinking, I grabbed her by the collar and pushed her back, saying, “Don’t you dare touch it!” She was stunned, a genuine look of shock on her face. “Nabil, you’re a Christian! I thought Christians were pacifists!” She said, still a little hot under the collar, I replied, “I’m not a pacifist, and if you touch her again, you’ll really find out!” Perhaps she realized her mistake, or perhaps she wanted to avoid letting him know how unpacifist I was, but she apologized to the young woman and they continued their conversation. Years later, I can’t remember her stunned expression. He was absolutely convinced that Christians should be pacifists, and as I consider Jesus’ words more carefully, I have to wonder whether he was right. My actions in Hyde Park that day, in a very small sense, show that defending victims with violence is a natural reflex. But throughout the Gospels, Jesus teaches us to abandon our natural inclinations for his eschatological teachings. Is it possible that, even when it comes to defending victims, Jesus does not want Christians to fight? I’m not sure, to be honest. I find such a teaching hard to believe, but if I were pressed on this point, I think I might have to stick to Jesus’ words in the Bible and forgo violence, even defensive violence, altogether. Although this is a dilemma for me as a Christian, caught between pacifism and defending the oppressed, I think it’s a very good place to be in theology. This means that Christians who want to engage in defensive violence must do so while recognizing it as a moral abomination. No follower of Jesus’ teachings can see violence as inherently good, even defensive. If Jesus had approved of any kind of violence, that would have been very different. Such assistance would have always opened the door to those prone to bloodshed.
This is what we see throughout Islamic history – with ISIS today and other Islamists inevitable in the future. Even Muslims who believe Islam is a religion of peace generally acknowledge that Muhammad permitted war under certain circumstances, and it is Muhammad’s life that Islamists appeal to to justify their terrorism. If Muhammad had commanded or participated in eighty-six wars over nine years after gaining an army, such activity would have been inevitable among his followers. In contrast, it is striking that Jesus Christ, the model for Christians, is never once reported to have carried a sword. This leaves his followers somewhere between an apocalyptic trust in God in pacifism and a reluctant use of violence in defense of the oppressed. There is absolutely no room for joy in violence for followers of Jesus.





Second question
Can we know that?
Is Islam or Christianity true?










A decade of experience as a Christian, contrasted with my first twenty-two years as a Muslim, leaves me with no other conclusion: Christianity is very different from Islam. The message it preaches is pure grace, the God it proclaims is objective love, its founder on earth is none other than the incarnate God, its scriptures are communal and diverse, and it leaves no room for joy in violence. Islam, as traditionally understood, is remarkably different at every stage. Virtually no one with a conservative and devout upbringing in Islam would be under the illusion that Christianity is Islam, especially one with as much to lose as Fatima. After growing up as a Muslim and defending her religion passionately, she became disillusioned with Islam and left it. It was with a full knowledge of the teachings of Islam that she found the message of Jesus compelling. So Fatima not only realized that the two were different, but the fact that she was willing to embrace Christianity after leaving Islam is proof that differences make a difference and are worth the risk. I also traveled from Islam to Christianity, but my path was not the same as Fatima’s. I was not initially disillusioned with Islam and did not see the beauty of the Gospel. As convincing as I now find the Christian message, as a Muslim I did not find it convincing at all. I believed that God was loving, Muhammad was peaceful, the Quran was beautiful, monotheism was the perfect doctrine, and true adherence to the Sharia makes one righteous before God. I felt no need for the Gospel and certainly did not consider it a superior message. But despite my preference for Islam, I had met many people from many religious backgrounds, and there was an undeniable trend: Christians preferred Christianity, Jews preferred Judaism, Hindus preferred Hinduism, and so on for people of all backgrounds. Was that why I preferred Islam as a Muslim? Realizing that people are more comfortable with their own worldview, I didn’t want to base my faith on Islam simply because it was compelling to me. Instead, I was a confident Muslim because I believed Islam was the truth.
Pursuit of truth
As a young, passionate Muslim, I proclaimed the teachings of Islam to anyone who would listen. In my mind, there was not the slightest shred of potential for Christianity to be true, so I regularly engaged in dialogue, pitting the claims of Islam against those of Christianity. In this, Christianity and Islam differ from many other religions. While worldviews often offer subjective reasons for belief, both Christianity and Islam have claims about the past that can be tested against the record of history. When it comes to the person of Jesus, their truth claims are contradictory and both cannot be true. For example, a fundamental Christian doctrine is that Jesus was crucified in the first century (Mark 15:37; Matthew 27:50; Luke 23:46; John 19:33; Acts 10:39; 1 Corinthians 15:3). In contrast, the Quran teaches the exact opposite: Jesus was neither killed nor crucified (4:157). Neither religion treats these accounts of Jesus as a myth, so we cannot resolve these conflicting positions in a metaphorical sense. He was either crucified or he did not die. Islam or Christianity must be wrong. Fully convinced that Islam was correct on all these counts, I was more than ready to challenge Christianity’s claims to truth in order to invite people to Islam. This was the reason I began researching ways to convert people, especially my friend David. However, David challenged me to apply the same criteria to the investigation of Christianity and Islam. I agreed, confident in the truth of Islam, and tried to study the religions objectively as much as I could. Over the course of four years, examining the claims of Islam and Christianity, I went from a firm believer in Islam to a reluctant acceptance of the Bible.
The evidence in favor of Christianity was so strong that I had no choice. What I discovered along the way is what I will share in the next five parts of this book.
The case of Christianity
When David and I first started discussing our beliefs, it started somewhat by accident. Just a few weeks after we met, while we were in Sorghum for a college event, we ended up rooming together. As the evening drew to a close, David pulled his Bible out of his backpack and began reading. As I had been trained as a young Muslim to oppose Christianity, I challenged him, accusing the New Testament of textual corruption. The meeting opened the door to a wide range of conversations, from the inspiration of the Bible to inerrancy to the divinity of Jesus and the Trinity, and much more. It flowed organically from one topic to the next, covering topics that had arisen over the course of our friendship. There was certainly value in discussing these topics in the context of our real lives, but after a year of such discussions, I realized that my knowledge of Christianity was more superficial than I had initially thought. It wasn’t like I realized I was wrong. Rather, I realized that I could not defeat David’s arguments. To be more precise, I wanted to explore these issues more systematically. It was then that we tried to distill Christianity and Islam to their core. At the very least, what must be true for the Christian message to be true? Conversely, what must be true for the Islamic message to be true? We wanted to eliminate all the problems and distractions, and these questions allowed us to skip all the less relevant issues and sectarian differences. There are many branches of both religions that have different teachings, but neither David nor I were interested in specific sectarian teachings or peripheral issues. What defines Christianity at its core, and what defines Islam? For Christianity, we find the answer in Romans 10:9: “If you confess with your mouth, ‘Jesus is Lord,’ and believe in your heart that God raised him from the dead, you will be saved.” Here we find the entire message of the gospel, formulated as the minimum requirement for saving faith. It has three components: (1) that Jesus died, (2) that he rose from the dead, and (3) that he is God. Fortunately, each of these three components can be tested from a historical perspective. Did Jesus die on the cross or not? As we have seen, this is a fundamental teaching of Christianity that happened in history, so we can open the pages of our historical records to see if they support or deny Jesus’ death. His resurrection is the same. Although this would be a supernatural event, it still happened in history, so we can examine the historical records to see if they support the claim that Jesus rose from the dead. Do they confirm or not? Finally, does the historical record show that Jesus made a claim to be God? If all three are true, then we have good reason to accept the Christian message, because together they make a compelling case. To illustrate, we need to remember that many people claim to have supernatural powers and to be divine. Usually such claims are pathological delusions of grandeur, and we are more than justified in dismissing them as madness. But if someone can demonstrate supernatural authority to support their claim, we have good reason to believe them. In other words, if someone says, “I am God,” we think they are crazy. But if they say, “I will prove my claim by rising from the dead,” and then they actually rise from the dead, we have good reason to believe them. This is exactly what Jesus said the resurrection was for. When people who Those who doubted Jesus’ claims asked him for a sign, and he said that the only sign he would give them was his resurrection. So, if history testifies that Jesus claimed to be God and rose from the dead, we have good reason to accept the Christian message. A very important point to note about the core of the Christian faith is that Islam rejects all three components. The Quran explicitly denies that Jesus ever claimed to be God (5. 116), and it also explicitly denies that he died by crucifixion (4. 157), thus implicitly denying his resurrection from the dead. To understand my journey, and to understand the fundamental incompatibility between Islam and Christianity, it is absolutely vital to internalize this point: the core claims of Christianity are explicitly rejected by Islam. Islamic teaching contradicts the core message of Christianity. Proving the claims of Christianity negates Islam, and vice versa.
As we have already examined, there are many points of agreement between Islam and Christianity, even about the life of Jesus, such as his virgin birth and his ability to perform miracles. But none of those points of agreement are the central proclamation of the gospel. The Bible does not say, “If you believe in the virgin birth, you will be saved.” It says we must believe that Jesus is God, that he died, and that he rose from the dead. Islam categorically rejects these ideas.
The case of Islam
When David and I thought about how to distill Islam to its essence, the answer came clearly in the testimony: “There is no god but Allah and Muhammad is the messenger of Allah.” At the very least, to become a Muslim, one must believe that Allah is God and that Muhammad is His appointed messenger. But is Allah, the God of Islam, the only God? And is Muhammad His true messenger? To examine these, one must turn to the Quran and the record of Muhammad’s life. The Quran, itself the revelation of God and the “proof” of Muslim belief, must be examined carefully. Similarly, Muhammad, as the only direct recipient of the Quran and present in the testimony, must be critically examined to determine whether he is truly the messenger of God. If we can determine that the Quran is the word of God, or if we can determine that Muhammad is the messenger of God, we have a good reason to accept Islam. Unlike the Christian case, where all the elements must be true to make a case, defending Muhammad’s prophethood confirms the inspiration of the Quran, and vice versa. So, conceptually, the issue of Islam should be easier, because only one point needs to be well defended: either the prophethood of Muhammad or the inspiration of the Quran. So, after careful consideration, David and I decided to study these five points:
1. Jesus' death by crucifixion
2. The resurrection of Jesus from the dead
3. Jesus' claim to be God
4. The Prophetic Status of Muhammad
5. The Divine Inspiration of the Quran
Together, these five points constitute the epitome of Christianity and Islam.
The Inspiration of the Bible and the Case for Christianity
The divine inspiration of the Bible is missing from this list, perhaps glaringly. Although David and I had examined the Bible, and its inspiration was crucial to Christian doctrine, we both realized that it constituted the “necessity” of the Christian faith, not the “reason” for it. To focus on the bare necessities of Christianity, we had to set aside many topics that were very important but not central, and the inspiration of the Bible was one of them. Theoretically, even if the Bible had never been written, Jesus could still have died on the cross for our sins and risen from the dead, making the message of Christianity real. The inspiration of the Bible is not central to Christianity. Unlike the Bible, the Quran is the “reason” for Muslim belief. When skeptics challenged Muhammad to provide evidence for his claims, his primary argument was the inspiration of the Quran. He repeatedly, at least five times, cited the divine origin of the Quran as a reason for people to trust Islam. In contrast, when the early Christians proclaimed the gospel, the main reason they pointed to was the resurrection of Jesus, not the text of the Bible. For these reasons, the Quran forms a central pillar in the case of Islam, while Jesus forms the main pillar in the case of Christianity. This is consistent with what we have already learned about Islam and Christianity: the Quran and Jesus are similar in these two religions, not the Quran and the Bible. However, let us be careful not to confuse divine inspiration and historical authenticity. We will examine what the various books of the Bible say about the death, resurrection, and divinity of Jesus, so we must examine whether their records on these matters are historically reliable. But of course, assessing the accuracy of specific claims found in the books of the Bible is very different from determining whether the entire Bible is the inspired word of God. No early Christian ever argued the latter as a defense of the Gospel, so we will not consider that argument here, although we will consider the historical validity of the Bible insofar as it affects our case for Christianity.
