In contrast, because it is the primary argument presented by Muhammad and early Muslims, we will consider the inspiration of the Quran.
Objective: Defense of faith versus evaluation of faith
Before I get into this research, I want to share something that took me years to truly understand: It is nearly impossible to study these issues objectively. Not only do we all have a strong belief in the religions we have believed in and our social circles are interested in, but our beliefs also color the way we perceive information. The same data is interpreted differently by people with different worldviews. When we examine Islam and Christianity as believers, as believers in one faith or the other, our Christian or Muslim presuppositions influence how we interpret the evidence, and we often see what we want to see. When I began examining the data, I came to the table with the assumption that Islam was true and interpreted the data based on that. No matter what facts David presented, I either made them fit my Islamic paradigm or found a way to refute them. Defending what you already believe is not difficult, and anyone who sets their mind to it can do it, whether Muslim, Christian, or whatever. What is difficult is pursuing the truth about the faith and evaluating it honestly. This feat requires that one be introspective and self-critical at frequent intervals. While we can never completely overcome our biases, the most important step we can take is to intentionally pursue fairness. As we examine the data, we should repeatedly ask ourselves, “Would an objective observer find the arguments convincing?” In the next five sections of this book, I will raise this question repeatedly as I evaluate the cases of Christianity and Islam. After examining the first three points, which bear on the case of Islam and Christianity, there will be a midway summary to consolidate the findings to that point before examining the case of Islam. While still a Muslim, I decided that the best question to begin my investigation was one that was a point of contention, but one that was easily examined historically and left the least room for subjectivity: Did Jesus die on the cross?







Part 6
Did Jesus die on the cross?










Chapter 21
Positive case
Unanimous records

In 2002, David and I organized a friendly interfaith dialogue with my father and two of David’s friends, Mike Licona and Gary Habermas. Mike was a martial arts instructor and insurance salesman who had begun graduate school in religion, and Gary was one of his professors. Gary had participated in many interfaith dialogues, Mike regularly hosted people of different religious backgrounds in his home, and my father was always eager to preach Islam, so everyone felt comfortable coming together and discussing deep beliefs. Part of the reason I felt comfortable, however, was that I had chosen a topic that we had heard Muslim leaders and scholars discuss dozens of times: Did Jesus die by crucifixion? In addition to our relative familiarity with the subject, the Quran took a bold stance on the subject, and I had confidence in the word of God. The Quran explicitly denies Jesus’ death by crucifixion. In verse 4.157 it says: “And their saying: We killed the Messiah, Jesus, the son of Mary, the messenger of God, and they killed him not, nor crucified him, but the matter was made doubtful to them, and those who differ about him are in doubt about it, and they have no knowledge of it except conjecture, and they killed him not.” Muslims interpret this verse in various ways. The view that my father and I defended that day was the view supported by Muslim debaters such as Ahmed Deedat and Shabbir al-Mutawfiq: Jesus did not actually die on the cross, but appeared to be dying. Although this is the position of prominent Muslim intellectuals, it is a minority among Muslims generally. Most Muslims, going back to the earliest interpretations of Islam, believe that the face of Jesus was placed on another person who was crucified in his place. My father presented our perspective for the first part of the night, and Mike and Gary then engaged with us, explaining their reasons for believing that Jesus actually died on the cross.
Scientific consensus
The most striking point of the evening for me, and perhaps most important to a mind accustomed to thinking in terms of authority, is that virtually no non-Muslim scholar agrees with the Islamic position. For all intents and purposes, there is a unanimous view in academia that Jesus died by crucifixion. While scholarly consensus is not proof in itself, it was a hard-hitting survey. Of course, I was not at all surprised that Christian scholars agreed on this point. What surprised me was the insistence of non-Christian scholars that this was a point so solid that it was beyond dispute. Gerd Ludmann is a German scholar who was so skeptical of the Bible that he famously said, “The person of Jesus himself becomes insufficient as a basis for faith.”
Yet even he did not mince words about the death of Jesus. In his book, What Really Happened to Jesus, Ludmann critically examines the life of Jesus from various angles, often completely ignoring the traditional Christian position. But in his section entitled “The Death of Jesus,” he omits only two sentences: “The fact that Jesus died by crucifixion, despite the hypotheses of a sham or a sham death that are sometimes put forward, is undeniable. There is no need to discuss it further here.” He then continues, as if it were pointless to dwell on the subject. Paula Fredriksen, another renowned scholar who frequently challenges Christian beliefs, concludes similarly to Ludmann, saying: “The most solid fact about the life of Jesus is his death: he was executed by Pilate, the Roman prefect, on or around Passover. In the way Rome had reserved for political rebels, namely, crucifixion. This point was succinctly put by perhaps one of the most provocative critics of traditional Christianity, John Dominic Crossan, who says, “There is not the slightest doubt that Jesus was crucified under Pontius Pilate.” He says elsewhere, “That he was crucified is as certain as anything historical.” As a graduate student in historical studies for the past decade, I have learned that scholars disagree on almost every historical point they discuss, and that it is almost impossible to find a consensus of scholars, yet almost all New Testament scholars today agree that Jesus died on the cross. Even those scholars who have no hesitation in rejecting traditional Christian beliefs, as we have seen, say that Jesus’ death on the cross is “irrefutable,” “the most solid fact about the life of Jesus,” and “as certain as anything historical.” I am not arguing here that Jesus must have died by crucifixion because almost all non-Muslim scholars believe that. That would be an appeal to authority, a flawed logical argument. I am simply recounting what Mike and Gary pointed out to me that night, which made me think about how much of a struggle we would have if we thought Jesus did not die on the cross. The evidence for Jesus’ death by crucifixion is so strong that almost every scholar who studies Jesus’ life believes it. In fact, the evidence is so strong that at least one Muslim scholar agrees with it. In the flurry of media attention that followed the publication of his book Zeal, Reza Aslan made it abundantly clear that Jesus “was definitely crucified.” Aslan is one of the most well-known Muslim scholars in the West, and given the historical evidence, he also believes that Jesus died on the cross, despite what the Quran teaches. He believes so much in the crucifixion of Jesus that he uses it as the basis for his entire theory of the life of Jesus. What evidence is there that would convince all these scholars - Christian, non-Christian, and even Muslim?
Cross Records
As I was taught by my professors at Oxford, the foundation of any good historical argument must always be primary sources: what do the historical documents themselves show? By all standards of ancient history, the accounts of Jesus are very early and very diverse. Only a few years after Jesus was crucified, Christians, Jews, and Romans report that Jesus died by crucifixion. This testimony has been unanimous for over a hundred years. Perhaps the earliest accounts are found in a letter written to the church in Corinth. According to many New Testament scholars, both Christian and non-Christian, 1 Corinthians 15 contains a doctrine that was formulated within five years of Jesus’ crucifixion, and it testifies that Jesus died and was buried: “For I delivered to you as of first importance what I also received: that Christ died for our sins according to the Scriptures, 4and that he was buried, and that he was raised on the third day according to the Scriptures.” (1 Corinthians 15:3-4) It is important to understand the significance of this data: before the New Testament was even written, Christians were passing on the basic doctrines of their faith to one another, and the death of Jesus was one of their first concerns. This teaching was not only present in the earliest days of Christianity, but was also a central part of their doctrine. That we have a record of such a tradition coming within five years of Jesus’ death is almost unprecedented in ancient history. Compared to most historical records, the speed It is lightning fast. Another such formulation of faith, which many scholars believe predates the New Testament, is found in the letter to the Philippians. Here again, the death of Jesus is not only present but also highlighted: “[Christ Jesus] humbled himself and became obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross” (Philippians 2:8).
The earliest biographers of Jesus’ life all testify that Jesus died by crucifixion: Mark, Matthew, Luke, and John. Moreover, many other New Testament writers and books agree. Without carefully considering the implications, some might argue that these sources are from the Bible, and therefore biased and should not serve as evidence. There are at least two problems with this view. First, all historical sources are biased, and although we should weigh biases when examining them, the mere presence of bias is not sufficient reason to discredit the accounts. If that were the case, we would have to discard all history, indeed all news reports and personal stories of friends. Second, these sources were written by people from a variety of backgrounds, and by and large, they did not begin life as Christians. Although originally non-Christians, they found the Christian message compelling enough to convert, often at great expense. So, in any case, their testimony may be given more credence. Regardless, the testimony of the early Christians is corroborated by non-Christian accounts: Josephus, a failed Jewish general who befriended the Roman emperor, also reports in the first century that Jesus died by crucifixion. He is joined shortly thereafter by Tacitus, a Roman historian who also reports Jesus’ death. In the first hundred years after Jesus, we have Christian, Jewish, and Roman accounts that Jesus died by crucifixion, and not a single account that he may not have died by crucifixion. This last fact becomes even more compelling when we consider the account that some people tried to explain why Jesus’ tomb was empty. Instead of Jesus not dying on the cross, they argued that his body had been stolen. So, even though there was a perfect opportunity to argue that Jesus did not die by crucifixion, it seems that no one thought of it. And there is a good reason for this: all people were familiar with the horror of the cross.
The horror of the cross
Simply put, the cross was one of the most vicious, agonizing, and effective methods of execution that human depravity has ever devised. The torture of the cross was so severe that a word was invented to describe it: agonizing, which translates from Latin as the pain of "the cross." The ancient Roman orator Cicero described crucifixion as "the most cruel and abominable punishment" and "the worst extreme torture." According to him, even the thought of the "horror of the cross" was too terrifying for Roman citizens: "The word 'cross' should not only be far from the character of a Roman citizen, but also from his thoughts, eyes, and ears." Seneca the Younger wrote this paragraph describing the despair of the crucified: "Can one be found who would rather perish in pain, or give up his life drop by drop, than die once and for all? Can one be found who would want to be tied to a cursed tree, long ill, already disfigured, swollen with ugly tumors on his chest and shoulders, and breathe life in the midst of long agony? I think he had many excuses for death even before he mounted the cross!" Crucifixion was an execution that Rome reserved for when they wanted to make a statement. There was no standard procedure for crucifixion, as executioners were often given permission to express their own brutality. Victims were sometimes fixed to the cross in uncomfortable positions, sometimes nailed to their groin, sometimes forced to watch their wives being brutalized, sometimes witnessing the massacre of their own families, and sometimes having their own murdered sons hanged around their necks. Crucifixion was not just another means of execution, as there were far more efficient ways to kill. The cross was intended to be brutal, and victims were not treated gently. Crucifixion often followed scourging, as in the case of Jesus, which was itself a horrific torture. The scourging was designed to tear through the skin and turn the muscles to pulp, causing the victim’s blood to “flow out of the veins.” Josephus tells us that victims were “whipped to the bone” and their intestines were sometimes exposed to whipping. This is why Seneca describes a victim as “a beaten and inert corpse” when placed on the cross. The ultimate end of crucifixion was execution, and it was easy to tell whether a victim of the cross was alive or dead: simply observe whether they were still moving. If they weren’t, they were dead, because that meant they weren’t breathing. Because of the way the victims of crucifixion were hung, their chests were completely expanded and their lungs couldn’t create the pressure needed to exhale. To get their breath out, they had to push against the nails in their feet and could only breathe as they sank. When they reached the point of exhaustion or blood loss, their bodies would sink, no longer able to breathe. They could not breathe and died of suffocation. That is why one way to hasten or ensure the death of victims was to break their knees, as is said to have happened to the robbers on either side of Jesus. Once their knees were broken, they could no longer breathe and would soon die. The Romans, however, had other means of ensuring death. Among other methods, they were known to set people on fire, feed them to wild animals, or, in the case of Jesus, pierce their hearts. This is a very concise and concise description of the horror of the cross. It was an execution that Rome considered for the most worthless or heinous of criminals. Perhaps it is now understandable why in ancient Rome the word cross was used as a curse of rank among the lower classes: “Crucify!” It is not surprising that no one in recorded history has survived a full-scale Roman crucifixion. However, what must be said is that It is surprising that a religious movement could be started with the ridiculous announcement that their Savior was crucified.
The ignorance of the cross
Given the incomparable shame of crucifixion, Christians promoted their message by saying that their Savior had died on the cross. If Jesus died such a horrible death, how could He save anyone? That is why people ridiculed Christians for this belief. The earliest known depiction of Jesus on the cross is a painting designed to mock a Christian named Alexamnus. It depicts Jesus on the cross as the head of a marcasite, and the inscription reads: “Alexamnus worships his god!” Crucifixion was a shameful thing to do. To worship such a person, even more so. This is the focus of an anti-Christian argument written by Minucius Felix, who says: “To say that their rites are centered on a man who was murdered for his own crime, and on the deadly wood of the cross, is to say that for these forsaken wretches, sanctuaries are appointed. It is fitting for them and their kind of worship.” In other words, if Christians are so depraved as to worship the Crucifix, they deserve to worship a Crucifix. Not only the Romans, but also the Jews saw the cross as a great disgrace. Deuteronomy 21:23 teaches that “Cursed is he who is hanged on a tree (for the death penalty).” In short, the Jews did not expect a suffering Messiah, let alone one who was cursed by God. That is why the Corinthian church had to make sure that despite all appearances, the cross of Jesus was still the gospel: “For the message of the cross is foolishness to those who are perishing, but to us who are being saved it is the power of God” (1 Corinthians 1:18). When we recognize the common understanding of the cross, it makes perfect sense why it would seem to be “foolishness.” Without a doubt, the message “Christ crucified is to Jews a stumbling block and to Gentiles foolishness” (1 Corinthians 1:23). To Jews and Gentiles alike, the message of a crucified Savior was hateful and ridiculous. In other words, anyone who heard the message of the cross was repelled by it, at least initially. It certainly was not an attractive proclamation. Given that the cross would elicit such ridicule and disgust, the question we must consider is why would Christians preach such a message? Why not preach an alternative, more appealing message, such as that Jesus survived the cross, or that, despite appearances, Jesus never went to the cross in the first place? Better yet, why not leave the cross out of Christian preaching altogether and teach that he died by some other means, or perhaps that he didn’t die at all, but rose directly to heaven? All of this would make the Christian message more appealing to anyone who would hear it. There is only one possible answer: Jesus did indeed die by crucifixion, and the disciples were preaching what they wanted to proclaim as truth.
Summarizing the positive
An entire book could be devoted to the death of Jesus, as indeed dozens have done, but we may summarize the positive case before examining the Islamic response. The basis of any historical case must be primary sources, and in this case, the sources are unanimous, diverse, primary, and abundant: Jesus died by crucifixion. Beginning almost immediately after Jesus’ death, more than a dozen writers and traditions recorded Jesus’ death by crucifixion, including Christian, Jewish, and Roman sources, and their testimony was unanimous. For over a hundred years, no account even suggests that Jesus survived death on the cross or circumvented his execution. This fits well with what we know about the methods of crucifixion, since no person in recorded history has ever survived a full-scale Roman crucifixion.




Chapter 22
Islamic answer
Thus it became suspicious.

After hearing Gary and Mike’s position, it was clear that my father and I would be in for a tough battle on this issue. We argued, regardless of the positions we had been taught in the mosque, and had much to say. Here I will recount the bulk of what we argued, as well as present the more common Muslim arguments against the death of Jesus. Islamic responses to the above argument can be categorized according to two main interpretations. 4. 157 Recall the central part of this verse: “They did not kill him, nor did they crucify him, but it was made doubtful to them.” The two common interpretations place relative emphasis on different parts of this verse. The view that Muslim debaters often hold, that Jesus survived the cross, focuses on the first part—“they did not kill him”—while the majority view focuses on the second part: “but it was made doubtful to them.”
They didn't kill him.
My father and I argued that Jesus was crucified but not killed. Jesus miraculously survived the cross, was taken down alive, was placed in a tomb for healing, and then was rescued from the Romans. This is not the same theory that Christian apologists call the Soven theory, a theory that was destroyed hundreds of years ago by the critical atheist scholar David Strauss. A naturalistic view, the Soven theory argued that Jesus somehow survived the crucifixion. Strauss’s criticism was powerful: Even if Jesus had somehow survived the crucifixion, his body was broken and mutilated, and he was in desperate need of medical care. The disciples may have been relieved that he survived, but this is no explanation for the beginnings or preaching of the early church. According to the historical account, Jesus’ disciples preached that the Lord of life had risen in glory, and this belief led them to deny death to the point of being more willing to die. According to Strauss, this would not have happened if Jesus had just survived the cross. Christian apologists who responded to the Suven theory focused on a different, more internal point: If Jesus had somehow survived the cross, his legs would still have been broken, his arms crippled, his sides pierced, his body mutilated, and otherwise incapacitated. They argued that even if Jesus had come out of the tomb, it would have been a miracle! But as Muslims, we realized that this required a miracle, and that is what we argued. This was what I called the Suven theory, and we argued that God miraculously preserved Jesus’ life on the cross. We presented this argument in a heartfelt package: “If God could perform the great miracle of raising Jesus from the dead, why couldn’t He perform a lesser miracle to keep him from dying in the first place?” In effect, this circumvented the inevitability of Jesus’ death. No matter how brutal the cross, God’s ability to keep Jesus alive is greater. We also argued that subtle traces of this divine plan still exist in the Gospels: Pilate did not want to kill Jesus because of the sleep God had given his wife. His attempt to release Jesus failed when the crowd demanded Barabbas. Pilate may have resorted to collusion with the executioner to ensure that Jesus’ body was neither killed nor hung on the cross for the usual length of time. Joseph of Arimathea may also have conspired with Pilate, as evidenced by his request for Jesus’ body. The women who came to the tomb must have brought aloes and myrrh for medicinal purposes. Jesus disguised himself as a gardener to escape the guards. The holes in Jesus’ hands showed that he had not been raised from the dead, etc. We also argued that Jesus himself did not want to die, as shown by his prayer in the Garden of Gethsemane. Jesus was so concerned about the cross that he sweat drops of blood. We argued that the best explanation for this was that he was begging God to save him from the cross. We linked this argument to a verse from Hebrews that says, “In the days of his flesh, when he had offered up prayers and supplications, with loud cries and tears to him who was able to save him from death, and he was heard because he believed” (Hebrews 5:7). God, the merciful God who protects his prophets, heard Jesus’ prayers and miraculously saved him from death on the cross. Given that God is more than able to save Jesus, and that the biblical record still contains evidence in favor of this theory, we argued that Jesus did not die by crucifixion.
But it was made to appear.
However, the more common position among Muslims regarding the death of Jesus is often called the substitution theory. This is the belief that Jesus' face was superimposed on another person. Often, Muslims argue that it was either Simon of Cyrene, who literally switched places with Jesus as he carried the cross to the place of crucifixion, or Judas Iscariot, who deserved punishment for betraying his prophet. This is a position that cannot be easily defended with historical arguments, but it is often supplemented with such arguments, primarily criticisms of the biblical narrative. For example, a common accusation is that the Gospels disagree about what happened when the women went to the tomb on the third day. Mark's Gospel depicts a young man dressed in white, Matthew tells of an angel who rolled back the stone, Luke describes two men who appeared with the women, and John says that two angels were sitting where Jesus was. The differences between the Gospels indicate confusion in each of their accounts. The writers did not know what really happened. Those Muslims who argue for the horrific Soven theory also use these points to support their claim, and the Quranic phrase "it appeared" also applies to their theory, but in a lesser sense. God performed the miracle of preserving Jesus' life, and in the end it appeared that Jesus was dead, when he was not. In the substitution theory, the words "it appeared" have a much more active meaning. The very miracle that God performs is that Jesus appears to be dead, when he is really dead.
Conclusion of the Muslim response
Ultimately, Muslims respond that, despite its appearance, God saved Jesus from the cross. He may have done so by miraculously preserving Jesus' body, as the Gospels skillfully recount, or he may have done so by replacing Jesus with someone else. In general, the biblical record is unreliable, and so there is no good reason to trust its narrative.
Chapter 23
Assessing the Islamic response
The Quran and the Historical Jesus

Both major Islamic theories about Jesus' death on the cross deserve careful consideration. Let us examine each one closely, taking into account the context of some of the Quran's teachings about Jesus, while delving deeper into the majority belief in the theory of succession.
Response to Suwon's theory
Before properly answering the argument, it is worth noting what the Quranic verse actually says: “They did not kill him, nor did they crucify him.” Ostensibly, this verse says that he was never even nailed to a cross, but we and other Muslims who argued for the theistic Soven theory took the second clause to mean that “he was not killed by crucifixion.” So basically we interpreted the verse as “they did not kill him by crucifixion.” It was a tense statement, but pointing to a problematic interpretation of the Quran does not answer the argument. To test this theory, we need to isolate its main point: Although Jesus could have died on the cross under natural circumstances, he did not die because God miraculously preserved him. Of course, this is an explanation that requires a miracle, but we must repeatedly remind ourselves that we are doing our best to investigate as objective observers. Should an objective observer conclude that such a miracle occurred? The miracle occurred unless there is another plausible explanation, and even then only under certain circumstances. In this case, there is another explanation, and it is very likely: Jesus died on the cross. For an objective observer to conclude that a miracle occurred in this case, one would have to believe that Jesus’ death on the cross was not even probable. But of course it is more likely. The explanation is clear. As for the verses cited as subtle works of divine design, it is worth noting that these verses are in the context of the four Gospels, where Jesus repeatedly prophesied his death and he did indeed die. It is poor handling of the texts to extract verses from their context and say that they claim the exact opposite of their context, unless there is a good reason to do so. In this case, there is not only no good reason to do so, but also a good reason: the verses predicting and announcing Jesus’ death are abundant and clear, while these “subtle traces” are often isolated and require an unlikely interpretation. One of the fundamental rules of sound hermeneutics, whether Quranic or biblical or secular, is to interpret unclear statements in light of clear statements, not vice versa. Ignoring the clear statements of Jesus’ death, and referring to these verses as an indication that God saved him, is poor research methodology. Some might point to the different accounts of the Gospels, such as the empty tomb scene, as a reason to set aside the Gospel testimony, but it does not follow logically. A conservative Christian might respond that these verses can all be reconciled: indeed, there were two angels on the scene who appeared as young men. All four Gospel accounts are consistent with this understanding, although none of them explicitly states it. But even less conservative scholars who might agree that the empty tomb accounts are inconsistent need not conclude that this somehow negates the uniform testimony of Jesus’ death on the cross. That does not logically follow. Furthermore, the theistic theory of the Seven is inconsistent with important historical facts. For example, it casts Pilate in a God-fearing light who was willing to conspire with the Jews to save an innocent man. Historically, the opposite seems to be true. Pilate was ruthless and did not hesitate to kill Jews if it meant maintaining order and Roman rule. This is true in history, as Josephus records Pilate’s willingness to kill innocent Samaritans, as well as robbing the temple treasury and beating those who protested. He was guilty, ordering his crucifixion when threatened with treason. Finally, and most problematically for the careful historian, the Soven theory offers no explanation for the origin of the Christian church. What compelled the early Christians to preach a crucified and resurrected Jesus if they themselves had conspired to save him? Were they liars, or should we dismiss other sets of evidence and argue that they did not preach a crucified and resurrected Christ? Ultimately, this theory requires the scholar to ignore not only all the evidence for the death of Jesus, but also our entire understanding of early Christianity, although the theory is shaped by dozens of sources. This is an important point to which we will return in the midway summary after Part 8.

Responding to the Succession Theory: The Quran as a Source for the Life of Jesus
The substitution theory is even less of a historical argument than Sowon's, and it must be remembered that no objective observer should conclude that Allah performed a miracle for which there is a clear explanation. Why should anyone argue that Jesus did not die and that God instead transferred the image of Jesus to someone else? In this case, there is really not much room for doubt: the basis of this Islamic belief is simply that the Quran insists on it. However, if we are to be fair, we must assess the potential for the Quran to be accurate. Do we have good reason to believe that the Quran might know something about Jesus that the New Testament does not? Of course, we must remind ourselves that we are investigating as objective observers and cannot start by assuming that the Quran was inspired by Allah. Until we can trace the Quran's teachings about Jesus back to earlier times, we must conclude that this testimony is six hundred years late and more than six hundred miles removed, and is therefore unlikely to tell us anything more accurate than the Gospels. It comes from the time of Jesus' eyewitnesses and from the vicinity of his community. But the teachings of the Quran about Jesus can be traced back to earlier times. Much of what the Quran teaches had already been taught. For example, consider 5.110: “Remember when God said, ‘O Jesus, son of Mary, remember My favor upon you and upon your mother, when I supported you with the Holy Spirit, so that you spoke to people in the cradle [by miracle] and in your old age [by revelation], and when I taught you the Book and the Wisdom and the Torah and the Gospel, and when you made from clay by My permission the shape of a bird, then you breathed into it and it became a bird by My permission, and you healed the blind and the lepers by My permission, and when you brought the dead [alive] from the grave by My permission, and when I protected the Children of Israel from you when you had brought them clear proofs.” We will focus on the two teachings in italics, starting with the second: the Quran teaches that Jesus miraculously brought clay birds to life. We find no narrative basis for this miracle. It is simply presented in a list of God’s provisions for Jesus. But for those familiar with the apocalyptic gospels, the context for this story is already known, because it is well-known. In the mid-second century, people began to produce stories about Jesus, “fables,” if you will, often basing their stories on the mysterious seals of his childhood and focusing on his ability to perform miracles. These fabricated gospels are called the infancy gospels. One of the more entertaining examples is the childhood Gospel of Thomas. In this infancy gospel, Jesus is five years old and is particularly mischievous, often bullying other boys with his supernatural powers. While Jesus was by a mountain stream making a pond, another boy came and left the puddles, so Jesus cursed him and immediately the boy dried up. Later, another boy accidentally ran past Jesus and hit him on the shoulder, so the young Jesus cursed the boy, who died immediately. The parents of these boys came to Joseph and told him to control Jesus, who blinded them. When Jesus’ teacher tries to teach him to respect his elders, the five-year-old Jesus rebukes him and instead begins to teach the teacher! Everyone is amazed at what a child Jesus is. Until his teacher exclaims in despair that Jesus must be an angel or God himself, Jesus laughs in approval and heals all the people he cursed. So the story goes, with the boy Jesus using his miraculous abilities to curse some people, heal others, carry water in his clothes, or even repair things. Whenever the people shouted that he must be God or an angel of God, he abated from his wickedness. To give the story a realistic tone, the author ends the story by tying it to the Gospel of Luke, in which Jesus is twelve years old and in the temple, where the elders are amazed at his knowledge, as they were. The Infancy Gospel of Thomas This entertaining story gives us the context for the Quranic teaching that Jesus gave life to clay birds. Early in the story, when a Jew saw Jesus making clay birds on the Sabbath, he went to Joseph to accuse Jesus of doing something illegal on the holy day. The mischievous little Jesus knew that making clay birds on the Sabbath was illegal, but playing with live birds was legal. The Quran refers to this famous account in 5.110 without providing any context, assuming it to be historical fact. The same Quranic verse also says that Jesus spoke to people in the cradle. Chapter 19 of the Quran, the chapter named after Mary, provides further details. Verse 23 shows Mary in labor, leaning against a date palm. In her agony, she says, "Would that I had died before this!" Jesus, who gives birth to him, replies, "Do not worry, your Lord has given you a river beneath you." Shortly thereafter, when his people ask him if he has had immoral relations, he points to Jesus and says that they should ask him themselves. They reply, "How can we speak to a child in the cradle?" The child Jesus replies, "Indeed, I am a servant of Allah. He has given me the Book and made me a prophet." This account has striking similarities to another childhood gospel, the Arabic Infancy Gospel of the Savior, also known as the Arabic Infant Gospel. In his introduction, after some historical errors about Joseph and Caiaphas, he says: “Jesus spoke and indeed, as he lay in his cradle, he said to his mother Mary: I am Jesus, the Son of God, the Logos, you brought him forth, as the angel Gabriel announced to you. And my Father has sent me to save the world.” The announcement of the beloved Jesus in the Quran is very similar to the Islamic version of the announcement of the newborn Jesus in the Arabic infancy gospel.
Response to the theory of succession: The mystical influence on 4. 157
Many other Quranic teachings can be traced back to earlier fictional accounts, but the important point for now is Message 4.157: “He was neither killed nor crucified, but the matter was made doubtful to them.” Is there any reason to believe that this teaching predates the Quran and is perhaps more reliable than the Gospel accounts? As it turns out, another well-known source in the second century taught exactly this. This Gospel quotes Basilides, a mystical teacher whose school of thought continued for centuries after his death. The word mysticism refers to secret knowledge, since mystics believed that people needed secret knowledge to escape from the material world, which was inherently evil. Irenaeus records what Basilides taught about Jesus’ death on the cross: “He [Christ] did not die, but Simon of Cyrene, who was compelled to carry the cross in his place. So that the latter, who had been transformed by him, to be thought of as Jesus, was crucified in ignorance and error, while Jesus himself took the form of Simon and, standing by, laughed at them. He was not killed or crucified, but was suspected (suspected) because he had changed his form with Simon of Cyrene. This is precisely what is connected with the theory of succession, the interpretation of the majority of the Quran. But why did Basilides teach this? Was it because Basilides had access to some historical truth? If so, perhaps the Quran knows something that the New Testament does not. The mystics had a wide range of beliefs, but one of the most common was that there were many gods who proceeded from the Father, the unbegotten God. Basilides affirms this belief and teaches that the first emanation of the Father is the mind of the Father. That is, Jesus, the first-born God. After other genesis which produced the lower gods, it was the lowest god who created the material world. According to Basilides, this is the God of the Jews with his angels. The Father sent Jesus to save the mystics from the God of the Jews, the creator of this evil world. Since the material world is evil, Basilides says that Jesus could not have had a material body, therefore he could not have been crucified. We find this in the passage which immediately follows: For since he was not born of the incorporeal power and mind of the Father, he transformed himself as he pleased, and thus ascended to him who sent him and mocked them. It could not be held and was invisible to all. Therefore those who know these things are freed from the governments which constituted the world. So that it is not for us to confess the crucifixion, but the one who came in the form of a man and was supposed to have been crucified, and who is called Jesus and sent by the Father, by which means he may destroy the works of the creators of the world. Thus the Quranic teaching about Jesus who “was not killed nor crucified, but was made suspicious” seems to go back to a second-century mystic. The source of this late and fabricated gospel is promoting “hidden knowledge” to support its polytheistic worldview, not providing historical information about the life of Jesus. We can be sure that the narrative 4.157 is not such as an objective scholar would consider reliable historical evidence, and so we must reject the alternative theory.
Response to the Succession Theory: Final Words
In the context of a Muslim scholar, such as myself, one might be tempted to respond that the verses of the Quran are from Allah, not from these fabricated sources, and are historically valid regardless of this. But as before, I had to remind myself repeatedly that I was investigating as an objective observer and could not come to this conclusion without assuming that the Quran was inspired. The temporal priority of the apocryphal gospels is obvious, as is the fact that their narratives are not historically accurate. But there is a stronger reason to challenge this assumption. The information in these three passages makes very good textual sense in the apocryphal gospels, but it makes no sense in the Quran. In the Infancy Gospel of Thomas, Jesus, as a miraculous mischievous spirit, breathed life into clay birds, which is entirely consistent with the text. In the Arabic Infancy Gospel, he uttered words at birth, because he was the eternal Word of God. In the Gospel of Basilides, he was neither killed nor crucified, because he was divine and did not have a physical body. But why does the Quran say that Jesus gives life to clay birds? No reason or context is given. The Quran merely refers to a well-known narration. Why could the infant Jesus speak at birth? Again, no reason is given. He can only explain why Jesus was not crucified and why someone else appeared like him? The Quran does not give a reason; it simply claims this secret knowledge as truth. These three narrations are much better in the context of the fabricated gospels, and given that the Quran is the later source of all of them, we must conclude that the Quran’s views on Jesus are from these later and fabricated sources that are historically unreliable and theologically contrary to Islam.
















Chapter 24
Conclusion
Jesus died on the cross.

Leaving Mike’s house that evening, my opinion about Jesus’ death had not changed. It is rare to overturn a lifelong belief overnight. But one thing was becoming clear to me: My belief that Jesus did not die on the cross was based on faith in Islam, not the facts of history. Historically, the evidence for Jesus’ death was overwhelmingly in favor of Christianity and against Islam. Of course, Islam could still be true. If there were other good reasons for believing in the Quran, such as the common Muslim arguments for the inspiration of the Quran, and if those arguments outweighed the historical evidence for Jesus’ death, I would be justified in maintaining my faith in the Quran. In Islam, for that reason, as far as my personal beliefs were concerned, I did not waver in my Islamic faith. In my heart, I believed that the Quran would soon be vindicated. But, as far as my research on the subject was concerned, I remembered my commitment to objectivity and had to be honest with myself. Would an objective observer conclude that Jesus died by crucifixion? Of course, atheist and mystical scholars answered this question for us with a resounding yes, absolutely. The reasons were numerous: the accounts of Jesus’ death appear with lightning speed, are claimed dozens of times over a hundred years, and the chorus of narratives is composed of Christian, Jewish, and Roman voices. No one had ever survived a full-scale Roman crucifixion, and if Jesus had, it would have been a much more appealing message to the early church than a crucified Savior. The answers of Islam, the Soven theory, and the substitution theory are untenable, both because they suggest miracles in the face of much more obvious and plausible explanations, and because they require a complete reexamination of the historical facts of early Christianity, as we will soon see. Furthermore, there is little reason to trust the Quran as a historical source about the life of Jesus, since it was composed six hundred years after Jesus and more than six hundred miles away from where he lived. Although there are narrations in the Quran that date back to earlier periods, they are from later, fabricated gospels that are not historically reliable. In conclusion, we must agree with Gerd Lodman: "The fact of Jesus' death by crucifixion is undeniable."















Part 7
Did Jesus rise from the dead?











Chapter 25
Positive case
The best explanation of the facts

Jesus’ death on the cross is not the end of the Christian message. The gospel is that Jesus then rose from the dead. While every other life ended in death, Jesus’ death ended in life, and his resurrection is the basis of all Christian confidence. Death is not something to be feared. Jesus has conquered it, and we are in him. His resurrection has been the source of Christian faith since the foundation of the church. In the book of Acts, Luke records the first Christian sermon, in which Peter concludes with the proclamation of the resurrection: “This Jesus God raised up, of which we are all witnesses” (Acts 2:32). In his next recorded sermon, Peter says, “You killed the Source of life, but God raised him from the dead, and we are witnesses of this” (Acts 3:15). No fewer than eleven passages in the book of Acts record the early church proclaiming the resurrection of Jesus. This was the message that established the early church: Jesus rose from the dead. But the importance of the resurrection is perhaps most clearly stated by Paul, who says in 1 Corinthians 15:14, “And if Christ has not been raised, then our preaching is in vain, and your faith is in vain.” If Jesus remains dead and does not rise, then the Christian preaching and the Christian faith are in vain. If He did not conquer death, neither will we. Did Jesus rise from the dead? Do we have good reason to believe in the resurrection of Christ? If so, this is a solid foundation for the Christian faith. If not, then Christians are the most pitiable people. This is what It was about a year after Mike and I sat in his living room, but this time there were seven hundred more people listening. In 2004, Mike Likona debated the question, "Did Jesus rise from the dead?" with Shabir Eli, a famous Muslim debater.


Minimal facts approach
That night, Mike was advancing an argument pioneered by his friend Gary, called the Minimal Facts Approach. The advantages of this argument are that it is powerful, easy to understand, and so simple that it can be stated in a single sentence: There are historical facts about the crucifixion of Jesus that almost all historians agree on, and that are by far the best explanation for them. The fact is that Jesus rose from the dead. To provide background, Gary had spent thirty years cataloging the positions of historical Jesus scholars, whether they were mystics, atheists, Jews, Christians, or any other worldview. He read everything that had been published about the historical Jesus in scholarly journals and monographs written in French, English, or German. He found that on some issues, more than 90 percent of scholars agreed. As he examined these facts, he realized that Jesus’ resurrection explained them best. So far. Any other explanation would ignore or suppress the historical facts about Jesus’ death. Indeed, when we consider the historian’s task, it is precisely that: to present a narrative model that makes the best sense of the historical record. In this case, the only model that really fits the facts is that Jesus rose from the dead. Taking a list of facts from Gary, Mike has narrowed it down to three, which we will now briefly examine:
1. Jesus died by crucifixion.
2. Jesus' followers truly believed that the risen Jesus had appeared to them.
3. People who were not followers of Jesus truly believed that the risen Jesus had appeared to them.
Fact 1: Jesus died by crucifixion. The previous chapter examined in detail the death of Jesus by crucifixion. More than any other issue about Jesus, historians are convinced that the death of Jesus is a historical fact.
Fact 2: Jesus’ followers really believed that the risen Jesus appeared to them. Historians are also convinced that Jesus’ followers came to believe that they had seen the risen Jesus. The reasons for this are many. First, the declaration appears early in church history. 1 Corinthians 15:3-7, the “flash of news” Christian creed that reports Jesus’ death a few years after his crucifixion, also includes a list of people to whom he appeared. The account reads: “Christ died for our sins according to the Scriptures, and was buried, and was raised on the third day according to the Scriptures, and appeared to Peter, then to the twelve, then to more than five hundred brothers at that time, most of whom remain to the present, though some have fallen asleep in death. Then he appeared to James, then to all the apostles.” According to the acclaimed and respected scholar James Dunn, “We can be quite certain that this tradition was codified as a tradition a few months after Jesus’ death.” The teachings that the early Christians chose to codify and pass on to one another included a list of people to whom the risen Jesus appeared. Not only is Peter first on this list, essentially making the announcement on his own authority, but the list says that Jesus appeared to five hundred people simultaneously. Second, the proclamation of Jesus’ resurrection is confirmed by eyewitnesses. For example, Paul, while recounting the above belief some twenty years after Jesus’ death, says that most of the five hundred eyewitnesses were still alive, as if to say, “If you want to talk to eyewitnesses of the risen Jesus, there are more than 250 to choose from! This follows the pattern recounted in Acts 10:40-41, where Peter emphasizes that they were eyewitnesses: “But God raised him up on the third day and made him manifest, not to all, but to witnesses chosen beforehand by him, to us who ate and drank with him after he rose from the dead.” Of course, Luke reports other accounts that emphasize that there were eyewitnesses to Jesus’ resurrection, such as the first gospel proclamation found in Acts 2:32, which we have already noted: “This Jesus God raised up, and we are all witnesses of it.” To Paul, Peter, and Luke, we can add John’s emphasis on the testimony of the risen Jesus, recorded in John 21:24. Third, the disciples were willing to die for their belief that the risen Jesus had appeared to them. Of course, being willing to die for a belief does not make that belief true, but it almost always ensures its truthfulness. People often do not give up their lives for what they know to be false. In this case, the disciples were willing to die for what they claimed to have personally seen: the risen Jesus. Various eyewitness testimonies are found in recorded history, from the testimony of Stephen (Acts 7:60) and Including prominent disciples such as James the brother of John (Acts 12:2) and Peter himself (1 Corinthians 5:2-7). Having seen Jesus overcome death, they no longer feared it as they had in the Garden of Gethsemane. They truly believed that Jesus had risen. For these three reasons and more, the vast majority of scholars are convinced that Jesus’ followers truly believed that he had risen from the dead. Accordingly, Wedderburn has stated: “It is an indisputable historical fact that at times the disciples somehow came to believe that they had seen the risen Jesus.”
Fact 3: People who were not followers of Jesus actually believed that the risen Jesus appeared to them. Finally, there were some who did not follow Jesus and actually believed that Jesus had appeared to them after He had risen from the dead. The first and most important of these was Saul of Tarsus, also known as Paul. During the time of Jesus, Paul was a student of Rabbi Gamaliel, one of the Tanakh, the most influential teachers of the Oral Torah in history. According to tradition, Gamaliel was the grandson of the great Jewish teacher Hillel, the founder of the Jewish school of thought known as the House of Hillel. With such noble Jewish lineage, Paul began to persecute Christians and had the power and zeal to arrest them and even oversee their execution. However, something happened to Paul that caused him to join those who were persecuting him, even giving up his high Jewish position. According to Paul, the risen Jesus appeared to him. In the book of Acts, Luke shares Paul’s testimony three times, where he makes it clear that he believed because he saw the risen Jesus. Paul himself testifies to this in 1 Corinthians 15. For proclaiming that Jesus had risen from the dead, he was flogged five times, beaten three times with rods, stoned to death, and finally beheaded. Paul had many opportunities to change his mind or repent, but he gave up everything, including his own life, and testified that he had personally seen the risen Jesus. Another key figure who denied Jesus throughout his life but followed him after his crucifixion is James, the brother of Jesus. In Mark’s Gospel, we find Jesus’ brothers coming to gather him because they thought he had “gone out of his mind” (3:21, 31), and in John’s Gospel, Jesus’ brothers mock him so much that the Gospel says, “not even his brothers believed in him” (7:3-5). Even when Jesus was being crucified, his brothers did not come to support him, so that their mother Mary had to be entrusted to John’s care (John 19:26-27). But after Jesus’ crucifixion, we see his brothers counted among the believers (Acts 1:14). The creed in 1 Corinthians 15 tells us why: The risen Jesus appeared to James (v. 7), who later became the leader of the Jerusalem church (Galatians 1:19) before being executed by Annas the son of Annas. Four ancient sources report the execution of James, the brother of Jesus, including the Jewish historian Flavius Josephus. So in addition to the disciples, Paul and James—men who did not follow Jesus during their lifetimes—gave their lives for seeing the risen Jesus. “That the followers of Jesus, and later Paul, had resurrection experiences, is, in my opinion, a fact,” says Saunders, a self-described “liberal, modern, secularist.” David Catchpole, emeritus professor at the University of Exeter, adds: “James’s appearance… is not something that can be worked out from existing sympathy or commitment. In this respect it was not unlike what later happened to Paul.” Scholars Shanks and Witherington add: “It seems likely that James, like Paul, was converted to the Jesus movement because he saw the risen Jesus at some point, for nothing before Easter can explain his becoming such a follower of Jesus. Much less a leader of the followers of Jesus.”
From facts to arguments
Let us now at least summarize the facts. When we consider the life of Jesus, we can be fairly certain of three conclusions: Jesus died by crucifixion. Jesus’ followers truly believed that the risen Jesus appeared to them. And people who were not followers of Jesus truly believed that the risen Jesus appeared to them. Given that the historian’s job is to present a narrative model that makes the best sense of the historical record, what is the best historical conclusion about Jesus’ life? According to the minimal facts approach, the best explanation of the facts, by far, is that Jesus did indeed rise from the dead. Any other explanation ignores or strains the facts too much to be acceptable. For example, one of the most common alternative hypotheses is that the disciples were hallucinating about Jesus’ resurrection. It is a well-known medical phenomenon that people who have recently lost loved ones may hallucinate their presence. Could it be that the disciples, who had been following Jesus for years and deeply trusted that he would be their Messiah, were so desperate to see him again that they hallucinated his return? Jesus’ death by crucifixion (fact 1) certainly fits into this theory, but does the fact that Jesus’ followers actually believed they had seen the risen Jesus (fact 2) fit in? When we consider that he appeared first to Peter, then to the twelve, then to five hundred at a time, and then to James, it seems hard to account for all of these appearances as a mourning hallucination. There is certainly no equivalent for five hundred people having the same mourning hallucination at the same time. For that reason alone, this hypothesis is highly unlikely. Even if there were five hundred unprecedented people who had mourning hallucinations, it seems unlikely that any of them would be convinced that they had seen Jesus himself rather than a passing dream. But for the sake of argument, let us assume that the hallucination hypothesis explains Fact 2: His disciples really believed that he had risen, but what they actually saw was a hallucination. Even if this hypothesis has the benefit of the doubt, it simply does not account for Fact 3: people who did not follow Jesus really believed that he had appeared to them. Paul does not fit the psychological profile of someone who hallucinates Jesus’ return. He had no emotional attachment to Jesus and no hope for him, and Paul had everything to lose. Why would Paul hallucinate the risen Jesus? It is simply not true. So the hallucination hypothesis suppresses Fact 2 and does not fit Fact 3. It is not a plausible hypothesis. Another theory is that the body of Jesus was stolen by the disciples, and they proclaimed the message of the risen Jesus to prove their Messiahship, even though it was a hoax. Fact 1, the death of Jesus, fits this hypothesis, but fact 2 does not: the disciples really believed they had seen the risen Jesus. It is unlikely that Peter, James, Stephen, and the others died for a known lie, and certainly not on purpose. Of course, the same can be said for fact 3. Paul and James, men who had not followed Jesus during their lifetime, have no reason to have done this trick. The stolen body hypothesis fits neither fact 2 nor fact 3. It is not a likely hypothesis. The common Muslim response, as we saw in the previous chapter, is that Jesus did not die on the cross. This theory explains facts 2 and 3, but it is a direct contradiction of fact 1: that Jesus died by crucifixion. The proposition of this hypothesis, again, is a contradiction in terms. The theory does not fit fact 1. It is not a plausible hypothesis. The hypothesis that fits all the seven facts, and fits them well, is exactly what the disciples, along with others who were not disciples, declared: Jesus rose from the dead. This is the only explanation that fits the puzzle pieces together. The resurrection hypothesis explains facts 1, 2, and 3. Since it is the only hypothesis that explains the historical facts without suppressing or ignoring them, an objective observer must conclude that the resurrection of Jesus is by far the best explanation for the data.
Miracle and the eyewitness
At this point, most non-Christian Jesus scholars follow Sanders and say, “I don’t know what actually led to these experiences.” Since there is no possible natural explanation, they offer none. This is sufficient, except that it imposes a naturalistic bias—that we cannot conclude that a miracle occurred. I argue that an objective scholar should not have such a bias. We should be open to the idea, but be very cautious about jumping to the explanation of a miracle. It is important to note that in the previous chapter I said, “An objective observer should not conclude that a miracle occurred unless there is another plausible explanation, and even then only under certain circumstances.” In the case of Jesus’ resurrection, unlike the substitution theory or the Soven theory, there is no other plausible explanation. All other conclusions diminish credibility. Even so, it is still not enough for an objective observer to conclude that a miracle has occurred. We need certain conditions. Specifically, we need a context that contains supernatural expectations. For example, let’s imagine that a blind man, rummaging through the cupboard above him, accidentally knocks over a bottle and spills its contents. The oil spills on his head. As he wipes it off, he realizes that he can see again. Should he conclude that a miracle has occurred? I would argue that he should not. Although something extraordinary and inexplicable has occurred, one should not automatically conclude that all unusual and inexplicable events are miracles. Now imagine that this same blind man has prayed earnestly for years to regain his sight. One day, while praying, he becomes convinced that God is guiding him to go to the stranger’s house. After arguing with himself out loud because he had never heard from God before, he decides he has nothing to lose and goes to the house and knocks on the door. The owner of the house comes to the door, and as the man fumbles for words to explain who he is, the owner of the house says, “You don’t have to tell us who you are. My family and I were just praying together and we had a strong intuition that God was sending a blind man to us for healing, and we are praying for his sight to be restored and anointing his head with oil.” The blind man is sitting and as the family prays for his sight to be restored, they anoint him for healing. Oil is poured on his head. As he wipes it off, he realizes that he can see again. In this latter scenario, should the blind man conclude that a miracle has occurred? I argue that in the latter case, he is justified in concluding that a miracle occurred. Although the physical act in both scenarios is the same—the oil is poured on his head—the latter scenario is accompanied by supernatural expectations. Given the context, it is not unreasonable to conclude that he experienced a miracle. Furthermore, if the circumstances are open to investigation by the investigator, the investigator should also conclude that a miracle occurred. Let us imagine that the man’s prayer and argument with God were recorded on surveillance video, such as a family prayer meeting and anointing with oil, as a result of which the blind man can now see. An objective investigator not only can responsibly conclude that a miracle occurred, but it is his duty to do so. Otherwise, he is not objective, but he is introducing a naturalistic bias. Likewise, if an extraordinary and inexplicable event has occurred in history, and that extraordinary event is laden with supernatural expectations, and there are solid historical facts surrounding that event that have no other plausible explanation, a historian can reasonably conclude that a miracle did indeed occur.
Conclusion
As a Muslim watching Mike’s discussion, I had to agree that if Jesus had really died on the cross, there was very good reason to believe that he rose from the dead. Historically, these three facts are undeniable: Jesus died by crucifixion, his disciples really believed that he had risen, and even men who were not his disciples really believed that they had seen him. Nothing explains these facts without strain except the resurrection hypothesis, and even as objective observers, the spiritual context allows us to conclude that a miracle occurred. Along with the early church, history testifies that Jesus rose from the dead. But despite what history showed, I was still not convinced that Jesus really died on the cross. There was one other issue that I and the Muslim world around me found not only problematic but also deeply offensive.








Chapter 26
Islamic answer
It's all Paul's fault.

After Mike had at least presented the facts for Jesus’ resurrection, Shabir Elly stepped in to defend the Islamic position. He responded by essentially challenging Fact 1, stating that we cannot be certain that Jesus died by crucifixion. This was not only an understandable response based on 4.157 of the Quran, but was in fact the expected response. Muslims are generally less concerned with the supernatural implications of the resurrection than with Jesus’ physical death in the first place. Part of the reason for this is because, along with Christians, most Muslims believe in the ascension of Jesus. Immediately after denying Jesus’ death on the cross, the Quran states in 4.158, “But God raised him up to Himself.” Given this belief, the average Muslim would not immediately think of the resurrection as a problem, usually assuming it to be the result of a Christian misunderstanding that Jesus died on the cross. According to the average Muslim, yes, Jesus was raised to heaven, but no, he did not die on the cross first. It was not a resurrection, but an ascension. For these reasons, of the hundreds of Muslim-Christian conversations I have seen and participated in, first as a Muslim and then as a Christian, Muslims make a great effort to challenge Jesus’ death, but generally take the rest of the story for granted. The Intact Resurrection Apart from what was discussed in Chapter 22, there is no common response to the resurrection arguments, with one important exception.
Muslims and Paul
Since the Quran teaches that Jesus was a prophet and that his followers were righteous men, we have great respect for Jesus’ disciples. According to 3.52, the disciples responded to Jesus’ call to follow God in righteousness, and in 3.55, Allah promises to exalt Jesus’ followers and make them superior to others until the Day of Judgment. The opposite of Islamic teaching is Christianity, which teaches that God became a man and died on the cross for our sins—teachings that Muslims find unthinkable and blasphemous. So, from our perspective as a Muslim community, Christian teachings were corrupted. But when did they become corrupted? When we trace these beliefs, we find that they were the beliefs of the early churches. Therefore, Muslims must believe that somehow, in the disciples’ own time, the early church was infiltrated and its message corrupted. This must have been the work of someone powerful, someone other than the disciples. There is only one obvious figure who fits this description: Paul. This is why Paul’s motives, character, and authority have been called into question. It was Paul who took the religion that Jesus taught and turned it into a religion about Jesus. In a debate with Mike, Shabir Ali described mainstream Christianity as Paul’s invention. “Later Christianity followed Paul’s line,” Ali argues. “There was tension between Paul and the early disciples of Jesus and Jesus’ family, so Paul celebrated and Jesus’ family and disciples were despised.” Ali points out that Paul’s disagreements with Peter and James are evidence of a split in early Christianity that ultimately led to Paul’s version winning out. The second common and important argument is that Jesus told his disciples to keep the law, while Paul said the law had been abolished. Evidence for this position is found by highlighting Matthew 5:17, where Jesus says, “Do not think that I have come to abolish the Law or the Prophets; I have not come to abolish them but to fulfill them.” This verse seems to imply that Jesus came to uphold the law, but Paul says in Romans 3:28, “For we maintain that a man is justified by faith apart from the works of the law.” Thus, Muslims say that Paul contradicts Jesus. There are many other common challenges to Paul that Muslims offer, but the third and final one that we will address here is one of the most important. Muslims often argue that Paul never met Jesus and rarely comments on the historical Jesus in his letters. So Paul should not be concerned with who Jesus really was, but rather with making Jesus the Son of God, even God himself. This is just a quick attack on the reasons why Muslims distrust Paul, seeing him as a corrupter of true Christianity and the founder of a pagan religion that worships a man and ignores the law of God—mainstream Christianity today. This view is espoused by Muslim apologists and imams around the world, and it filters down relentlessly to ordinary Muslims like me. In fact, almost every Muslim-Christian conversation I have heard about early Christianity ends up deadlocked over Paul himself. It is hard to overstate how much Muslims distrust Paul and how much they hold him responsible for the form of Christianity we see today.
Conclusions from the Islamic Response to the Resurrection
Not only do Muslims disagree that Jesus actually died on the cross, they question the entire foundation of Christianity because a man who was not a disciple and had never even seen Jesus successfully infiltrated the ranks and hijacked the early church. The religion that Jesus taught became a religion about Jesus, and what was a religion of following God’s law and worshipping God alone became a religion that ignored the law and worshipped a man alongside God. Although Christianity was very similar to Islam in its beginnings, it is gone forever because of Paul.












Chapter 27
Assessing the Islamic response
Paul and the disciples from the right perspective

To be fair, the Muslim challenge to Paul is not entirely without reason. He is a very remarkable figure in the early church who has had an equally remarkable influence. Can we really trust Paul? If Paul never met Jesus before his crucifixion, how could he have accurately conveyed the religion that Jesus taught? Could modern Christianity possibly be the product of Paul? This question became popular in academic circles in the 1980s, and some Western scholars even answered it in the affirmative. Perhaps the most notable such answer was given by the Jewish scholar and playwright Hayam Maccoby in his 1986 book Mythmaker: Paul and the Invention of Christianity. Maccoby goes so far as to say that it was Paul who founded Christianity. Similar arguments were made by the equally distinguished Karen Armstrong, who favored many Muslim apologists, who said: “Paul not only had a significant influence on Christianity, but… in a real sense he was its founder. He could be called the first Christian.” Because of efforts like those, this view became a hot topic of debate in academic circles, but the dust quickly settled and the victory was decisive: Paul remained a follower of Christ, not the founder of Christianity. The scholarly consensus has remained the same ever since. All this being said, it goes without saying that this is a good question that deserves much more space than we have here to cover, so we will answer Paul’s question broadly and briefly. We will then examine the specific problem that this challenge to Paul poses for the Islamic view of Jesus’ disciples, before finally addressing this issue of Jesus’ resurrection.


Paul's Review
Part of the reason why almost no scholar today agrees with the common Muslim portrayal of Paul is that it portrays him as a usurping deceiver. I recall the imam at our mosque teaching us that Paul saw a power vacuum among the disciples after Jesus’ death, so he infiltrated the church and rose through the ranks to take the reins. The problem with this portrayal is obvious to those who know Paul’s background: as a student of Gamaliel, in the lineage of Hillel, he was far more confident of his power than he was in the persecuted church. He already had the authority to arrest heretics and preside over executions, and his power only increased. Instead, he chose the humble life of a persecuted man who toiled for his wages (Acts 18:3). This Islamic portrayal of Paul as a deceiver is also suspect, because Paul was honest enough to die for his faith. The account of his martyrdom comes a few years after the flogging, and scholars have no doubt about it. As we have already discussed, those who are willing to die for their beliefs may well be wrong, but they are almost certainly right. According to the record, Paul was flogged five times, beaten with rods three times, and even stoned until he was presumed dead before he was finally executed (2 Corinthians 11:24-25). If he had simply deceived the people, he would have had ten opportunities to repent before receiving life-threatening punishments. Would he not have stopped his rebellion? If he was going to lose his life, what would he gain by deceiving everyone? In fact, Paul gave up power, prestige, personal security, and even his own life. He gained nothing materially by following Jesus.
Paul, Jewish Law, and the Disciples' Authority
It is true that Paul had a dispute with Peter, as recorded in Galatians 2:11-14, but the reading of the text is important. Paul informs us that shortly after his conversion, he went to Peter and stayed with him for fifteen days. After sitting with Peter and sometimes James, the brother of Jesus, Paul traveled from city to city for fourteen years, proclaiming the gospel. Then, to make sure that he was still preaching according to the disciples’ teachings, he returned to Jerusalem and delivered his sermon to the disciples. After hearing him, Peter, James, and John confirmed him and sent him back to Antioch, saying that he should preach to the Gentiles while they preached to the Jews in Jerusalem. Throughout this time, Paul refers to the disciples as “pillars” of the church and “venerable pillars” (Galatians 2:6, 9). This discussion only occurs when Peter is in a new situation and acts with a double standard in order not to offend some people. After leaving Jerusalem, where he was a Jewish preacher and the church was full of Jewish believers, Peter went to Antioch in Syria, “the center of Gentile Christianity.” Peter was willing to eat with Gentiles because he no longer considered the Law binding (Galatians 2:12; Acts 10; 15:10-11). But when believers of Jewish background came to Antioch from Jerusalem, Peter did not want to offend them, so he stopped eating with Gentiles. This, significantly, caused problems in Antioch, and Paul corrected Peter for his double standard. It was Paul who first went to Peter and stayed with him and James. Later, Paul handed down what he taught to the disciples and continued to preach only when he received their approval. These are not the actions of one who wants to overthrow the church, but the actions of one who submits himself to the authority of the disciples. When it comes to Jewish law, it was Peter who declared his agreement to Paul’s ministry to the Gentiles, and it was Peter who did not consider Christians under the law, so much so that he ate with the Gentiles. The only criterion Paul used to argue with Peter was Peter himself: Peter was willing to eat with the Gentiles at one time, but at another time he refused to do so. Paul never tried to claim authority over Peter, but he challenged Peter with his own criteria: “If you, though you are a Jew, live like the Gentiles and not like the Jews, how is it that you compel the Gentiles to live like the Jews?” (Galatians 2:14). According to the book of Acts, it was Peter himself who began preaching the gospel to the Gentiles. Acts chapter 10 shares the story of Peter’s vision of preaching to the Gentiles and then being led by God to Caesarea to do just that. While preaching to Cornelius and those with him, Peter says, “You yourselves know how unlawful it is for a Jew to associate with or visit a Gentile. But God has shown me that I should not call any man common or unclean” (Acts 10:28). This was the attitude that Peter alluded to in Acts 15:7-11 when Peter himself suggested that Gentiles not be bound by the law. So Paul does not rebel against Peter and James. Rather, he submits to their authority. When there is a need to correct Peter, Paul does not dare to invoke his own authority, but rather reminds Peter of his own standards. Finally, Paul is not the one who absolves Gentiles from following the law. That is, Peter, the very disciple who began the era of evangelization to the Gentiles.
Jesus came to fulfill the law.
So if Peter and Paul agree that people are not bound by the law, what are we to make of Jesus’ words in Matthew 5:17, which says: “Do not think that I have come to abolish the Law or the Prophets; I have not come to abolish them, but to fulfill them”? Does this mean that Jesus came to follow the Law and expects his followers to do the same? The first thing to note is that Jesus says that he has come to “fulfill” the Law and the Prophets. Not “to follow” but “to fulfill.” The Greek word here (pleroo) has the sense of being complete and ending. When someone does something, it is finished and no longer binding. For example, if we take out a loan, we are obligated to make regular payments, but after we have paid off our loan in full, our payments end. In the same sense, Jesus is saying: “Do not think that I have come to "To abolish the Torah and the Prophets; I have not come to abolish them, but to fulfill them." To say that “fulfilling” the law and the prophets means “following” the Jewish law is to misunderstand Matthew 5:17 and miss its meaning. Second, when the Jews referred to the law and the prophets in this way, they were referring to the books of the Old Testament, not the commandments of the Jewish law. Remember, the Hebrew Bible is made up of the law and the prophets and the writings. So Jesus is saying here that he came to fulfill the Old Testament, not to follow the commandments. Putting these insights together, we can understand this verse correctly: Jesus is saying that the Old Testament is being fulfilled in him. He is not abolishing it, but rather fulfilling it. The Old Testament is being fulfilled because Jesus has come. There is much more to say about these rich verses, especially since the verses immediately following Matthew 5:17 are sometimes thought to imply that Jesus expected people to follow the Jewish law. Follow. Close study shows that this is not the case. It should also be noted that in the Sermon on the Mount, Jesus teaches some morals that directly contradict Jewish law, such as not swearing. Elsewhere in Matthew, Jesus says several times that he came to bring something new, not simply to follow the old (Matthew 9:16-17; 13:52). So Peter’s teaching that people are not bound by the law is a teaching that Paul consistently applies to Gentiles, and it is entirely consistent with Jesus’ message.
Paul and the Historical Jesus
The final challenge to Paul that we will address is the claim that Paul, having never seen Jesus, was less concerned with the historical Jesus than with making Jesus the Son of God, even God Himself. Although it is commonly assumed, it is not certain that Paul never saw Jesus during Jesus’ earthly ministry, for this is a conclusion based largely on silence. It is true that Paul does not often speak of the historical Jesus in the New Testament. But that does not mean that he was indifferent to the life of Jesus. Paul visited Peter for fifteen days after seeing the resurrected Jesus (Galatians 1:18). What, then, do we suppose, did Paul and Peter do during those two weeks? As scholar Dodd puts it: “We may suppose that they did not spend all their time talking about the weather. Paul learned about the historical Jesus while he sat under Peter. In fact, the word Paul uses for his meeting with Peter is historia, the Greek word from which the English word history is derived. He had a strong interest in the historical Jesus. So why don’t we see Paul talking more about the life of Jesus in his letters? In fact, he talks about the historical Jesus, especially in 1 Corinthians where he interprets the words of Jesus or discusses the life of Jesus a total of six times. Furthermore, some of the accounts Paul cites about the life of Jesus show that he knew more than he said in his letters, such as the words “on the night he was betrayed” (1 Corinthians 11:23). It is hard to imagine that Paul would have said these words without knowing what happened on the night of Jesus’ betrayal. Yet Paul never discusses the details in his letters. Which leads to the second most important point: Letters are not meant to be comprehensive, especially letters between close relationships. I have written fewer than a dozen letters to my wife, and if people read those letters assuming they contain everything I wanted to tell her, they would be sorely disappointed. My wife and I have a strong relationship, and we talk for hours every day. What is said in person will be left unsaid in written communication, and usually only the essentials are expressed in the letter, while the most consistent things have already been said in person. Similarly, Paul personally knew the people he wrote to and spent weeks, sometimes years, with them. To expect anything different from Paul’s letters is to impose unrealistic expectations.
