So we can conclude that Paul was very interested in the real life of Jesus, not just the theological Jesus. The question of whether Paul made Jesus the Son of God, or perhaps even God himself, will be fully explored in the next chapter.
Summarizing Paul
There is little reason to doubt Paul’s sincerity. He received his training from Peter and submitted to Peter’s authority. He followed Peter and taught that Gentiles did not need to keep the Law, a corollary of Jesus’ teaching in Matthew 5:17. Although Paul did not know Jesus during his ministry, we can be sure that Paul learned historical facts about Jesus from Peter and knew many things about him that he did not mention in his letters. The common Muslim claim that Paul hijacked Christianity, imposed his own teachings, and corrupted the true religion is not only inconsistent with the biblical record, but also historically unfounded and has very little scholarly support. But beyond that, there is a theological problem for Muslims who argue that Paul corrupted Christianity.
The problem from the Islamic perspective of Paul
The common Muslim view of Paul has significant problems, even when viewed from an Islamic perspective. First, what happened to the disciples? How were they so easily overcome by Paul that they were either persuaded by his trickery and followed him or their voices were completely silenced, with no record of their opposition? Was this outsider so much more powerful than Jesus that he could have thwarted all of Jesus’ work and teachings? As a Muslim, I have never seen an example of how this could have happened, and I have never heard one since I left Islam. The problem becomes even more acute when we review one of the Quranic verses that promises Jesus: “O Jesus, I will take you to Myself and raise you up to Myself and purify you from the [taint] of those who disbelieved, and I will make those who follow you above those who disbelieved until the Day of Resurrection” (3:55). Allah promises to give the apostles superiority over the unbelievers, and Jesus is delivered from such unbelievers. The Muslim view of Paul as having overpowered the disciples and hijacked the message of Jesus seems to ignore the Quranic promise to the disciples. But it says absolutely nothing, never so much as to mention his name. Given the central role that Muslims often think Paul played in the corruption of Christianity, the silence is deafening. Why does the Quran not mention him? Is it because of the omission of the Quran that Muslims in the early and classical period of Islam, such as Tabari and Qurtubi, considered Paul to be a follower of Jesus? In US criminal law, as elsewhere in the world, three aspects of a crime must be proven before a suspect can be found guilty: means, motive, and opportunity. The Islamic view that Paul hijacked Christianity does not guarantee any of these three. Paul could not have had the necessary means because Allah had promised to make the disciples invincible. There is no plausible motive for Paul to deceive the church, since his efforts only brought him persecution and a death sentence. And no model has been proposed to explain how Paul might have had the opportunity to overcome all the disciples and kidnap the church. Of course, not only must Paul be presumed innocent until proven guilty, but as far as this investigation is concerned, there is simply no evidence to convict him. The case is closed.
Refocusing on the Resurrection
Finally, let’s get back to the original question: Did Jesus rise from the dead? The truth is, even if we ignore Paul entirely, we still have good reason to think that Jesus did rise from the dead. The disciples repeatedly proclaimed Jesus’ resurrection in the book of Acts, and their belief that Jesus rose from the dead is the best explanation for their transformation from fearful followers in the Garden of Gethsemane to courageous martyrs at the hands of the Romans and others. They no longer feared death because Jesus had conquered death. The same is true of James, Jesus’ unbelieving brother, who was willing to pay the ultimate price after seeing the risen Jesus. These accounts are found in the Gospels, Acts, John, 1 Peter, Hebrews, 1 Corinthians, Josephus, and elsewhere. To argue effectively against the resurrection from an Islamic perspective, one must do more than discredit the Pauline accounts. The resurrection of Jesus engulfed the disciples. It was their initial catalyst, their central message, their driving belief, and their ultimate hope. To deny this through an Islamic perspective, a perspective that honors Jesus and the disciples as men of God, is to ignore everything we know about the birth of the church.




Chapter 28
Conclusion
Jesus rose from the dead.

At the end of the debate, my friend David and I discussed what we thought about the Christian defense of the resurrection and the Islamic critique. We ended up sitting in his car in the parking lot for a few hours, processing our thoughts and thinking about the arguments. There was no question in my mind that Shabbir Ali was the more eloquent speaker and debater, and he was excellent at engaging the crowd. But when I examined the arguments carefully, even as a Muslim, I had to conclude that Mike’s minimal facts approach was more convincing than Shabbir’s critique. History seemed to definitely testify that Jesus died by crucifixion, that his followers sincerely believed that he rose, and even some who were not his followers sincerely believed that they had seen him. Mike suggested that the best explanation for these historical facts was that Jesus rose from the dead. Shabbir offered no better alternative. Instead, Shabbir tried to dismiss the data that did not fit the Islamic view: the death of Jesus and the trustworthiness of Paul. When we consider the arguments that Muslims commonly use to discredit Paul’s testimony, we find that they are very problematic. There is no reason to think that Paul was not sincere. The evidence shows that he submitted to the authority of the disciples, that Peter was the one who suggested to the new Christians that they not be bound by the law. And in fact it was Peter, James, and John who extended their hands to Paul in his ministry to the Gentiles. The common Islamic description of Paul as the one who hijacked Christianity not only ignores the promises of Allah in the Quran, but also provides no motive or means for Paul’s corruption of the church, and this requires a complete disregard for the records of the early church. So in answering the question I had asked myself, “Would an objective observer conclude that Jesus rose from the dead?” I had to admit that Jesus’ resurrection was indeed the best explanation for the historical facts. Mike’s explanation of the evidence was far more convincing than Shabbir’s selective rejection of the evidence. But just as when I researched the death of Jesus, the conclusion did not force me to abandon Islam. I believed that my Islamic faith was proven by other means, and that the Christian faith still had at least one fatal flaw: Jesus never claimed to be God. This was a critical point for me. As far as I was concerned, Jesus’ death and resurrection were important issues to consider, but whether Jesus claimed to be God was the real game-changer. I was prepared to go to any lengths to defend my faith and prove that he did not.



















Part 8
Did Jesus claim to be God?












Chapter 29
Positive case
Jesus was always God.

Nowhere is the gap between Christian and Islamic theology wider than over the person of Jesus. For Muslims, the doctrine of a divine Christ is blasphemy, and the Quran teaches that whoever accepts it will build his house in the flames of hell (5:72). For Christians, faith in the divinity of Christ is essential to salvation (Romans 10:9). What wider divergence could there be? As a Muslim in the West, I proudly saw myself as a true monotheist who honored Jesus. In contrast, the Christians around me, whether they were unwittingly or knowingly, were blaspheming the one true God and insulting Jesus. All other differences between Muslims and Christians were secondary to me, far less important than this one most important issue. In fact, I even boldly told Christians that Islam was superior to Christianity on this very issue. This was the slogan we heard in the mosques: “Islam is the true monotheism.” The Quran tells Muslims that Jesus never claimed to be God. Instead, people began to believe this after Jesus left the earth (5:116-117). So we believed that later Christians were responsible for the corruption of true Christianity. Knowledgeable people in our mosque told me that Roman paganism had influenced Christian thought, as Roman gods often had sons who were demigods. Other Muslims argued that the Council of Nicaea was responsible for Jesus’ death, while others accused Paul of this blasphemy. Regardless of the exact model, most of us believed that the Bible does not even portray Jesus as God, and certainly Jesus never claimed to be God himself.
But did he? If we set aside our Islamic beliefs and ask this question as objective observers, would we conclude that Jesus claimed to be God? Based on my own experience, the answer is a revolutionary and surprising yes. More than anything else, exploring this question has changed my life forever. My biggest opponent in discovering the answer was my own will. I didn’t want to see Jesus’ claim to divinity in the pages of history, so I kept backing down and shifting my stance to avoid what was becoming more and more apparent. While I can’t track every point of this internal fencing competition, I will provide the conclusion of each major contest.
Does the New Testament teach that Jesus is God?
At first, I wasn’t sure if the New Testament anywhere said that Jesus was God. I remember watching a Muslim speaker named Hamza Abdulmalik challenge Christians by saying that this doctrine is not found anywhere in the Bible. But then I watched a debate between him and James White, a Christian presenter, and the issue was completely resolved: Apart from anything that could potentially be hidden, 2 Peter 1:1 calls Jesus “God and Savior,” as does Titus 2:1-13.
Do the Gospels Teach That Jesus Is God?—The Gospel of John
Muslims certainly have no allegiance to the Bible, or even the New Testament. The Quran says that the Gospels were revealed as the word of God. I quickly made my case that, regardless of what other books of the Bible say, Jesus is not God in the Gospels. It was then that my friend David pointed me to the Gospel of John. There is no doubt that Jesus is presented as God in this Gospel. From the very beginning, John declares unequivocally that Jesus is God, that he has always existed, and that he is the instrument of all creation (1:1-3). Thus, the first three verses of John’s Gospel present Jesus as “the eternal God by whom the worlds were made.” John’s introduction ends by calling Jesus “the only God” (1:18). As John’s Gospel progresses, the Christology becomes more packed and detailed. Jesus is worthy of God’s honor (5:23). He asks people to believe in him as they believe in God (14:1). He claims to be able to save (5:21) and the earthly manifestation of God (14:8). He is the king of another world (18:36-37). He rules over all things (3:35). And he claims to be able to do whatever people ask in his name after he is gone, more or less meaning that he is omniscient, omnipotent, and omnipresent (14:13). Furthermore, he advises his opponents that his identity is central to salvation (8:24) and that he always existed before Abraham (8:58), in both cases using the divine name Yahweh from the Old Testament, “I am.” In what some consider the climax of the Gospel, a disciple realizes who he is and cries out in affirmation, “My Lord and my God!” to which Jesus responds, “Have you believed because you have seen me? Blessed are those who have not seen and yet have believed” (20:28-29). The climax of the Gospel of John is when a disciple declares that Jesus is God, and Jesus praises his affirmation. From beginning to end, the Gospel of John presents Jesus as God. This discovery struck a blow to my Islamic faith. If Jesus really did claim to be God, then the Quran must have been wrong about Jesus, which in turn meant that Islam was a lie. I couldn’t accept this, so I had to find a way out.
Do the Gospels Teach That Jesus Is God?—The Beginning of Mark’s Gospel
After some research, I found out that there was a famous scholar named Bart Erman who criticized Christianity and argued that the Gospel of John was unreliable. “Jesus is, without a doubt, portrayed as divine in the Gospel of John,” Erman says. But since John was written sixty years after Jesus, it doesn’t have to be accurate. The belief that Jesus was God was invented after his death, in the decades between Jesus and the writing of the Gospel of John. That’s why it’s not found in the other gospels. If Jesus himself had claimed to be God, why would we have to wait until the fourth gospel to hear about it? That Jesus became divine after his death was something we as Muslims believed, and I found Islamic websites that regularly quoted Erman. I decided to take his approach, arguing that if Jesus really did claim to be God, we would have taught about his divinity in the first of the four gospels, Mark. So I decided to show David that Mark presented Jesus as just a man, not God, and I immersed myself in the Gospel of Mark. The more I learned about Mark, the more I realized that it was a very Jewish gospel, written with the Old Testament in mind. The book refers to Jewish sources more than seventy times, with a strong preference for the book of Isaiah, and never once does it explicitly refer to a Greco-Roman source. When I read Mark through that lens, with Hebrew glasses, I realized that Armen was sorely mistaken. Not only does Mark present Jesus as God, but the main point of Mark’s Gospel is that Jesus is Jehovah. Mark begins by quoting a passage from the Old Testament: Isaiah 40:3-5. In that passage, “a voice of one crying in the wilderness, ‘Prepare the way of the Lord, make straight in the desert a highway for our God… Then the glory of the Lord will appear.’” So Isaiah prophesies that Jehovah, the God of Israel, will appear and a voice in the wilderness will announce his coming. Mark tells us in 1:4 that John the Baptist is that voice in the wilderness and that the one whom John announced his coming was Jesus. In other words, Mark equates Jehovah with Jesus, saying: We have been waiting for a man to announce the coming of Jehovah, our God. John the Baptist is that man, and he announced the coming of Jesus. In fact, Mark combines his reference to Isaiah 40:3-5 with Malachi 3:1, where the text explicitly states that the messenger (again John the Baptist) will appear in the presence of God himself in his temple. As Isaiah alludes, he equates God with Jesus. For added emphasis, Malachi ends a few verses later with the statement that if the Israelites do not accept the messenger, God himself will come. Thus, Mark, at the very beginning of his Gospel, uses numerous Old Testament sources to equate Jehovah with Jesus. To the Jewish reader, Mark’s introduction is very similar to John’s: he declares that Jesus is God himself. Mark continues in 2:3-10, telling us that Jesus forgave the sins of the paralyzed man. The scribes on the scene thought to themselves, “He is blaspheming. Who but God can forgive sins?” To the Jews, blasphemy is an accusation that someone disrespects him, usually by saying the name Jehovah or claiming divine status for themselves. Clearly, Jesus did not blaspheme God here, nor did he utter the divine name. Their accusation of blasphemy can only mean that Jesus considered himself God by claiming the divine privilege of forgiving sins. In response, far from denying that he claimed to be God, Jesus demonstrated his power to forgive sins by healing the paralytic. This not only demonstrated his spiritual authority, but it also reminded the scribes, who knew the Hebrew Scriptures well, of Psalm 103:2-3, which says: “Bless the Lord, O my soul, and forget not all his benefits; for he has forgiven all your sins.” "He will forgive you, and heal all your diseases!" When the scribes accuse Jesus of claiming to be God, instead of denying it, he goes even further by healing a paralytic, thereby doing something that only Jehovah in the Psalms does. Later in the same chapter, Jesus says, referring to himself, “The Son of Man is Lord even of the Sabbath” (Mark 2:28). Until we know the Old Testament well, it is easy to miss the fact that the Sabbath is the fourth of the Ten Commandments (Exodus 20:8). When Jesus refers to himself as Lord of the Sabbath, he is claiming lordship over the Ten Commandments, even though there is only one Lord: Jehovah. In Mark 4:35-41, we find the distressed disciples in the midst of a storm with waves so high on the water that they are breaking the boat and starting to fill it. In the midst of the trouble, they call out to Jesus. Jesus He rebukes the wind and says to the waves, “Peace! Be still!” Then the wind ceases, and there is a great calm (v. 39). The disciples ask themselves in amazement, “Who then is this, that even the wind and the sea obey him?” (v. 41). Now, we must understand that Mark expects us to answer these rhetorical questions by turning to the Old Testament. In Psalm 107:25-30, men are so dangerous in a stormy sea that their courage melts and their reason fails. “Then they cry to the Lord in their trouble, and he brings them out of their distress. He makes the storm calm and still, and the waves of the sea are still” (vv. 28-29).
So in the Old Testament, when people are at sea in a storm and are afraid of death, they call upon the Lord to calm the storms and still the waves. In Mark, when the disciples are at sea in a storm and are afraid of death, they call upon Jesus to calm the storms and still the waves. Mark again identifies Jesus with Jehovah. In another seafaring passage, Mark 6:45-52, the disciples are struggling to row against the wind. In the midst of the stormy waves, Jesus walks toward them on the water. For those who know the Old Testament, the allusion is clear: in Job 9:8, when Job speaks of Jehovah, he says: “He stretches out the heavens by himself, and treads on the waves of the sea.” What Job says only Jehovah can do, Mark shows Jesus to do. After discussing the highlights of Mark 1-6, we see that Mark’s effort is clear: He portrays Jesus as Jehovah. But despite the clarity and numerous allusions, I was still not convinced. What convinced me that Mark portrays Jesus as Jehovah was the climax of the gospel—the trial of Jesus.
Do the Gospels Teach That Jesus Is God?—The Climax of Mark
In Mark 14:55-64, Jesus is brought before the high priest and the religious leaders. Those who have brought Jesus to this trial have been seeking his destruction since the beginning of his ministry (3:6). They hope to incriminate him through his words against the temple, but without sufficient witnesses or a solid charge against him, the trial is underway (14:55-59). The high priest then stands up and demands that Jesus tell them who he is. It seems that the high priest hopes that Jesus can be incriminated through his claims of identity. When Jesus answers, he gives the religious leaders more than they had hoped. Jesus’ words are: “I am, and you will see the Son of Man sitting at the right hand of Power and coming with the clouds of heaven.” If we do not know the Old Testament, the meaning of his words will not be clear to us, but to the Jewish religious scholars, it was so clear that they condemned him to death for blasphemy. What exactly did Jesus say? In Mark 14:62, Jesus makes a double reference to the Old Testament, claiming Jehovah’s privileges and position for himself. The first reference is to Daniel. Jesus quotes from Daniel 7:13-14, an apocalyptic vision of the prophet Daniel, which says: “I was watching in the night visions, and behold, one like the Son of Man was coming with the clouds of heaven. And he came to the Ancient of Days, and they brought him before him. And there was given him dominion and glory and a kingdom, that all peoples, nations and languages should serve him. His dominion is an everlasting dominion that will not pass away, and his kingdom will not be destroyed.” In this passage from Daniel, a being who appears to be human (one like a son of man) approaches God. Although he appears human, his entrance is on the clouds—the entrance reserved in the Old Testament for Jehovah. Then the one who is like the Son of Man is given dominion, glory, and an eternal kingdom, although it is assumed that only God will have dominion and glory in the eternal kingdom. Finally, the passage says that all people will serve the Son of Man, but the word serve, whether in Aramaic or Greek, always indicates service to God. Thus, Daniel 7 presents the Son of Man riding on the clouds, as only Jehovah can. Then he receives dominion and eternal glory over his kingdom, as only Jehovah has. There all people will serve him with a divine service, as only Jehovah deserves. The Son of Man in Daniel 7 is a divine human son. Throughout Mark’s Gospel, beginning at 2:10, Jesus calls himself “the Son of Man,” although he never explicitly defines what he means by the term. In Mark 14:62, the climax of the Gospel, Jesus finally reveals to everyone who he is by quoting Daniel 7:13-14: He is the Son of Man in Daniel 7. He is Jehovah. But claiming the title “Son of Man” was not his only act of blasphemy against the religious scholars. Jesus also says that he has the right to sit on God’s throne, as if to remove all doubt. When he says that they will see the Son of Man “sitting at the right hand of the Mighty One,” he is referring to Psalm 110:1, which says: “The Lord said to my Lord, ‘Sit at my right hand until I make your enemies your footstool.’” Sitting at the right hand of God was a right that no one had dared to claim or ascribe to anyone else up to this point in the history of Second Temple Judaism. It entailed sitting on the throne of God and was equal to it. It claimed to be the heir of God, one who shares in the sovereignty with God. According to one scholar of the Psalms, “sitting at the right hand of God,”… has a very specific meaning: “The king shares in the rule. In this position of honor, in the structure of the power of God, he participates in the strength of Jehovah in battle and victory.” After learning all this, I understood why the religious scholars wanted to crucify Jesus for blasphemy. When Jesus claimed from Daniel 7 that he was the Son of Man and from Psalm 110 that he was the Lord of David, “both claims imply divine position, authority, and power.” In answer to the question, Who are you? Jesus’ response is essentially: “I am the one who deserves eternal worship from all humanity in my kingdom, where I will sit on the throne of God. I am Jehovah.” After reading Mark through the lens of Jewish scripture, I could no longer avoid the obvious. From introduction to climax, Mark’s Gospel is a statement of Jesus’ divinity. The first biography of Jesus ever written is designed to teach that Jesus is Jehovah.
Was Jesus God before the Gospel?
Although I was shocked to find the divinity of Jesus even in the earliest Gospels, that was only because I was looking for this teaching as a Muslim, and Muslims tend to focus on the Gospels. If I had paid more attention to the chronology of the New Testament, I would not have been surprised. The vast majority of scholars believe that Christians taught the deity of Jesus before Mark in our earliest Christian writings: the letters of Paul. In 1 Corinthians 8:6, Paul divides the Jewish Shema into two parts. The Jewish declaration of one God is presented as two persons, God the Father and the Lord Jesus. Also, in Romans 9:5, Paul says that Jesus is “God over all, to whom be thanks forever.” But the most interesting of these is certainly Philippians 2:
Let this mind be in you, which was also in Christ Jesus: Who, being in the form of God, thought it not robbery to be equal with God: but made himself of no reputation, taking the form of a servant, and being made in the likeness of men. And being found in fashion as a man, he humbled himself, and became obedient unto death, even the death of the cross. Therefore God also highly exalted him, and gave him the name which is above every name, that at the name of Jesus every knee should bow, of those in heaven, and of those on earth, and of those under the earth, and every tongue confess that Jesus Christ is Lord, to the glory of God the Father. — Verses 5-11
This piece, Carmen Christi, is called the “Hymn of Christ,” and its Christology is one of the highest that can be found. At the beginning of the text, it predates Jesus’ existence in the form of God, his ability to decide and act before the incarnation, and even his own hand in being born on earth. Thus the beginning of Carmen Christi makes it clear that Jesus is God incarnate. It then describes the future, when every knee will bow and every tongue will confess to the Lordship of Jesus. The type of Lordship of Jesus is the Lordship of Jehovah, for these are the words that Jehovah uses of himself in one of the most powerful monotheistic verses of the Old Testament: “I am God, and there is no God besides me... Every knee shall bow to me, and to me every tongue shall swear allegiance” (Isaiah 45:22-23). Once again, we find the first passages of the New Testament replacing Jesus with God. But how early is this passage, and is Paul really the author? The scholarly consensus is that this passage is not something Paul himself wrote, but rather that he quoted it. Beginning largely with the work of Ernest Lemayer in 1928, scholars have argued that this is a hymn that uses non-Pauline vocabulary and grammatical structure. The vocabulary in parts of this hymn is so strange that it is often found nowhere else in the New Testament. Not a single word is found anywhere else in the Greek language. Lemayer concluded that the hymn was written in Greek by a man other than Paul, whose native language was Semitic. Since then, others have agreed, and some have even argued that the hymn was originally written in Aramaic and later translated into Greek. These and other reasons combine to lead scholars to conclude that the hymn was composed in the late 1300s. So not only Paul, but the pre-New Testament church, perhaps as early as the Aramaic-speaking church, attributed the identity of Yahweh to Jesus.
Conclusion of the Positive Case for the Deity of Jesus
As I finished my research on the divinity of Jesus, I realized that every layer of Christian doctrine presents Jesus as divine. It cannot be argued that the divinity of Jesus was a late invention, a development of Christology. Not only does the Gospel of John present Jesus as divine, but even the Gospel of Mark and the writings of Paul present Jesus as Jehovah. The earliest evidence, probably from the same decade as Jesus’ crucifixion, identifies Jesus with Jehovah. For the early Christians, Jesus is more than a prophet, more than a Messiah, and more than a divine person. He is Jehovah himself.



Chapter 30
Islamic answer
Did Jesus really say "I am God"?

When I learned to read the New Testament in light of the Old Testament, I was able to see the divinity of Jesus clearly in its pages, but it took me years to get to that point as a Muslim. Although the Quran affirms that both the Torah and the Gospel are inspired, Muslims are not encouraged to know each in detail, so rather than reading the Bible on its own terms, we read it through the lens of the Quran and Islamic teachings. That is why, until I rejected the divinity of Christ, I was not only prone to it, highlighting verses that seemed to challenge the doctrine, but I also expected Jesus to say certain things and speak in certain ways if he truly claimed to be God. These two challenges were my main arguments against Christians: there are many verses in the Bible that seem to deny the divinity of Jesus, and Jesus never clearly says, “I am God.” These were collectively my most compelling reasons for doubting Jesus' claim to be God, and they are the same arguments used by most Muslims today.
Verses that deny the divinity of Jesus
Thanks to the work of Ahmed Deedat and other Muslim apologists, there are many verses from the Gospels that Muslims readily use to argue that Jesus explicitly denies divinity. One of my favorites was John 17:3 because it sounded very Islamic in its declaration: “And this is eternal life, that they might know you, the only true God, and Jesus Christ, whom you have sent.” Like the testimony, this verse also declares the existence of one God and the messenger whom he has sent. Obviously, the messenger is inferior and separate from the sender. As far as I was concerned, this verse showed that Jesus’ teachings were very similar to Islam, and that he denied being God and said that the Father was the “only true God.” Another verse that I often used because of its sheer clarity was John 14:28: “The Father is greater than I.” How can Jesus be God if the Father is greater than him? This shows that Jesus is both separate from and inferior to God. Another verse to a similar effect is Mark 10:18: “Why do you call me good? No one is good except one, God alone.” Jesus again distinguishes himself from God and makes it clear that he is inferior. As we as Muslims would expect, there are texts in the Gospels that show Jesus praying to God (Mark 1:35) and that people should worship only God (Matthew 4:10). Mark 14:36 ​​seems to show Jesus praying to the Father in despair, saying, “Abba, Father, all things are possible for you. Take this cup away from me; nevertheless, not as I will, but as you will.” Not only does Jesus pray to God, but he seems to emphasize that all things are possible for the Father, not for Jesus, and that Jesus is completely dependent on Him. Regarding this dependence, Jesus says that he can do nothing apart from God: “Amen, amen, I say to you, the Son can do nothing of himself, but what he sees the Father doing” (John 5:19). His inability to do anything on his own is revealed again in Mark 6:5, when he returns to Nazareth and “could not do any mighty work there.” Indeed, Jesus could not even prophesy without God’s intervention, for he said: “No one knows that day or hour except the Father, not even the angels in heaven, nor the Son” (Mark 13:32). We have repeatedly argued that Jesus is portrayed as a human being and that the term “Son of God” does not imply divine office. Adam (Luke 3:38), David (Psalm 2:7), Solomon (1 Chronicles 28:6), and even unnamed strangers (Genesis 6:2) were called “sons of God.” In fact, the Bible taught that even we can become children of God (Romans 8:14, Galatians 3:26). Therefore, Jesus was “Son of God” in the same human sense. Finally, Jesus must have been just a human being, for He ate (John 21:12), cried (John 11:35), slept (Mark 4:38), was hungry (Matthew 4:2), was thirsty (John 4:7), and even got tired (John 4:6). Can God really get hungry, thirsty, and tired? Of course God cannot, and therefore Jesus is not God. Also, he called himself a prophet (Luke 4:24), not God. The issue is resolved when we consider his preferred term for himself: “Son of Man.” By using this title, Jesus emphasized that he was only a man, not God.

Jesus never explicitly says, "I am God."
In addition to pointing out verses that seemed to deny Jesus’ divinity and establish his humanity, I often challenged Christians by pointing out that he never actually said, “I am God.” If Jesus were God, why didn’t he just come out and say it? God certainly boldly declared his divinity in the Old Testament with verses like “I am God, and besides me there is no other” (Isaiah 45:5). Christians argue that Jesus is the same God who makes the bold declaration in Isaiah 45. In fact, there seems to have been a specific situation where some Jews interpreted Jesus to mean that he was God, but Jesus dispels this misinterpretation (John 10:33-36). The Jews said, “You are making yourself God,” and Jesus replied, “Is it not written in your Torah, ‘I said, you are gods’?… Why then do you accuse me of blasphemy because I said, ‘I am the Son of God?’” When people think that Jesus is claiming to be God, he tries to clarify the situation by explaining that many people in the Old Testament were called “gods” and therefore it is not wrong for Jesus to call himself the “Son of God.” If he really was God, why didn’t he say in that example, “Yes, I claim to be God?” Why would he never say that? Clearly, he did not claim to be God.
Claims of Divinity and the Historical Jesus
These were the initial arguments I presented against the divinity of Jesus, arguments that I had been taught by the Muslim community, and I was sure that they were powerful. Later, as I progressed in my understanding of the response to these arguments, I presented another challenge that was more subtle and complex: Even if it could be shown in the Gospels that Jesus claimed to be God, what reason do we have to think that this refers to the real Jesus? In other words, even if John portrays a divine Jesus, can we really think that Jesus claimed to be God himself? The same is true of the earlier New Testament writings, such as Mark and Paul. Even if they wanted to show that Jesus claimed to be God, perhaps they invented it? Given the evidence, and given the concept itself, the belief that Jesus claimed to be God is simply ridiculous.

Conclusion of the Muslim response
As Muslims, this was an area where we were very certain: Jesus never claimed to be God. He never claimed to be, and there are many verses where he denies that and teaches that there is only one God. There is no God but One, and Jesus is only His messenger.











Chapter 31
Assessing the Islamic response
Let the context speak.

Some verses presented in isolation challenge the belief in the divinity of Jesus, but here’s the problem: verses are not meant to be taken in isolation. More than the interpretation of the Quran, verses in the Bible depend on their context for proper interpretation. Paying attention to the context of the verses and looking more closely at the doctrine of the divinity of Jesus will resolve many of the challenges Muslims face. To address the whole answer, we will also consider Mark’s messianic mystery and the possibility that Jesus’ claim to be God is rooted in Jesus himself.
Considering the context of the text
When I was a Muslim, I used many verses from the Gospel of John to challenge the divinity of Jesus, such as “The Father is greater than I” and “The Son can do nothing of himself.” As I became more familiar with the Gospel, I began to see a fundamental problem: John introduced his Gospel by declaring that Jesus was God, as we discussed in chapter 30, and he ended his Gospel with the climactic statement of one of the disciples that Jesus was God (John 20:28). In between, there were many verses that glorified Jesus and gave him honor only for God’s sake. This is why even skeptical scholars like Bart Ehrman say that Jesus is portrayed as God in the Gospel of John. How could I use verses from the Gospel of John to deny the divinity of Jesus when that Gospel as a whole definitely declared that Jesus was God? It would be dishonest to take verses out of context to suit my purposes, rather than see what they really say. It was this realization that led me to change my approach to the Bible. Instead of looking for verses in the text that I could use to support my Islamic positions, I began to read each verse carefully for the meaning it was trying to convey. This meant understanding the verses in light of each other and putting the pieces of the puzzle together. How can the Gospel of John call Jesus Lord and God (John 20:28) and say that the world was created through him (John 1:3), while also saying that “the Father is greater than Jesus” (John 14:28) and that Jesus can do nothing except the Father (John 5:19)? A proper understanding of the Gospel of John must explain all of these verses, not just some of them. The way to account for them, the way the Christians of Nicea and Chalcedon did for them, is to know that Jesus is God, the Father is God, the two are not the same, but there is only one God. In other words, the only way to explain the teachings of the Gospel of John is through a monotheistic model with multiple persons: a Trinitarian model. In this model, the Father is greater than Jesus and Jesus does nothing outside the will of the Father, but the Father and Jesus are both God. A pertinent question that Muslims often ask is: “How can Jesus be God if the Father is greater than him?” The question is easy to answer with an illustration. When I compare myself to the President of the United States, I do not hesitate to say that the President is greater than me. He is in charge of the entire nation and one of the most powerful men in the world, while I am just an ordinary citizen. So the President is greater than me, much greater. But we are both equally human. The President is in his essence just a man, just as I am, and in that sense we are equal. So when I say, “The President is greater than me,” I am referring to his office, not his essence. In position, he is greater than me. In essence, we are equal. Likewise, when Jesus says, “The Father is greater than I,” that does not mean that Jesus is not God. The Father has a different role, a higher position than Jesus, but that does not mean that the Father is greater in his essence. Both are equal in principle. Both are God.
Reconsidering the Doctrine of the Divinity of Jesus and the Trinity
A proper understanding of the doctrine of Jesus’ divinity resolves many Islamic arguments, even those that use the texts of Mark, Matthew, and Luke. The fact that Jesus was hungry and thirsty does not challenge Jesus’ divinity, because Christians believe in the ultimate unity we discussed in Chapter 10—that Jesus has a divine nature and a human nature, meaning that he was truly human and truly hungry. God can indeed be a prophet. Since God can come into the world according to Judeo-Christian teachings, he can have a human nature and be both God and man. There is nothing reciprocal about his humanity and his divinity. I remember resisting this argument when I first encountered it as a Muslim. Surely, to be God is to be unlimited, while to be human is to be limited. How can someone be both limited and unlimited at the same time? For example, God knows everything, and Jesus did not know everything (Mark 13:32). How can Jesus be God? The Christian answer, found in Philippians 2, is that God voluntarily limited the expression of his divinity when he became man. Yes, theoretically Jesus could have known everything while He was on earth, but He did not do so because “who, being in the form of God, did not consider equality with God something to be grasped, but emptied Himself, taking the form of a servant, and being made in the likeness of men.” (Philippians 2:6-7) He voluntarily limited Himself and became a true man so that as a man He could atone for the sins of man. That is why He was a true man. Jesus was born. He could grow in wisdom and stature. He was dependent on the Father and therefore could pray to the Father. This is not “God praying to Himself,” as some claim, because the Son is not the Father. There are two people here, two I’s. Although there are many verses that I have used to challenge the divinity of Jesus as a Muslim, these two explanations resolve almost all of them: the context of the verses and understanding the doctrine of the hypostatic union.
Why didn't Jesus say, "I am God"?
Understanding the Messianic Mystery
Muslims ask a good question when they ask why Jesus did not boldly and openly declare his divinity, but a good answer is readily available: he did not want to immediately declare his identity. This is clearly stated at the beginning of Mark’s Gospel. When Jesus was casting out demons, “he did not permit the demons to speak, because they knew who he was” (Mark 1:34). Mark is clear that Jesus wanted to keep his identity a secret for a time. There were several reasons for this. When people came to learn about Jesus, they gathered around him so much that he could no longer go out into the cities (Mark 1:45). Also, when the Pharisees and Herodians began to see what Jesus was doing and claiming, they began to plot how they could kill him (Mark 3:6), but Jesus did not want to be killed until the right time. When Jesus and the disciples discussed this, he explained to them why he wanted to keep his identity a secret: “For the Son of Man must suffer many things and be rejected by the elders and chief priests and scribes, and be killed, and after three days rise again” (8:31). So Jesus did not want to publicly announce his identity. This is known among scholars as the “messianic mystery.” When the days of his death and ascension drew near, he went straight to Jerusalem (Luke 9:51). It was then that he was arrested and taken to the religious teachers to be questioned about his identity. As a Muslim, I did not accept this until I noticed something amazing. Both Muslims and Christians agree that Jesus is the Messiah, but Jesus publicly declared that he was the Messiah only once in the Gospels. That is at one point in his trial before the religious teachers, the same passage where Jesus claims to be God.
To reiterate, the one time in the Gospels that Jesus publicly claimed to be the Messiah was the same time that he publicly claimed to be God. Since Muslims believe that Jesus Christ is the Messiah, even if he publicly declared it only once, we cannot expect him to declare his divinity more or more boldly. He was not in the business of declaring his identity over and over again. He chose to wait for the right moment. Also, although Jesus did not often declare his identity publicly, the Gospels inform their readers of his identity through the narrative of the text. For example, Mark 1:1 identifies Jesus as the Son of God; Mark 1:11 shows God Himself as Jesus’ Son. Verse 3:11 shows a demon claiming that Jesus is the Son of God. By showing spirit beings proclaiming Jesus’ Sonship, Mark is able to let his readers know that Jesus is not a mere mortal, allowing readers to share the secret even before Jesus’ public announcement at the trial. As discussed in the previous chapter, Mark uses other devices to let the perceptive reader know that Jesus is more than human. He uses arguments, such as the public forgiveness of the paralyzed man’s sin (2:5). He uses claims of sovereignty, such as his sovereignty over one of the Ten Commandments (2:28). He shows Jesus performing miracles that only God should be able to do, such as calming the seas (4:38-39) and walking on water (6:48). Throughout his gospel, Mark prepares the reader for the moment when the messianic secret will be revealed, when Jesus will tell everyone who he really is and will tie all his words and actions together. This makes 14:62, the climax and revelation of God, all the more powerful and important to understand. It is there that Jesus claims from Psalm 110:1 that the divine Son of Man of Daniel 7 and the one who sits on God’s throne are both Jesus’ most powerful declarations of divinity in the Gospels, and also anchor our confidence that Jesus actually used these terms.
Assessing historical accuracy
Historically, what is strong about Jesus’ claims against religious scholars is that they pass the most rigorous scientific standards for historical accuracy. The title “Son of Man” is used more than eighty times in the four Gospels, almost always by Jesus himself, on multiple occasions. Scholars are certain that Jesus actually used the term for himself, because there was no widespread expectation that Christ would be the Son of Man. Moreover, early Christians did not actually refer to Jesus as the Son of Man. Why do all four Gospels show Jesus calling himself the Son of Man, when no one expected Christ to speak in this way and when people later did not actually refer to him in these terms? The most plausible reason is that he did in fact use the title. Thus, it passes the most rigorous standard of historical scrutiny, the standard of dissimilarity. That Jesus called himself the Son of Man is virtually certain. Likewise, there can be no doubt that Jesus, the Lord of David, is considered to be the one who sits on the throne of God in the early history of Christianity. This is the most common Old Testament phrase in the New Testament, deeply rooted in the Christian conscience. He is the one who sits at the right hand of power and is worthy to rule the world with the Father. So we can be sure that Jesus’ boldest declaration of his identity, when he publicly declared that he was both Christ and God, is the very words that Jesus himself actually uttered.
Conclusion of our assessment of the Muslim response
The fact is that not only the Gospel of John, but all the Gospels teach that Jesus is God. Although there are some verses in the Gospels that might challenge the divinity of Jesus, understanding them in their context and understanding the Christian doctrine of the divinity of Jesus resolves them. Even Erman, the skeptic and favorite Muslim scholar, has now changed his mind and says: “Of course, the idea that Jesus is God is not a modern invention. As I will show in my discussion, it was the view of the early Christians immediately after Jesus’ death.” The best explanation for this immediate belief that Jesus is God, as the evidence shows, is that Jesus himself claimed to be God.


Chapter 32
Conclusion
Jesus claimed to be God.

The earliest Christian records are unanimous: Jesus is God. All four Gospels teach that Jesus is divine, and even before they were written, Christians had established the incarnation of God as the core of their faith. This was not a teaching that evolved over time, but one that existed at the beginning of the church and was rooted in Jesus’ proclamation. When we study the life of Jesus through the lens of first-century Judaism, including the Old Testament accounts of God’s meeting with man and the expectation of His return, it makes sense. The common Islamic response, that there are verses that contradict the teaching of Jesus’ divinity, usually ignores the context of those verses and at other times misunderstands the messianic mystery or the doctrine of the hypostatic union. When I studied the Gospels as a Muslim, I was shocked to discover these truths. Having always believed that the doctrine of Jesus’ divinity was invented decades, not centuries, after Jesus’ death, I realized that the Islamic explanation for Christian beliefs did not work. The early Christians, including the disciples themselves, believed that Jesus was God. How could the disciples have come to this conclusion, especially given the Jewish emphasis on monotheism and the worship of only one God? The best conclusion is that Jesus himself claimed to be God. The Gospels tell the truth. As a Muslim, my mind balked at this, but from the perspective of an objective researcher, I had to admit that this was the best explanation for the evidence. Putting together the components of the Christian case, I had little room for objection as a Muslim. The historical evidence was so inconsistent with Islamic teachings that I had no basis for standing.


Midway's summary to the second question
Evaluating Christianity and the Efforts of Islam
To consider the Christian origin

After studying the historical origins of the Christian faith, I came to this conclusion: Jesus’ death on the cross is as certain as anything historical. That he rose from the dead is by far the best explanation for the events surrounding his death. And that Jesus claimed to be God is the best explanation for the immediate declaration of Jesus’ divinity. Put all this together, the historical evidence for Christianity is very strong: Jesus claimed to be God, and he proved it by rising from the dead. The Christian case is strong. Despite my strong desire to believe in Islam, I had to admit that history favors the Christian claims and, even more reluctantly, challenges Islamic teachings. To believe the Quran, when it says that Jesus did not claim to be God, we must ignore the best explanation of early Christian beliefs. And to believe the Quran’s teachings about Jesus’ death, we must reject all historical evidence. So the record of Christian origins testifies against Islam. But the evidence is actually even more problematic for Islam than it first appears. Far more problematic is that Islamic teachings about Jesus are completely inconsistent with history.
The Absolute Incompatibility of Islam with the Identity of Jesus
For Islamic teachings to be true, it is not enough for Jesus to simply deny God. Jesus must declare that he is merely a human prophet. But the oldest records are decidedly against Islam on this point. Even a cursory glance at the Gospels shows us that Jesus sees himself as greater than a mere man. Of course, as we have seen before, Jesus sees himself as able to forgive sins, receive worship, heal people at will, demand the respect that is due God, hear and answer prayers, redeem humanity by his death, and even exist before Abraham was born. But Jesus also praised those who called him God (John 20:28), claimed to be the king of another realm (John 18:36-37), to come down from heaven (John 3:13), and to be the judge on Judgment Day (John 5:22-23). ​​Such teachings are not found only in the Gospel of John. The synoptic gospels agree that Jesus is the king of an eternal kingdom (Matthew 25:34), that he is the one who will judge humanity (Matthew 25:32), that he has the authority to save people (Luke 23:43), that he will take those he has chosen to heaven (Mark 13:27), and that his return to earth will be like a master returning to his own house (Mark 13:35). Of course, all of this is within the context of Jesus’ claim to be the heir to God’s throne of Psalm 110, seated on God’s throne, and as the divine Son of Man of Daniel 7, worshipped by all people in his eternal kingdom. These are the teachings of the Gospels, the first biographies of Jesus’ life, compiled during the lifetimes of Jesus’ disciples. There is absolutely no early Christian record of a merely human Jesus. All the evidence indicates that Jesus’ followers uniformly believed that he was divine, certainly more than a mere human. How can the Islamic model explain this? Why did Jesus’ followers preach a superhuman Jesus, and indeed God himself? Without rejecting history, there is no other explanation. Islam asks us to believe that Jesus was so incapable as a teacher and prophet that he failed to impress this simplest truth on his followers: that he was merely human. Given that the central proclamation of Islam is monotheism, this means that Jesus was a huge failure. In fact, he was worse than a complete failure, because he left his disciples with the exact opposite of monotheism. Can I really conclude that Christ was woefully inadequate? Of course not, but if we are to take the historical evidence seriously, that is what Islam wants us to believe. The record of Jesus’ identity is not only slightly inconsistent with Islamic teachings. The record of Jesus’ identity is completely incompatible with Islam. If Jesus had really taught monotheism, he would have been a completely unworthy Christian, worse than a disgraceful failure.
The absolute incompatibility of Islam with the original proclamation of Christianity
Similarly, Islam teaches that the disciples were godly men, but the entire evidence is that the early church was proclaiming the death and resurrection of Jesus—something that Islam denies. How could I explain this discrepancy as a Muslim? The only option that the historical evidence could justify was that the disciples simply got the facts wrong. But upon examination, this created a serious problem for my Islamic belief. If God saved Jesus from the cross when it appeared that Jesus was dead, as most Muslims believe, then God is responsible for the disciples’ proclamation of Jesus’ death and resurrection. Thus, God started Christianity, a false religion that has turned billions away from Islam. Worse still, Christians believe that Jesus is God because of their belief in his resurrection, yet the Quran tells us that people who believe that Jesus is God will go to hell (5:72). Can I really believe that God deceived the disciples just to save Jesus from the cross and allowed them to proclaim that Jesus was risen, thus sending billions of people to hell? Again, there is absolutely no record of any disciple preaching anything other than the death and resurrection of Jesus. Why didn’t God tell them that Jesus did not die on the cross and did not rise from the dead? God could easily have stopped them from preaching the death and resurrection of Jesus. But history is undeniable: the foundation of the disciples’ preaching was the proclamation of Jesus’ resurrection. So, if it is true that God saved Jesus from the cross, then God’s deception is responsible for the establishment of the Christian church and the billions of people who practice polytheism, an unforgivable sin. Can I really conclude that God made such a huge mistake? Of course not, but if we are to take the historical evidence seriously, that is what Islam wants us to believe. The records of the early proclamation of Christianity are not only slightly inconsistent with Islamic teachings. The records of the early proclamation of Christianity are absolutely inconsistent with Islam. If God saved Jesus from the cross and did not warn the disciples, then He is a deceitful God who is responsible for the damnation of billions. For these reasons, if we are Muslims and take the historical evidence for the origins of Christianity seriously, we must conclude that Jesus was an unworthy Christian and that God is a deceitful God.
Islam's rejection of history
Of course, Muslims do not believe this, and neither did I. I chose the only remaining option: reject all historical evidence for the origins of Christianity, no matter how much there is, how extensive it is, and how well-founded it is. The historical record is incredibly extensive, including dozens of sources from dozens of writers—Christian, Jewish, and Greco-Roman. Could I really ignore everything and turn a blind eye to all the evidence in order to maintain my Islamic beliefs? I also knew something I could no longer ignore: scholars are almost unanimous that Jesus died on the cross, the early church believed that he rose from the dead, and early Christians believed that he was superhuman and even divine. Not just Christian scholars, but even skeptical scholars—virtually all of them—were on one side of the issue, contrary to Islamic teachings. As a Muslim, I had great respect for the scholars, but I now knew that their conclusions were contrary to my faith. People who spend their lives studying these issues are against Islam. As I thought about all this, my mind kept returning to the question I forced myself to ask: "Would an objective scholar discard all evidence? Would an objective scholar come to terms with Islam on these issues?" The answer was clear: of course not. Believing in Islam about the history of Christian origins would mean rejecting all circumstantial evidence, and no objective scholar would do that.
A tentative conclusion
After spending three years researching Christianity as a Muslim, I tentatively concluded that Christians have good historical reasons for believing in their faith. It was a shift in my mind that was completely inconsistent with what I had been taught as a Muslim, but it was inevitably true. And since the evidence also challenged my Islamic beliefs, I was forced to either ignore it all (which my mind could not do), become a Christian (which I could not even imagine), or believe that, despite how strong the evidence for Christianity was, the case for Islam must be stronger. The last option was the only viable option for me, so I found myself assuming that the case for Islam must be stronger than the case for Christianity. But my friend David would not let me act on such an assumption without providing evidence. That is why I set out to research Islam: to prove that the case for Islam is stronger than the case for Christianity. My first step was to gather evidence that Muhammad was a prophet of God. After spending some time preparing, David invited me to speak at a discussion group of people from different religious backgrounds that met monthly at Mike Likona's house. It was there that I made my positive case that Muhammad was a prophet of God.











Part 9
Is Muhammad a prophet of God?










Chapter 33
Positive case
Predicted pattern

I bear witness that Muhammad is the Messenger of God.” This is the declaration of a devout Muslim, proclaimed several times a day from every minaret before the daily prayers and recited in the heart of every Muslim during prayer. This declaration, “There is no god but Allah and Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah,” is enough to bring a person to the fold of Islam. There can be no doubt that the status of the Messenger of God, his prophethood, is the central pillar of the Islamic faith. Growing up as a Muslim, Muhammad’s prophethood was a given and an integral part of our world, and sharing Islam meant convincing hearts of him. That is why when my friend Dawood asked me to share Islam with a room full of non-Muslims, I chose to present and defend the Prophet of Islam. Sitting in Mike Likona’s living room, talking to Christians, Buddhists, atheists, and mystics, I brought up the prophethood of Muhammad. I gave three reasons: Muhammad’s life and character are proof of his prophethood. That Muhammad was prophesied in the Bible. And that Muhammad had a God-given insight into science.
The life and personality of Muhammad
I began by presenting a picture of Muhammad's life that had been passed down to me through the generations, telling everyone that Muhammad had a very gentle and humble childhood. His father died before he was born, his mother died shortly after, and he spent most of his childhood as an orphan. He became a trusted and respected young merchant, and eventually married a widow fifteen years his senior instead of a young girl. Muhammad was thus a very noble young man who, even before he received his prophetic call, was uninterested in worldly attractions. It was only with reluctance that he accepted the mantle of prophethood. In the pagan environment of Mecca, Muhammad boldly proclaimed monotheism without regard for his own personal welfare. He understood the plight of widows, orphans, and the poor, and fought for their rights. Despite his peaceful message, the Meccans' hatred of the monotheism message led to persecution. Despite being protected by his uncle, Muhammad was ridiculed and threatened, even having camel entrails thrown at him while he was prostrating in prayer. His followers, who had no protectors, did worse, even martyring themselves. The persecution increased over the years, and Mecca eventually imposed a ban on Muslims, resulting in the deaths of Muhammad's uncle and wife. When a plot to assassinate Muhammad was in the offing, he fled to Medina, where he finally found enough support to defend the fledgling Muslim people. From then on, Muhammad invited the tribes of Arabia to accept Islam, and many joined the Muslims because of the beauty of the message. All this time, the Meccans kept coming to Medina to destroy the Muslims, but Allah miraculously defended them. After ten years of fighting such defensive battles, Muhammad made a treaty with the Meccans that allowed him to travel to Mecca and visit the Kaaba. Unfortunately, the Meccans broke their treaty, so Muhammad marched on Mecca with ten thousand followers. The Meccans could not withstand the might of the Muslim armies, so they surrendered. Although these were the same people who had persecuted and killed the Muslims—the same people who had caused the death of Muhammad’s beloved uncle and first wife, and the same people who had repeatedly led the Muslims into battle at the cost of so many lives—Muhammad showed them mercy. By forgiving them all, the fame of Muhammad’s message spread throughout Arabia, and by the time Muhammad died two years later, the entire Arabian Peninsula had embraced Islam. Muhammad’s character was such: in addition to being a champion for women and orphans, he was a steadfast preacher of monotheism, a great leader, and a conqueror of kindness. His life and character testify that he was the Messenger of God.
Muhammad in the Bible
A more common argument for the convincing character of Muhammad among Muslims is that the prophet was prophesied in the Bible. The Quran teaches that Muhammad was prophesied in the Torah and the Gospel: “This messenger is not a learned man, but they find [his name] written with him in the Torah and the Gospel” (7.157). For this reason, Muslims are willing to use the Bible for verses to defend Muhammad’s prophethood. There are two such passages of Scripture that we use more than any other, usually quoting from the King James Version: Deuteronomy 18:18-19 and John 16:12-14.

Deuteronomy 18
In Deuteronomy 18:18-19, God says, “I will raise up for them a prophet like you from among their brothers, and I will put my words in his mouth, and he will tell them everything I command him. Whoever does not listen to my words that he speaks in my name, I myself will demand it of him.” In this passage, God addresses Moses and says that a prophet like Moses will come from among the Jewish brothers and will speak in the name of God. Muslims often argue that, even though Christians believe that this passage refers to Jesus, it is talking about Muhammad. Who is more like Moses: Jesus or Muhammad? Moses was born naturally, came with the law, led his people for many years, was a statesman as well as a prophet, had to lead his people out of oppression, married and had children, and finally died a natural death. None of this is true of Jesus, yet all of it is true of Muhammad. Moses is undoubtedly more like Muhammad than Jesus. Furthermore, the prophecy says that the prophet will be “from among their brothers,” that is, the Jewish brothers. The Jewish Israelites and their brothers are Ishmaelites. Therefore, the prophecy speaks of a prophet like Moses from the descendants of Ishmael. It must be Muhammad. Finally, the text says that the prophet “shall speak the words which he shall speak in my name.” will reveal. In the Quran, every Surah begins with “Bismillah.” Therefore, Muhammad spoke in the name of God more than anyone else, and every Surah of the Quran testifies to this. So I presented the common argument that through Moses in the Old Testament, God foretold the coming of Muhammad.
John 16
The Gospel of John, chapter 16, also speaks of a prophet who will come, I reasoned. It says: “I have yet many things to say to you, but you cannot bear them now. Howbeit when he, the Spirit of truth, is come, he will guide you into all the truth: for he shall not speak of himself; but whatsoever he shall hear, that shall he speak: and he will shew you things to come. He shall glorify me: for of that which is mine shall he take, and shew it unto you” (verses 12-14). Here Jesus speaks of a prophet, the Spirit of truth (verse 13), who will come and finish Jesus’ message. Who is this but Muhammad, the only prophet of a great religion to come after Jesus? As in the prophecy of Deuteronomy, this passage describes one who does not speak of himself, but speaks only of what he hears. The Quran is a revelation given to Muhammad and was not written by him in any way, so he only spoke what he heard, as prophecy says. Furthermore, John 16 says that the Spirit of truth will glorify Jesus. Unlike the Jews who said that Jesus was an illegitimate child, Muhammad declared that Jesus was truly born of a virgin, thereby glorifying him while others were not, fulfilling prophecy. To complete the discussion, the Greek of John 16:7 uses the word parakletos to describe this “Spirit of truth.” This word is very similar to the Greek word periklutos, which means “praised.” In Arabic, the word Muhammad means “praised.” Therefore, Jesus used the Greek word for Muhammad when he prophesied about the Spirit of truth. For all these reasons, we can be sure that through Jesus, God foretold the coming of Muhammad in the New Testament. These were just two prophecies that I selected that night from more than two dozen such prophecies that Muslims commonly argue establish the authority and prophethood of Muhammad.
Miraculous scientific knowledge
The last argument I presented to the audience was Muhammad’s amazing scientific knowledge. This argument is usually made in defense of the Quran, but I and many other Muslims made it in defense of Muhammad’s prophethood. Muhammad, while delivering the Quran and answering questions recorded in the hadiths, showed an insight into science that he could not possibly have known as a man in the seventh century. This can only mean that Allah had given Muhammad supernatural wisdom and that he was therefore a prophet of God. For example, in 23:12-14, the Quran describes the development of an embryo in stages. By describing the development of an egg from fertilization to differentiation, the Quran demonstrates a familiarity with a scientific field that was unknown in Muhammad’s day. It was not until modern medical advances that we find mankind discovering what had already been revealed in the Quran. Another common argument we used was that the Quran reveals miraculous geological facts about mountains. In 78. 6-7, the Quran describes mountains as "pegs," indicating that they have roots that extend beneath the surface of the earth. Only in modern times has science discovered the existence of such roots. Furthermore, the Quran teaches that these roots of mountains stabilize the earth, which the science of plate tectonics has only recently established. Since Muhammad was the one who received these amazing scientific teachings, he must have been a prophet of God.
Ending the positive case of Muhammad's prophethood
After presenting my argument to the discussion group for forty-five minutes, I felt confident that I had argued strongly for the prophethood of Muhammad. His life and message are intrinsic proof that he is a prophet of God. His coming was foretold by prophets before him, such as Moses and Jesus. And the advanced scientific knowledge that was revealed through him proves that he is a prophet of God. These are collectively compelling reasons to believe that Muhammad is a prophet and that Islam is the truth sent from God. But then questions arose, issues that I had not seen that challenged Muhammad’s prophethood.













Chapter 34
Answer
Don't forget the cross-references.

When I finished my talk on the historical Muhammad, the first question came from Mike. Since he had studied Islam for his debate with Shabbir Ali, he knew that there were holes in my presentation, evidence that I had not presented before concluding that Muhammad was a peaceful character. “Nabil,” he said, “there are other verses in the Quran, such as: ‘Kill the infidel wherever you find him.’ How do we know what you quoted is the most important?” Although I was very offended when people questioned Muhammad’s character, it was fair to ask. If I were to present Muhammad’s great character as evidence that he was a prophet of God, that would mean that I would have to allow for counter-evidence that might lead us to conclude that Muhammad was not a prophet of God. The same is true of the prophecies about Muhammad and Muhammad’s scientific knowledge. We need to weigh the possible counter-examples. When we consider the full body of evidence, is Muhammad's prophethood still the best outcome?
Critical consideration of the character of Muhammad
When we read about the life of Muhammad, there is no doubt that Muhammad taught many good things. This goes beyond simply declaring monotheism and submission to Allah. Muhammad taught people to feed the poor; to love others for the sake of Allah; to avoid stealing, adultery, and infanticide; to free slaves, to help the weak, and to serve those who cannot work for themselves. And much more. When considering the historical record of Muhammad’s life, one must conclude that he taught many moral and noble things. But before one can conclude that he was a prophet of God, one must consider the counter-evidence, and we will only consider the evidence that is found in the sources that most Muslims consider reliable: the Quran and the Hadith of Sahih Bukhari and Sahih Muslim. The best place to start is at the beginning. When he received his first revelation, Muhammad reacted in a very strange way: he committed suicide. “He intended to throw himself off the top of a high mountain several times, and every time he climbed a mountain peak to throw himself down,” an angel appeared to him and told him not to kill himself. Is this the behavior of a prophet? No prophet in the Old or New Testament committed suicide after seeing an angel. Muhammad’s initial encounter with a spiritual being is not at all like an encounter with an angel. According to the same hadith, this spiritual being was called a namus. Namus grabbed Muhammad and squeezed him so hard that he could not bear it. He squeezed Muhammad’s namus three times until Muhammad finally did as he was told. To the casual observer, this would seem like a violent episode, not like an encounter with an angel. Muhammad’s early ministry seems a little prophetic. When we consider whether he was a man of peace, we should note that although Muhammad’s ministry was filled with war, this in itself is not a reason to reject his divine mission. Joshua and others in the Old Testament were commanded to fight. But what is remarkable is how enthusiastically Muhammad embraced war. He said that war was literally the best thing in the world. Nothing is more rewarding for a Muslim than jihad, and it is better than praying without ceasing and fasting constantly. Muhammad taught that dying in war was so great that it was the only thing that would cause a person to leave Paradise. As for Muhammad’s treatment of his enemies, Muhammad sometimes cursed them and encouraged his subjects to compose insulting poetry. He once asked Allah to fill people’s homes with flames because Muslims were late in their daily prayers. At other times, Muhammad would send assassins to kill his enemies in their sleep and even to deceive and exploit them in order to assassinate them. He punished some of his enemies by cutting off their hands and feet, striking them in the eyes with hot irons, and licking dirt until they died. He led wars against unarmed towns. He allowed even women and children to be killed in night raids. On more than one occasion, Muhammad annihilated tribes by killing all their men and teenage boys while dividing their women and children into slaves. This is in stark contrast to the image of Muhammad, who reluctantly fought only in defensive battles. Nor does it appear that Muhammad fought only against those who attacked him. Muhammad said: “I have been commanded to fight people until they testify that there is no god but God and that Muhammad is the Messenger of God... Then they will save their lives and property from me.” Muhammad states in another hadith: “I will expel the Jews and Christians from the Arabian Peninsula and leave none but Muslims.” Based on these records, it cannot be denied that Muhammad fought against the people and wanted to expel them from Arabia based on their beliefs. They would save their lives and property from Muhammad only if they converted to Islam. How can we argue that Muhammad was so peaceful that it proved his prophetic status? In assessing Muhammad’s character, we have considered only two issues in any depth: the beginning of his ministry and his teachings on war. These alone are enough to question Muhammad’s character and prophethood, but if more is needed, skeptics usually bring up much more, such as concerns about his spiritual talent, his teachings on women, his disturbing theological teachings, his misunderstanding of Judaism and Christianity, his unfulfilled prophecies, his orders to perform pagan rituals, and his special privileges for himself. We will only briefly mention the first two. Regarding his spiritual talent and authority, apart from having contemplated suicide on several occasions, Muhammad said that he was a victim of black magic. He had delusions of doing things he had not done. He mistook demonic inspiration for divine inspiration and said that Satan had placed verses of the Quran in his heart which he then used in prayer. Regarding his assessment and treatment of women: Muhammad permitted prostitution through temporary marriage. When he was fifty-two years old, he married his nine-year-old bride, Aisha, who was still playing with dolls. Muhammad allowed his men to have sex with captive and slave women, without worrying about getting them pregnant or being sold. And Muhammad said that women would be the majority of the inhabitants of Hell because they were ungrateful to their husbands. The topics we have covered should be sufficient for our current topic. Although Muhammad may have taught many good things and at times been a kind man, given the overwhelming evidence of the opposite, we cannot conclude that his character was excellent enough to prove that he was a prophet of God.
Searching for Muhammad more accurately in the Bible
Although there are many suggested prophecies of Muhammad in the Bible, the prophecies of Deuteronomy 18 and John 16 are the ones we know to be the strongest and therefore most often predicted. However, a common problem in the way we approached them was that we tried to make the text conform to our expectations. Instead of understanding what these words meant and what they were intended to convey, we did our best to read them as prophecies about Muhammad. For example, when reading Deuteronomy 18, we never bothered to consider the Old Testament concept of a prophet, and always assumed only the Islamic concept. In the Old Testament, a prophet is anyone who prophesies because the Spirit of Yahweh rests on him. The Old Testament speaks of prophets (Judges 4:4) and entire groups of prophets at once (1 Samuel 10:5), and neither of these things fits the Muslim idea of prophecy. However, where our interpretation of the words became a real problem in Deuteronomy 18 was the way we interpreted “from among their brothers.” This phrase cannot mean from among the Ishmaelites. Whenever the Bible uses this phrase, it means “nations,” which always refers to close relatives or tribes. It never means a completely different group from a distant ancestor. You don’t need to be completely familiar with the use of this term in the Bible to conclude this, because the previous chapter defines it for us. In Deuteronomy 17:15, the Bible says, “You shall certainly set over you a king whom the LORD your God chooses. You shall set one from among your brothers as king over you; you may not set a foreigner over you, who is not one of your brothers.” So the text defines the word for us: a brother from among the Hebrews is a Hebrew. He could not be a foreigner, and the Ishmaelites were foreign. This text cannot be about Muhammad. Likewise, in John 16, we did everything we could to read Muhammad into the text, regardless of the counter-evidence that simply did not allow it. The Greek word was parakletos, not periklutos, no matter how much we wanted Jesus to use the Greek equivalent of Muhammad. And just as brothers were defined in the preceding verses of the Deuteronomy passage, parakletos was already defined in the same John passage. In 14:26, the text tells us that parakletos is the Holy Spirit. There is no room for speculation; this was a prophecy about the Holy Spirit living within Christians. It was not about the coming of any man, let alone a prophet. That is why the text says of the Spirit of truth: “He is the Spirit of truth, who gives life to the world.” He cannot receive him, because he neither sees him nor knows him; but you know him, because he dwells with you and will be in you...” (John 14:17). The disciples already knew the Spirit of truth because the Holy Spirit was with them and would soon be in them. How could this be Muhammad? Muhammad was not with them before and could not possibly have been in them. In context, we cannot conclude that the Spirit of truth is Muhammad from John 14-16. When we consider any of the so-called “Muhammad in the Bible” prophecies, it is the same. In most of them, the Bible does not predict the coming of anyone at all. In others, the entire text provides contradictory evidence that forbids us from drawing any conclusions about Muhammad. There is simply no convincing prophecy about Muhammad in the Bible.

Muhammad and science
The counter-evidence and context for the arguments that establish Muhammad’s prophethood are problematic, and so it is when we examine the argument from miraculous scientific knowledge, which Muhammad considered to be so advanced that it must have come from God. Consider, for example, Quranic embryology. Before we even look at the text of the Quran, we must consider what mankind had already discovered in order to properly frame what might be miraculous. About a thousand years before Muhammad, Aristotle published a treatise on embryology called On the Generation of Animals. This book is far more scientific and detailed than anything Muhammad suggests, as it is a complete book on the subject of embryology. In section 734a, Aristotle explicitly discusses embryonic development in stages. In section 745, he notes that the embryo is attached to the uterus by the umbilical cord. Galen, a Greek scientist who lived about five hundred years after Aristotle and five hundred years before Muhammad, also wrote a treatise on embryology, on the natural faculties. Agreeing that development occurs in stages, he says: “Now nature, in the first stage of the genesis of the animal, makes bones, cartilages, nerves, membranes, ligaments, veins, etc., and in this she uses a faculty which is generally called the terms, generative and alternative.” Both Aristotle and Galen had carefully defined terms and concepts, and they discussed in great detail the process of embryonic development. Now let us turn to the Quran, where we quote verse 23. 12-14: "And We created man from an extract of a drop of blood, then We placed him as a drop of sperm in a firm place, then We made the drop into a clot, then We shaped the clot into a lump, then We made the lump into bones, then We clothed the bones with flesh, then We brought him forth in another creation. Praise be to Allah, the Best of creators."
At the outset we must conclude that there is nothing miraculous here. It cannot be claimed that Muhammad did not know the details of embryology, when complete books were written by Aristotle a thousand years earlier and Galen five hundred years before that. Furthermore, the Quran uses weak terms and concepts that do not approach the scientific precision of Galen. Many Muslim explanations of this passage assume that "alaqah" and "madghah" have specific embryological references. And if they already believe that the Quran contains miraculous scientific knowledge, they may assume this, but they cannot assume this to prove that it does.
