The unrefined language makes this passage indecipherable, not miraculous. What can be gleaned from the initial phrase is the concept of development in stages, but this is certainly not miraculous, as it was known in Muhammad's time. Similarly, Aristotle had already described the attachment of an embryo to the uterus through the umbilical cord (i.e., a thing of the word). In the case of a thing of the word, women in Arabia, like women today, suffered miscarriages and saw the embryos that the Quran describes. Given the details, we can only guess what is meant by that part of the text. As far as we understand the text clearly, the only part of this Quranic verse that makes an explicit claim is that bones first grow and then are covered with flesh. Unfortunately, this is incorrect. Modern science has taught us that a single embryonic layer, the mesoderm, differentiates into bones and flesh at the same time. Therefore, the part of this passage that is clearly stated is scientifically incorrect, and the whole passage is much less than texts written a thousand years ago. It is simply not a miraculous passage. Much could be said about the seemingly miraculous insight into mountains, that they stabilize the earth and have roots, but it will be useful to simply point out the patterns. Here too, the scientific claim is false. Mountains do not provide stability in the earth’s crust. Instead, they are the result of tectonic instability. While the claim that mountains have roots beneath the earth’s surface is true in a sense, the Quran is certainly not the first book to make this claim. We see this claim at least three times in the Bible: Job 28:9, Psalm 18:7, and Jonah 2:6. As a Muslim, I believed that there were dozens of examples of miraculous scientific knowledge in the Quran, but when I examined them closely, I realized that each one was subject to at least one of three criticisms: First, the verses were made to state things they did not claim (such as the “Muhammad” prophecies in the Bible). Second, the science was actually known before Muhammad's time (such as embryonic development in stages). Or third, the science was incorrect (such as bone development before muscle). There are many examples of the third criticism that really nail the coffin of this argument. These incorrect statements are found in both the Quran and the hadith. For example, in the area of embryology alone, the Quran is incorrect about spermatogenesis, claiming that semen is produced between a man's spine and his ribs (86. 7). In the hadith we find a case that is very telling: A man asked Muhammad to prove his prophethood by answering questions correctly. One of the questions was why a child might resemble its father more than its mother, or vice versa. Muhammad replied that Gabriel had informed him of the answer to his question: the first parent to ejaculate during intercourse determined the child's appearance. It goes without saying that this is scientifically incorrect, since genetics determine the child's appearance. We shouldn’t blame a man in the seventh century for being unfamiliar with Mendelian genetics, but in this case, Muhammad was actually trying to prove with his scientific insight that he was a prophet of God, and he was very wrong. That bitter fact alone was enough to give me pause as a Muslim. There were many other times when Muhammad was wrong about his science, and examples of this can be found in the Quran as well as the Hadith. For example, apart from what we have already seen, the Quran says that the sun sets in a pool of muddy water in the west (18.86), and it assumes that the stars are meteors (67.5). In the Hadith apart from what we have already seen, Muhammad teaches that flies have diseases on one wing and antidotes on the other, that cumin cures all diseases, and that camel urine cures stomachaches. These are just a few examples of Muhammad’s many scientific inaccuracies.
Ending the response to the case of Muhammad's prophethood
It is worth noting that we have not attempted to refute Muhammad’s prophethood. We have considered the arguments used to prove Muhammad’s prophethood and have concluded that they are quite problematic. Although I and other Muslims have often argued that Muhammad should be followed because of his excellent character, I have not been able to prove that argument in the face of counter-evidence. Although Muhammad provided abundant moral teachings and at times displayed a kind and peaceful personality, there are many other accounts of Muhammad’s brutality and joy in war, his spiritual deficiencies, and his disturbing treatment of women, among other concerns. Also, there are no prophecies about Muhammad in the Bible. Reading the full text of the proposed passages and carefully considering their wording shows that none of these passages are prophetic. Likewise, there is no miraculous scientific knowledge in either the Quran or the Hadith. On the contrary, the historical and textual context shows each instance to be incomplete. Furthermore, many examples of flawed science can be found in the texts of the Quran and Hadith, which prevents us from considering the text miraculous.




Chapter 35
Response evaluation
Hadith vs. History

The three reasons that Muslims usually give for accepting Muhammad as a prophet—his character, the prophecies of the Bible, and his miraculous scientific insights—are all very problematic. Consequently, we have no reason to believe that Muhammad was sent by God. The argument is incredibly simple. By simply stating the reasons for Muhammad’s prophethood and examining them closely, we have done something that most Muslims never do. In general, most Muslims inherit a complete picture of Muhammad from their parents and teachers as children and then assume that the stories they have always heard are true. This was the case with me, and since I had never really considered the arguments rational, I did not respond rationally to the counterarguments. Although I had invited my friend David to study the subject with me, we often argued bitterly about Muhammad. I remember accusing him more than once of “dragging our prophet through the mud,” when in fact, in my heart I knew that I didn’t know how to deal with what I was learning about Muhammad. The reality is that most Muslims simply haven’t read the primary sources of Muhammad’s life, but instead have only heard a review that has filtered out the problematic narratives. When they hear these stories for the first time, they don’t know how to react. When I began reading these narratives with my own eyes, I tried to sort through each one individually, looking for explanations and ways to explain or refute any accounts that challenged my image of the peaceful and noble Muhammad. The individual problematic narratives quickly grew into dozens of such stories, and within a few months I was trying to explain away over a hundred accounts of Muhammad’s life that I simply couldn’t believe were true. This is when I began to take a more systematic approach to the life of Muhammad. How could I know which accounts of Muhammad's life were reliable and which were not?
Hadith sciences and our historical method
As Muslims, we knew that it would be a long time after Muhammad’s death before anything significant was written about his life, but people did not stop talking about him. For more than two hundred years, stories about the Prophet Muhammad were passed down orally from person to person, and among the authentic accounts, many forgeries abounded. By the time a systematic attempt was made to classify them, more than five hundred thousand accounts of Muhammad’s life were in circulation, and it is generally estimated that the vast majority of them were false. How can we tell which accounts of Muhammad’s life are reliable? I first considered the classical Islamic method of assessing the authenticity of hadith. In the field of hadith, which is translated as “the science of Muhammad’s sayings,” Muslim scholars rank individual accounts of Muhammad’s life based on criteria such as the degree of fame of a story and the people who narrated it. The most authentic hadith is ultimately graded as sahih, meaning “authentic” or “trustworthy,” while the weakest hadith are considered da’eef (“weak”) or even mudu (“fabricated”). Imam Bukhari and his disciple Imam Muslim were two of the most respected scholars in the third century after Muhammad, and they collected only the hadith they considered authentic and indisputable. Most Muslims consider their collections to be above reproach. Using only the hadith they confirmed, I hoped to see Muhammad as the noble and peaceful man I had always been taught, with no problematic narrations about his life. But that was not to be. Even among the authentic collections, there were problematic narrations. In fact, every single narration in the previous surah was either from the Quran or from the authentic collections of Imam Bukhari and Imam Muslim. I began asking the imams to resolve these problematic reports in the sahih, and I also searched the Internet to see how other Muslims responded to the criticisms. What I learned was that many were willing to accuse even the authentic collections of narrations of fabrication, and to find ways to refute those narrations that they considered authentic by the great scholars of ancient Islam. That’s when I realized they were playing a dangerous game. They were essentially finding ways to select from the ancient record which narrations were reliable and which were not, and create a Muhammad that they felt comfortable with. I didn’t want to do that. I wanted to know who Muhammad really was and whether I should follow him as my prophet, not create a Muhammad in my mind that was worth following. I decided to rethink my approach and study Muhammad historically, rather than through the science of hadith. What were the first narrations written about Muhammad, and how early were they written? It was then that I realized some shocking facts about early Islam. First, in Muhammad’s time, people didn’t write books in Arabic. The first Arabic book to be composed was the Quran, and it became a written book even after Muhammad’s death. There was no such thing as written Arabic literature, it was only oral. This is because, secondly, people were still figuring out how to write Arabic. Arabic script was far from standardized and had only been invented a century or two before Muhammad’s time. For these and other reasons, thirdly, no one wrote a biography of Muhammad’s life until about 140 years after Muhammad’s death. At that time, there were certainly no eyewitnesses to Muhammad’s life, and people were generations removed from the events being discussed. Can we trust such a narrative to be an unfiltered and accurate portrayal of Muhammad? The first biography, Sirat Rasoolullah, was written by someone named Ibn Ishaq, but the book itself is actually lost. Ibn Ishaq taught a man named Al-Baqa’i, who published Ibn Ishaq’s book himself, and Al-Baqa’i taught a man named Ibn Hisham, who revised Al-Baqa’i’s edition, and this is the version we have today. Why did these men each make their own versions? Ibn Hisham tells us in his introductory remarks: “Subjects that are embarrassing to discuss, matters that would upset some, and such narrations as Al-Baqa’i told me I could not accept as reliable, I have omitted all of these.” In other words, the first biography of Muhammad was notorious for its forgeries, embarrassing material, and uncomfortable facts. What we have today has been filtered many times, both for forgeries and for difficult facts. It is because of such deliberate editing that historians do not consider later accounts to be as reliable as earlier accounts, all other things being equal. Earlier accounts have not been filtered as much, and are therefore more likely to be true. Also, people are more prone to forgetting information over time, especially information that doesn’t fit the narrative as a whole. When it comes to the record of Muhammad’s life, we simply don’t have such raw or unfiltered data. Everything has been filtered through generations. Even though the first biography has passed through layers of filtering, it still contains shocking material. Muhammad personally oversaw the beheading of nine hundred men in one day, digging trenches in the markets so that their bodies could be thrown into mass graves after being beheaded. He ordered the murder of an old man who had written a poem complaining about Muhammad. A woman lamented the old man’s death in the poem, so Muhammad ordered her killed, and her blood was spilled on her children while she was breastfeeding. Muhammad ordered a city treasurer to be tortured to extract the location of the money, so his men lit a fire with flint and steel on the treasurer’s chest until he was nearly dead, and finally beheaded him. When Muhammad was about to execute a man, the man pleaded, “Who will take care of my children?” to which Muhammad replied, “Hell!” Just a cursory glance at the earliest biographies reveals many disturbing accounts of Muhammad. By the time the hadiths were written down under the supervision of men like Imam Bukhari and Imam Muslim, many of these accounts had been removed from Muhammad’s biographies, just as Ibn Hisham and al-Baqarah had filtered the accounts they had received. Because of this selective filtering, the entire hadith collection is inherently incomplete: they contain only those accounts that several generations of early Muslims, each one choosing to save. As we have seen, even those that were preserved were often considered incomplete and fabricated.
What can we really know about Muhammad?
For this reason, non-Muslim scholars of early Islam are very hesitant to trust information about Muhammad’s life. Almost none accept the science of hadith criticism as it is, most of them only hope to extract historical kernels of truth from the hadith. Some scholars have even given up hope of such success, saying that almost nothing can be known about Muhammad. These scholars come from a variety of religious and non-religious backgrounds. One Muslim scholar concluded that, given the nature of the evidence, Muhammad son Kalisch may not have existed at all. Muhammad son Kalisch completed his doctorate in Islamic jurisprudence in 1997 and became the first German professor to hold a chair in Islamic theology. When he arrived at the University of Münster in 2004, he was criticized by some for his zeal for Sharia. But then, according to the Wall Street Journal, Kalisch “wanted to examine Islam as much as Christianity and Judaism.” At first he defended Muhammad in print, but the more he studied, the more he realized that there were significant problems with the account. The word Muhammad occurs only four times in the Quran, and it is not clear whether it is a noun or a title. The Quran 61. 6 seems to say that the name of the prophet was Ahmad, not Muhammad. There is no further evidence of Muhammad’s existence until the early eighth century, when coins bearing his name were struck. “The more I studied, the more unlikely it became that the historical figure was the root of everything,” says Muhammad Kalish. Other scholars have come to similar conclusions because of the gaps in the historical record. Their concerns are more than just an argument from silence. If the traditional understanding of Islam is correct, it is indeed very problematic that we do not find more information about Muhammad in the earliest historical records. If Muhammad was the prophet of the Arabs, and if they were energized and motivated by his teachings, why do the Arab conquests of the Middle East, North Africa, and Iran never mention him by name? These conquests occurred in the mid-seventh century, immediately after Muhammad’s death, yet no contemporary record mentions Muhammad. In fact, none of them mentions the Bible or even the word Muslim. It is not that there is no record. There is abundant record, based on the correspondence and writings of the conquerors, but Muhammad is never mentioned, no Bible is mentioned, and the conquerors are never called Muslims. Other evidence also makes historians scratch their heads: Although Mecca is known as a trading center, it never appeared on any trade route until the end of the eighth century. None of the early mosques faced Mecca (until about the end of the eighth century, they all faced Jerusalem or Petra). Mecca is mentioned only once in the Quran. The description of the land in the Quran is very similar to Mecca, much more like northern Arabia. And the list goes on. For these reasons, not only one Muslim scholar, but many scholars are skeptical of the traditional origins of Islam and even the existence of Muhammad, at least as the early Islamic documents describe him. The truth about the origins of Islam, they say, is sadly obscure. There is almost nothing we can know with certainty about the historical Muhammad.










Chapter 36
Conclusion
The crossroads of historical Muhammad

Both as a Muslim and now, ten years later, I have not concluded that Muhammad did not exist. Given the sheer volume of stories and accounts, as well as their relative coherence, it seems to me more likely than that Muhammad did. But I must agree with the basic consensus of non-Muslim scholarship: we cannot know much about Muhammad with certainty. The obvious alternative is to trust the Islamic accounts of Muhammad’s life, the hadith, and the seerah. But if we judge their authenticity by the standards of the hadith sciences, we still find a Muhammad who is not convincing as a prophet of God. He may have been a great seventh-century general who adhered to the cultural standards of his time, but he was certainly not the greatest moral model of all time, nor someone to whom I would declare my allegiance. If we abandon the hadith sciences and adopt the historical method of assessing the earliest biographies and accounts of Muhammad’s life, we find a more brutal and problematic picture of Muhammad. This is the dilemma I had as a Muslim: either I could trust the historical sources of Muhammad's life and find a man I would never want to follow as a prophet, or I could question the sources and find a reason for the prophet. In either case, I could not conclude from the evidence that Muhammad was a prophet of God.
Riots in Ashgabat
Just like Professor Muhammad Son Kalisch, I was confident in Islam and in following Muhammad until I applied the same critical criteria to his life that I did to the life of Jesus. While critically studying Jesus’ claim to divinity, I was willing to set aside the Gospel of John because it was written fifty-five or sixty years after Jesus’ death, even if eyewitnesses to Jesus’ life were still alive at the time. If I had treated the accounts of Muhammad’s life in this way, I would have had to set aside absolutely everything and would have no reason to consider him a prophet. But his record presented me with a dilemma: if I believed in the authenticity of the records, then there was no way I could follow Muhammad as a prophet. His character was certainly not so great as to compel us to accept him as a prophet. He had many spiritual shortcomings and was very violent throughout his ministry, among other concerns. The record also did not show that God had approved of his ministry: he was not prophesied in the Bible, and he had no significant scientific insight. Disappointed, I concluded that I simply could not declare Muhammad a prophet of God based on the historical record. I could no longer recite the Testimony without a doubt, and this troubled me. The only way I could continue to follow Muhammad was through the Quran. If the Quran could be shown to be the word of God, then the one delivering it must have been a messenger of God. I clung to the hope that my research into the Quran would not only confirm Muhammad but also completely surpass the evidence for Christianity.















Part 10
Is the Quran the word of God?










Chapter 37
Positive case
There is no other book like it.

The Quran is the jewel of Islam. It is the “proof” of Muslim belief and the foundation of faith. Its place in Islamic theology is what Jesus is in Christian theology, and as a Muslim, my trust in nothing less than the text and its transcendence is built on. We have inherited our high regard for the Quran from the Muslims around us and from the world of our tradition. I grew up hearing portions recited aloud in daily prayers and asking my mother about them every day, and I memorized its chapters. Thus, I memorized the last seven surahs of the Quran by the age of five, and the last fifteen by the age of fifteen. While I was more familiar with the Quran than most Muslims I knew, I was certainly not unusual. Some in our Muslim community memorized the entire Quran by their teenage years. We look to the Quran more than any other physical object in the world. After all, we believed it to be the word of God. When I lost my historical confidence in Muhammad's prophethood, I placed all my hope on the pillar of the Quran, believing it to be strong enough to confirm Muhammad's prophethood. To be honest, I was really convinced that it would hold up because I had heard so many arguments for its inspiration.
The Literary Excellence of the Quran
It is the deep conviction of believing Muslims that the excellence of the Quran is unparalleled and its literary quality is unparalleled. This is not only because it is a statement of Allah, the Word of God on earth, which is inherently true, but also because the Quran says so. When people asked Muhammad to prove that the Quran was inspired by God, the answer came: “None but Allah can produce this Quran... or they say, ‘He has forged it.’ Say, ‘Then produce a chapter like it, if you are truthful.’” (10:37-38). The Quran therefore challenges skeptics to strive to produce something equal or better, and makes it clear that no one but Allah can produce a recitation as sublime as this. They will never be able to do so, even if they enlist the help of every human being and every devil: “Say, ‘If all mankind and jinn were to gather together to produce the like of this Quran, they could not produce the like of it’” (17:88). The fundamental challenge is repeated many times in the Quran. Muslims believe that this challenge was posed to the cynical Arabs who specialized in poetry. They were never able to produce anything as sublime and compelling as the Quran, and so the challenge remains to this day.
Fulfilled prophecies of the Quran
If the Quran can be shown to contain fulfilled prophecies, then we have good reason to believe that it is from God. One of the clearest prophecies is found in Surah 30, Ar-Rum. In it, the Quran mentions the recent loss of the Roman Empire and predicts that the Romans will eventually regroup and defeat their enemies within a few years (30.2-4). This is exactly what happened. The Persians defeated the Romans in 614, but Emperor Heraclius finally defeated the Persians in 622.
Other prophecies, which are more far-reaching in their predictions, have also come true. For example, the Quran says: “Their hearing and their sight and their skins will bear witness against them as to what they used to do” (41:20). Reading the verse, one wonders how skin can bear witness against a man. Muhammad could never have known about fingerprint analysis, that people’s skins can bear witness against them. So this verse is a prophecy that has come true in modern times. Many other prophecies like these two, foretelling events in ancient and modern times, confirm that the Quran is the word of God.
Miraculous scientific knowledge in the Quran
Muslims often argue that there is amazing scientific knowledge in the Quran. In addition to the examples in chapter 33, Muslims often argue that the Quran speaks of the Big Bang in 21:30, which says: “Do not those who disbelieve know that the heavens and the earth were joined together, then We separated them?” In addition to the miraculous astronomical insight, the verse goes on to provide a miraculous biological insight: “And We created from water every living thing.” Muhammad could not have known about the Big Bang or the fact that water is essential to all forms of life. The best explanation for this amazing scientific knowledge is that the Quran must have come from Allah.
The mathematical wonders of the Quran
Although this was not an argument I ever used, many Muslims argue that the Quran displays mathematical wonders that can only be the result of Allah’s authorship. One type of mathematical wonder is numerical parallelism: the word “month” appears twelve times in the Quran, the word “day” 365 times, the words “man” and “woman” appear an equal number of times, the words “angels” and “devils” appear an equal number of times. And the words “this world” and “the hereafter” appear an equal number of times. Such numerical parallels can only be the result of a divine mind behind the text. In addition to these patterns, there is a code hidden in the Quran. The Quran says in 74.30: “[And] over it [Hell] are nineteen [guardians].” When we analyze the Quranic text for patterns, we start to find the number nineteen everywhere. To give just a few examples, the total number of surahs in the Quran is divided into nineteen parts. The first revelation of the Quran had nineteen words. And the first chapter, which was revealed in chronological order, has nineteen verses and a total number of words divisible by nineteen. Dozens of such examples can be found, and the number nineteen is not a small prime number, making it very difficult to show it as a common pattern for a text. Given the Quranic verse on the number nineteen, the strangeness of the number, and the prevalence of the pattern, one can be confident that Allah is the author of this text.
Complete memorization of the Quran
One of the most essential reasons for the Muslim faith is the complete preservation of the Quran. Every word, every letter, every line of the Quran has remained exactly as it was revealed, from Allah to Muhammad to this day. This is a fundamental belief because of the view of the Quran in Islam: it is the eternal expression of Allah, so it never changes. Its immutability is also essential because it is the basis of the Sharia, the law for all time. This is the miraculous proof of the Quran’s inspiration, for it is predicted in the Quran: “Indeed, We have revealed this Quran gradually, and We will certainly be its guardian” (9.15). And since we believed that every other holy book had been corrupted by its followers, this miracle is all the more powerful.

Positive case conclusion
In our circles the Quran was truly above discussion. For numerous reasons, the Muslim community is convinced beyond any doubt that it is the word of God: its text is incomparably superior, it predicts prophecies that have been fulfilled, it holds hidden scientific truths waiting to be discovered, its amazingly calculated text can only be the product of a divine mind, and the text is perfectly preserved. Such were the arguments we had always believed in, and I had been convinced of their power all my life. However, when it became the only remaining pillar of my faith, I had to examine these arguments much more carefully than before. They were now the sole foundation of my faith, and I had to make sure that they could bear the weight of my entire Islamic worldview.












Chapter 38
Answer
In what way is this a miracle?

When I began my research on the Quran, I became increasingly convinced that it was the word of God, but I had to be able to show that an objective researcher would also find the case convincing. That's where the case began to fall apart.
The Literary Excellence of the Quran: An Incomplete Test
One of the first things that became apparent was that the Quran’s own defense of its own inspiration—the challenge of writing a revelation like that—is virtually impossible. I remember well Mike Licona’s response when I told him the challenge of the Quran. He replied bluntly, “I’ve never seen anything better written than the Quran.” “Have you ever read Psalm 23?” Shocked by his outrageous statement, I replied that Psalm 23 was not on the level of the Quran, but he simply contradicted me, saying that it was one of the most powerful verses ever written, and more moving than anything he had read in the Quran. It was then that I took a step back and reevaluated the test. What was it really asking? I began to think that it must be the inimitable Arabic of the Quran, not the English translation or commentary. But did that mean that the test was limited to Arab speakers? That couldn’t be the case either. The people who had specifically set out to answer the challenge of the Quran had previously written a book of Christian teachings in Arabic called Furqan al-Haqq, “The True Discernment.” They had incorporated many of the teachings of the Psalms into this Arabic book and written them in the style of the Quran. Their results were so convincing that they read the text aloud, reciting it in the style of the Quran in the middle of Arab Muslim cities. Many passersby would hear the recitation and, mistaking it for the Quran, would thank the reciters for reciting it. So it is clear that this test did not work for Arab speakers, and even for Arab Muslims. Researching the subject online, I found Muslim apologists who argued that the Arabs who misunderstood Furqan al-Haqq were probably uneducated and therefore did not appreciate the sublime beauty of the Quran. An expert in Arabic would find the text of the Quran inimitable. It was then that I found an assessment by a scholar named Gerd Poin, who specializes in the Arabic spelling of the Quran. Poin argued that “every fifth sentence simply doesn’t make sense.” Poin added, preemptively defending his claim: “Of course, many Muslims—and Orientalists—will tell you otherwise, but the fact is that one-fifth of the Quran is incomprehensible.” To this, Muslim apologists responded that Gerd Poin was not a native Arabic speaker. The Quran should have been assessed by an Arabic expert who was himself an Arab and spoke Arabic. With each step, it seemed that the test was being redefined to somehow protect it from scrutiny, but this begs the question: In what sense is this challenge relevant to us? If it is only for Arab speakers, it makes no sense to the vast majority of people in the world today. If people have to be experts in classical Arabic, then it would only apply to a very small number of people in human history. Of course, all of this is apart from the subjective nature of the test and its non-sequential nature: who can say whether one composition is superior to another? By what standard? The Quran certainly does not set the parameters that its defenders do. And even if it turns out that the Quran is the best writing humanity has ever seen, that does not mean it was inspired. Stradivarius, a man who lived from the mid-1600s to the 1700s, is famous for making the finest violins ever heard, acoustically. Even with our modern technology, we have not been able to reproduce their perfection. If he had said that his violins were made by God and offered their unparalleled quality as proof, we would have called him crazy or a liar. How does a superior product prove divine origin? For these reasons, the literary superiority of the Quran is an imperfect test, and as I found with Mike, it is not convincing to non-Muslims—the very people for whom it is intended. An objective scholar will not be convinced.
Quranic Prophecies: Not Really Prophecy
When we examine the prophecies of the Quran carefully, we find that there is virtually no case to test. For example, let us consider the claim that 41.20 is a prophecy about fingerprints. The full context of the verse is: “And [remember] the Day when the enemies of Allah will be gathered to the Fire and will be confined [and divided into groups] until, when they reach it, their hearing and their sight and their skins will testify against them as to what they used to do. And they will say to their skins, ‘Why did you testify against us?’ They will say, ‘He who gives speech to all things has made us speak, and He created you the first time, and to Him you will be returned.’” (41:19-21). Reading the text, we find that the Quran is actually talking about the Day of Judgment, that skin will speak with its voice, its eyes, and its ears. This prophecy is not about something happening on earth, but rather an apocalyptic description of judgment. Only by taking this verse out of context and imposing a fabricated meaning on it can this verse be interpreted for our time.
Of course, if we try hard enough, we can do this with almost any text, and it's easier with apocalyptic texts like this. For example, I just opened the Bible to a random section of the book of Revelation, and my finger landed on 11:8, which says, "And their dead bodies will lie on the ground in the street of that great city, which is figuratively called Sodom and Egypt, where also their Lord was crucified." I could argue that this was a miraculous prophecy of the Arab Spring, describing the aftermath of the Tahrir Square protests that occurred in January 2011 and resulted in the deaths of over eight hundred people. Furthermore, the city of Cairo, where Tahrir Square is located, is often figuratively called Egypt in Arabic. Thus, eight hundred bodies were laid out in Tahrir Square, the public square in the great city that is figuratively called Egypt. Revelation 11:8 predicts this event with great accuracy almost two thousand years in advance. Of course, this is not what the text predicts, and I am just making this all up, but if I really wanted to, I could make the case sound convincing, especially if I took the verse out of context. This is exactly what we see in the vast majority of so-called prophecies in the Quran. There is no reason to think that the Quran is any of these so-called prophecies. It expresses prophecies. This is not to say that there may not be hidden prophetic meaning in the text of the Quran, but for it to be a prophecy that will convince the objective scholar, it must meet the minimum criterion of being clearly a prophecy, not a text that can be transformed into one. For example, the passage about the Roman victory in 30.2-4 does indeed seem to be a prophecy, the clearest example of a prophecy in the Quran, so it is worth considering more carefully. There are two minor problems with considering this fulfilled prophecy and two major problems. In short, the first minor problem is the nature of the prophecy; it is not so much a prediction. Given the ongoing nature of the conflicts between Byzantium and Persia at the time, it is certain that a Byzantine victory will occur at some point. It is almost like saying: "The Chicago Bulls just lost to the Los Angeles Lakers, but the Bulls will eventually win." The second minor problem is the number of years that the Byzantines They were victorious. The Quranic verse uses a word that implies ten years or less, but it took the Romans seventeen years to finally defeat the Byzantines. There are two much more significant problems with using this as a prophetic fulfillment. First, Quranic verses were regularly abrogated, and the Byzantine victory occurred during Muhammad’s lifetime. If it seemed that this was not going to happen, the text could easily have been abrogated. Second, and relatedly, the Quran was not collected into one book, even later. If this prophecy had failed, it could have been left out of the final collection of the text, like other verses. So this example of a Quranic prophecy, the best the text offers, is not convincing. It is essentially a 50/50 prediction that could easily have been retracted, and if we take the text literally, it seems that it did not come true anyway, because the Byzantines did not defeat the Persians until The estimated time has passed.
Miraculous scientific knowledge: considering context and context
With regard to the prophethood of Muhammad, we have already seen that the Quran contains scientific inaccuracies – such as the setting of the sun in water and the production of sperm between the spine and ribs – which are enough to cast serious doubt on the scientificity of the Quran. As before, simply examining the context and context of such supposed miraculous knowledge is usually enough to conclude that the argument is not convincing. This rule of thumb holds true here as well. Looking again at 21:30 of the Quran, it actually says: “Did the disbelievers not know that the heavens and the earth were of one piece, then We separated them.” It does not say that the universe was one, but rather that the heavens and the earth were joined together and God separated them. This is really just an approximation of what God was doing in Genesis 1:6 – nothing new or unique to the Quran. Similarly, it is not difficult to imagine that all living things are made of water, since humans and animals all drink water and survive very little in a desert where there is not much water. Finally, it is perhaps worth noting that the Quran repeatedly states that the jinn are made of fire, not water, and some consider this to be a contradiction in the Quran. Thus, these examples, as before, are shown to be far from convincing given the actual text of the verse, the historical context of the claim of science, and the accuracy of what the Quran states. There is no miraculous scientific knowledge in the Quran.

Mathematical Wonders:
What makes them special?
Although many Muslims find reasoning from mathematical patterns compelling, to be honest, I never have. All sorts of amazing patterns can be found in the world around us if we simply look for them. This is true of the natural world, as anyone who has considered the golden ratio will attest, and it is true of man-made products as well. For example, by looking for patterns in Moby Dick or The Whale, one can find predictions of the future, including the assassinations of Leon Trotsky, Martin Luther King Jr., John F. Kennedy, Abraham Lincoln, and Princess Diana. If you look hard enough, and if you can change the parameters, you will find patterns. And we know that the person who “discovered” the pattern of the number nineteen in the Quran did exactly that. Rashad Khalifa, the first to publish the pattern of the number nineteen, spent some 250 pages defending the fifty-two different ways in which the number nineteen was found in the text of the Quran. According to a popular Muslim scholar, Bilal Phillips, “Most Muslims readily and unquestioningly accepted Khalifa’s claims because they had an aura of ‘scientific fact’ about them.” But some Muslims vehemently criticized the argument for the number nineteen, pointing out major flaws in Khalifa’s argument. Phillips himself wrote an entire treatise against it, entitled “The Numerical Miracle of the Quran.” In it, Phillips says, “The theory of nineteen is a fabricated hoax that cannot withstand serious scientific scrutiny.” Part of Phillips’ response to Khalifa concerns the Quranic verse quoted by Khalifa, 74. 30-31. Reading the full text in this case makes it clear that the number nineteen refers to the number of angels who guard the fire of hell, not to the patterns of miracles in the Quranic text. As we have seen others do with Quranic “prophecies,” the Caliph simply took words out of their context and tried to fit them into his own theory. Another part of Phillips’s response concerns the inconsistent method and arbitrary choices required to argue for the number nineteen. “Using Dr. Caliph’s inconsistent method of constructing multiples, 8 can be established as the axis of the numerical code of the miracle of the Quran,” says Phillips, and he goes on to give eight reasons for this. However, the bulk of Phillips’ response to the Caliph’s argument is a systematic analysis of Caliph’s publications and calculations, concluding that the Caliph had “fabricated data to artificially create the sum of letters that are multiples of nineteen.” Not only does the Caliph count letters and verses inconsistently, using “an incomprehensible system of word recognition that is completely at odds with the rules of classical and modern Arabic grammar,” but he also wants to leave out two verses from the Quran to make his calculations work. Phillips’ arguments against the “miraculous model” are compelling, as is his conclusion that the Caliph was willing to manipulate the Quranic text simply to fit the data. In a similar way, arguments for miraculous numerical parallels seem to contradict the text and manipulate the phrase. For example, the Quran does not use the word day 365 times. It does 360 times, and for the other five to work, proponents of the argument would have to distort the data and allow words to be counted that are not exactly the word of the day. The same is true of the number of times the moon is used and the supposed parallel occurrences of man and woman and the world and the hereafter. Since Arabic is a Semitic language and uses a three-letter root system for its words, different words are very similar in spelling, and this often works to the advantage of those who are willing to "twist the data" to make their own arguments. To summarize my response to the argument about amazing mathematical patterns, I will quote Phillips's closing statement: "It can be concluded that the theory of nineteen as a numerical code is a miracle of the Quran in the Quran itself and that the few instances where nineteen and its multiples occur are simply coincidences taken out of proportion." The same can be said of all the mathematical wonders found in the Quran.
Complete memorization of the Quranic text:
In what way is it perfectly preserved?
Finally, while we firmly believe in the complete memorization of the Quran, the reality is that it is impossible to prove it. When Uthman produced an official, edited version of the Quran and destroyed all other copies, he left no means for future historians to determine whether the Quran we know today actually dates back to Muhammad. Uthman destroyed all evidence, and it seems that he did so precisely because there were so many different versions. Despite the fact that complete memorization cannot be proven, it can be refuted to a considerable extent. In the first place, the text of the Quran was not always written down, but sometimes only known by memory. This was why Umar persuaded Abu Bakr to compile a copy of the Quran in the first place. Many Qurans were dying on the battlefields, and Umar said, “I fear that there will be a greater loss of life among the Qurans on the battlefields than a large part of the Quran will be lost.” If the Quran had been written, why was it feared that it would be lost when its reciters died? The fact is that parts of the Quran were not written and had to be collected from people's memories. Sahih al-Bukhari explicitly states that the Quran was collected from "palm branches, thin white stones, and from men who knew it by heart." The same hadith tells us that at least two verses were known only to one person: "I searched the Quran until I found the last two verses of Surah At-Tawbah with Abu Khuzaymah al-Ansari, and I could not find them with anyone other than him. In other words, two verses of the Quran were recorded on the testimony of one person. If he had not memorized them, they would have been lost and we would not have been any the wiser. Sahih al-Bukhari says that when the Quran was first written down, a verse was missed and had to be found later: “When we abrogated the Quran, a verse from Surah al-Ahzab was lost to me, and I used to hear the Messenger of God reciting it. So we searched for it and found it with Khuza’mah ibn Thabit al-Ansari.” It seems that verses were constantly being forgotten, lost, and difficult to retrieve. Muhammad himself said that Muslims easily forget verses of the Quran. “Continue reciting the Quran, for it leaves the hearts of people faster than a camel.” He was not exaggerating, for he even forgot verses of the Quran. When he heard a man reciting the Quran at night, he said, “May God have mercy on him, for he has reminded me of verses that I have forgotten.” If, according to the most reliable narrations, only one person knew parts of the Quran and ignored others, and Muslims forgot verses, wouldn’t it be possible that some of the Quran had been completely discarded? Can we really say that such a shaky text has been completely preserved? Unfortunately, we cannot. Sahih Bukhari puts the nail in the coffin by recording this hadith: “Umar said: Ubayy was the best of us in reciting the Quran, but we forget some of what he recited.” Ubayy said: I took it from the mouth of the Messenger of Allah and I never forget it.
So the best reciter of the Quran was of the opinion that verses of the Quran had been omitted.” Muhammad himself chose Ubayy as one of the best teachers of the Quran, but he disagreed with the Quran of today. In short, not only is there no way to prove the complete memorization of the Quran, but it seems to have been refuted: parts are missing, and one of the greatest teachers of the Quran, chosen by Muhammad, was opposed to the modern edition. Much more could be said against the argument for complete memorization, but we have only been content with the evidence in Sahih Bukhari, and not even all of it. Finally, as we saw in Surah 14, the text of the Quran was being abrogated. Muhammad abrogated some of the older verses and replaced them with newer ones. The Quran itself testifies to this in 2. 106 and 16.101. Muslims may be able to accept this phenomenon as a divine command, and they are well within their rights to do so, but to an objective scholar, it strongly challenges the claim of perfect preservation. Rather, the phenomenon of abrogation makes the text of the Quran appear highly artificial and man-made. Of course, even if the Quran were preserved in its entirety, this would not necessarily be a miracle. Many texts have remained unchanged throughout the ages. But given its abrogation of text, missing sections, forgotten sections, and the controlled destruction of all kinds, an objective scholar is forced to ask: “In what way is the preservation of the Quran miraculous?”
Ending the answer to the positive case
When we begin to dig beneath the surface, the arguments for the divine inspiration of the Quran are not all that convincing. The literary superiority of the Quran is untestable, subjective, and unprovable. The prophecies of the Quran are not convincing. The science of the Quran is truly problematic. The numerical patterns are often distorted data with exaggerated interpretations. And the Quran is not preserved in any miraculous sense. Since there is no convincing reason, there is no reason to accept the Quran as the word of God.











Chapter 39
Response evaluation
What kind of book is the Quran?

When evaluating these counterarguments as a Muslim, my immediate response was to bring more evidence to the table, to provide more examples of fulfilled prophecies and more examples of miraculous scientific knowledge. We had heard dozens of such examples in our lives in the mosques. Surely, if the first few examples were not convincing, one of many others would have to. It took me months of citing example after example until I finally realized that, in fact, they all succumbed to the same basic criticisms. When I became familiar with the history of the Quranic text, the process was quite different. Then, it was not a series of examples that were called into question, but the fundamental narrative of my Muslim faith. We had learned from everyone, not just the imams in the mosque, but from our parents and elders and the books we had read, that the Quran was in perfect transmission. Muhammad had transmitted it to his scribes, the scribes had written it down and memorized it, the Muslim people kept it in their hearts and recited it regularly during prayer, and it was easy to write and memorize. All copies today are exactly the same. That is what we were told. But as I read Sahih Bukhari, especially the book Fadil al-Quran, it became clear to me that much evidence of the narration I had inherited had been left out. The truth about the text of the Quran and its history is shocking to Muslims who I am sharing it with today, but it is well documented. Arabic writing was far from perfect at the time of Muhammad, which is why there was no such thing as a written Arabic book. When the text of the Quran was originally written, the letters and vowels had not yet been standardized, which led to confusion. For these reasons, what the scribes wrote was a memory aid for the oral text. This is actually a relatively undisputed fact even among Muslim Quran scholars. What is controversial is the result: the text of the Quran was fluid in Muhammad’s time. He recited the same verse in several ways, and he said he could do it in up to seven ways. If at any point a text needed to be abrogated, it could simply be abrogated and replaced by another. Since the text of the Quran was not seen as a written text, this did not pose much of a problem: all that was needed was to refrain from reciting some verses and “forget” what had been revealed. The advantage of a written text is that it should have clear boundaries. The book begins with a specific verse and ends with a specific verse. With an oral text that is recited in parts, repeated in various ways, sometimes abrogated, and never publicly recited from beginning to end, it is difficult to determine its exact content. For this reason, when the Quran was finally written down, there was much disagreement about its Sharia. We have already seen that the final version of the Quran was problematic for Ubayy, who claimed that parts of it had been lost, and Ubayy was one of the best teachers of the Quran. According to early Islamic sources, Ubayy had two surahs at the end of his Quran that are not in the modern Quran: Surah Hafd and Surah Khalil. Ubayy was not alone in including these surahs, as at least two other companions of Muhammad believed that they were part of the Quran. But Ubayy was not the best teacher of the Quran. When Muhammad gave a list of the four best teachers of the Quran, he first mentioned Abdullah ibn Mas’ud. He disagreed with Ubayy, saying that Ubayy’s last two chapters were divinely revealed supplications, not scripture. However, on the same basis, he disagreed with the modern Quran, insisting that Surah 1, as well as Surahs 113 and 114, were divinely revealed supplications, not parts of the Quran. He removed them from his final version, limiting his canon to 111 surahs. The Quran of today, not compiled by a teacher named Muhammad, is only one of many Quranic manuscripts that were officially approved by the Caliphate and became the standard text by burning the others. When this happened, the Quran changed from a primarily oral text to a primarily written one, and what had previously been recited from memory was now read from a page. The Arabic script, which was not yet perfect, needed to be standardized and finalized. The Quran is the driving force behind the development of this standard Arabic script.
Three hundred years after Muhammad's death, the Arabic script had become more or less standardized, so a scholar of considerable authority, Ibn Mujahid, ordered that all but seven different readings be outlawed. Some of his elders opposed him, because they had been reciting the Quran in what was now considered illegal since childhood, and they continued to recite the Quran in the same way they had always done. One of these, Ibn Shanabd, was beaten until he publicly recanted. The proliferation of different Quranic readings was thus controlled. However, these seven readings each multiplied once more as they were read by different recipients, until around 1924, when the Islamic world produced its first Arabic Quran. The Cairo Royal Edition selected one of the eighty readings for mass production, the reading of Hafs as narrated by Asim. And this is the Quran that most of the world knows today. But some parts of the Islamic world still have Qurans based on other readings, such as the Warsh reading narrated by Nafi. There are significant differences between these Qurans, but the vast majority are minor – usually just differences in intonation. Regardless, few Muslims realize that just a hundred years ago, there were about eighty different readings of the Quran in the Islamic world, and even today there are significant differences in the Quran. This is the real history of the Quran, not what the elders and mosques have told us. The Quran began as an oral text, evolved into a written text that was not unanimously agreed upon, and was even shaped and constructed by human power up until the 20th century. The textual transmission of the Quran has been obliterated by human contrivance and intervention, and none of the other reasons for the inspiration of the Quran hold up to scrutiny. There is truly no reason that would lead an objective scholar to believe that the Quran was inspired.












Chapter 40
Conclusion
There is no convincing reason to think that the Quran is the word of God.

As I studied each of the arguments for the inspiration of the Quran, a pattern became clear: I could find sufficient reason to defend my faith in the Quran, but none of the arguments could stand on their own merits. Could I persuade a careful scholar who had not previously believed in Islam? Could I continue to believe that the Quran is literary superior to any other book? Of course, I could reject assessments such as those made by my friend Mike or by scholar Gerd Poyn. Could I believe that the Quran contains miraculous prophetic knowledge? Yes, I could find such knowledge in the text, even if it was not obvious to others. Could I believe that the Quran was miraculously preserved? Yes, I could assume that Allah guided the Quran throughout its formation and evolution to keep it faithful to its original text. But none of the arguments are convincing to an objective scholar. The Quran cannot be used as a miraculous argument to convince someone who has carefully examined the evidence. And that was me: someone who wanted to believe in the inspiration of the Quran, but would only do so if the evidence was strong. After carefully examining five common arguments, I saw that they were not strong enough to prove faith, but in fact had to be proven by faith.





Conclusion on Question 2
Islam or Christianity?
The evidence is clear.

Assessing the Case of Islam and Its Efforts to Audit Islamic Roots
To be a Muslim, one must confess the testimony: “There is no god but Allah, and Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah.” The best way to test the truth of the testimony is to investigate the prophethood of Muhammad and the claim that Allah inspired the Quran. Even though my deepest desire was to defend the religion of Islam and remain a Muslim, the truth became inescapable: there was no reason to defend Muhammad’s prophethood, and there was no compelling reason to think that the Quran was from God. Once again, not only does history not support the traditional narratives of Islam, it shows that history is completely inconsistent with Islamic origins. When we use the same criteria to assess the origins of Islam that are used to assess the origins of Christianity, we find a gap in the historical record. The contemporary records of the Arabs of the mid-seventh century, supposedly the first Muslims after Muhammad, show that they are not referred to as Muslims and never refer to their scriptures, never mention Muhammad by name, never mention Mecca. And he did not pray towards Mecca. Given the vast body of records from that time, especially those from many countries conquered by the Arabs, this is not a reason for silence. The contemporary historical record is simply inconsistent with the traditional narrative of Islam.
Similarly, the history of the Quran is inconsistent with the narrative we have been taught as Muslims. We were told that the Quran had never changed and that every letter had remained exactly the same from the time of Muhammad to the present day. On the contrary, the Quran had been fundamentally transformed, initially as a highly fluid oral text and then transformed into a written text that remains, to varying degrees, mobile even to this day. Traditional Islamic narratives of Muhammad and the Quran are fundamentally inconsistent with the historical record. These are the pillars of Islamic faith, and their foundations are unfounded. This meant that if I wanted to remain a Muslim, I had to do so on the basis of some reason other than objective truth. I could remain a Muslim because I liked the Islamic message, because I wanted to have religious order, or because I just wanted to please my family. But if Islam had taught me anything, it was that I should submit to God, not man. This meant following the truth, no matter where it led. Of course, the reason I was researching Islam was to answer the Christianity case. Now that I had explored all avenues, I had to be honest with myself and evaluate the Christianity case and the Islam case for the last time.
Evidence for Christianity vs. Islam
After thoroughly examining the claims of Islam and Christianity, even when I was a Muslim, there was no avoiding the obvious truth: the evidence for Christianity was much, much stronger than the evidence for Islam. The three main claims of Christianity, that Jesus died on the cross and rose from the dead, proving that he was God, are solidly rooted in history. Even though Islam denies these points, I concluded that the historical evidence for Jesus’ death on the cross was as strong as anything historical, that his resurrection from the dead was by far the best explanation for the facts surrounding his crucifixion, and that his claim to be God was the best way to explain the proclamation of the early church. These conclusions were not unique, but based on the consensus of scholars across the theological spectrum. In other words, the truth of the Christian message makes the most sense of the historical evidence. In contrast, none of Islam’s main claims for truth, that Muhammad is a prophet and that the Quran is the word of God, are convincing. Muhammad’s character does not lead one to believe that he was a man chosen by God, nor is it foretold in the Bible. He had no miraculous scientific insights recorded in either the Hadith or the Quran. Hence, it cannot be shown that the Quran was inspired by literary quality, fulfilled prophecies, mathematical patterns, or miraculous memorization. The traditional Islamic narrative is inconsistent with both the history of Christianity and its own historical record. To believe the Islamic narrative of Christian origins while taking the historical record seriously would require us to conclude that Jesus was a completely incapable Christian and that God was a deceptive God. The historical record of Islamic origins leads many scholars to wonder whether Muhammad ever existed, leading scholars to think that the Quran was originally a much more fluid and indeed a very different book than it is today. The Islamic narratives of Christian origin, and even of Islamic origin, are inconsistent with history. In other words, to believe in the truth of Islam is to ignore the historical evidence. As a Muslim, I wanted to base my beliefs not on blind faith, not on what appealed to me, not even on my family heritage. I wanted to ground my faith in reality. If I wanted to take the record of history seriously, I would have to abandon my Islamic faith and accept the Bible. But that would come at a great cost, essentially everything I had ever known. Is it worth sacrificing everything for the truth? Is the truth worth dying for?















Conclusion
Is the truth worth dying for?
Is it for that?








Leaving Islam could cost you everything: family, friends, career, everything you’ve ever known, and perhaps even life itself. Is it really worth sacrificing everything for the truth? The answer is simple: It depends on the value of the truth. When we consider the Bible, we uncover the deep mysteries of the universe. We find a Triune God whose love is eternal and absolute, who did not need the world but created it out of the overflow of his love. In him, Jehovah, we have a Father who loves us unconditionally, who offers us boundless grace, who comes to us when we turn to him, who makes us purposeful and arranges all things for the good of those who love him. In Jehovah we find a Son who is willing to bear our pains, who leads us through suffering in exemplary humility, who lightens our burdens, and who makes a way for us to live life to the fullest even if we die. In Jehovah the Holy Spirit, we receive our Comforter, who fills us with grace, transforms our hearts, renews our minds, and sends us into the world as God’s hands and feet to serve others as He does. The gospel is the answer to our individual pains, to the sufferings of the world, and to the mysteries of life. There is no God but one, and He is the Father, the Holy Spirit, and the Son. There is no God but one, and He is Jesus. It is worth all the suffering to receive this truth and to follow Him. God is more beautiful than life itself, and the one who loves Him is ready to die at the time of death, not only to glorify Him, but to rush to His embrace. Although we will die, we will live. Sarah Fatima Al-Mutairi knew this when her brother locked her in his room. She knew that there was a great difference between the path of Muhammad and the path of Christ, and she was sure of the truth of the Gospel. She decided not to repent because of her faith in Christ. On August 12, 2008, an article was published in the Saudi newspaper Al-Akhdood with the headline: “A member of Al-Hisbah assassinated his sister for converting to Christianity.” The article shared these details: “A Saudi citizen who worked for the Group for the Promotion of Virtue and the Prevention of Vice in the Eastern Province killed his sister on the accusation that she had converted to Christianity. According to sources close to the victim, the killer attacked her by burning the girl and cutting out her tongue. Our sister, twenty-six-year-old Sara Fatima, was convinced by the grace of our God that it was worth giving up everything to have Him. She chose Jesus over this life. It seems that she did not spend her last moments reconsidering her faith. Instead, her heart was filled with the sorrow of Muslims. In her final moments before her brother returned to take her life, she posted a poem on the Internet. Although he cut out her tongue and took her life, her living voice now reaches us. These are the words of Sara Fatima, who passed away:
My tears are rolling down my cheeks and oh! my heart is sad.
How cruel you are to those who become Christians!
Christ says: "Blessed are all who are persecuted"
And we endure everything for Christ's sake.
What do you care that we are apostates?
You will not enter our graves and you will not be buried with us.
Your swords don't matter to me at all!
Your threats are not about me and we are not afraid.
By God, I am a Christian to the point of death!
O my eyes, weep for what has been a sad life.
Because I was away from the Lord Jesus for years.
O history, record it! And O witnesses, bear witness!
We are Christians who walk in the way of Christ.
Take this knowledge from me and write it down well!
Jesus is my Lord and He is the best protector.
I advise you to feel sorry for yourself.
Look at your hateful gaze, how ugly it is.
Man is a brother to man, O scholars!
Where is humanity and love? And where are you?
I pray my last words to the Lord of the Worlds.
Jesus Christ, the Manifest Light of Guidance:
Change their hearts and correct their discernment.
May it spread love among you, O Muslims.
Amen Amen To my sister, Sarah Fatima, I say this: You were only a Christian for a month, but your faith is an example to us all. May your voice resonate forever, and may we follow you as an inspiration, even unto death. We are sure that we will soon be with you in the arms of Jesus.
